Conelike radiant structures
Daniel J. F. Fox

Abstract
. Analogues of the classical affine-projective correspondence are developed in the context of statistical manifolds compatible with a radiant vector field. These utilize a formulation of Einstein equations for special statistical structures that generalizes the usual Einstein equations for pseudo-Riemannian metrics and is of independent interest. A conelike radiant structure is a not necessarily flat affine connection equipped with a family of surfaces that behave like the intersections of the planes through the origin with a convex cone in a real vector space. A radiant structure is a torsion-free affine connection and a vector field whose covariant derivative is the identity endomorphism. A radiant structure is conelike if for every point and every two-dimensional subspace containing the radiant vector field there is a totally geodesic surface passing through the point and tangent to the subspace. Such structures exist on the total space of any principal bundle with one-dimensional fiber and on any Lie group with a quadratic structure on its Lie algebra. The affine connection of a conelike radiant structure can be normalized in a canonical way to have antisymmetric Ricci tensor. Applied to a conelike radiant structure on the total space of a principal bundle with one-dimensional fiber this yields a generalization of the classical Thomas connection of a projective structure. The compatibility of radiant and conelike structures with metrics is investigated and yields a construction of connections for which the symmetrized Ricci curvature is a constant multiple of a compatible metric that generalizes well-known constructions of Riemannian and Lorentzian Einstein-Weyl structures over Kähler-Einstein manifolds having nonzero scalar curvature. A formulation of Einstein equations for special statistical manifolds is given that generalizes the Einstein-Weyl equations and encompasses these more general examples. There are constructed left-invariant conelike radiant structures on a Lie group endowed with a left-invariant nondegenerate bilinear form, and the case of three-dimensional unimodular Lie groups is described in detail.



1.
Introduction

A
statistical structure

(\nabla,h)
comprises a pseudo-Riemannian metric
h
and a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
satisfying
\nabla_{[i}h_{j]k}=0
. The compatibility condition is equivalent to the complete symmetry of the
cubic form

\mathcal{L}_{ijk}=\nabla_{i}h_{jk}
. The torsion-free connection
\bar{\nabla}=\nabla+h^{kp}\mathcal{L}_{ijp}
constitutes with
h
the
conjugate
statistical structure having cubic form
\bar{\nabla}_{i}h_{jk}=-\mathcal{L}_{ijk}
. Conjugacy of statistical structures is an involution that generalizes duality of hypersurfaces in flat affine space. The self-conjugate statistical structures are pairs comprising a metric and its Levi-Civita connection.

The notion of statistical structure was introduced by Amari, see
[
3
]
, and Chentsov, see
[
132
]
, in the course of applying differential geometric methods to study the Fisher-Rao Riemannian metric on the parameter space of a parametric family of probability spaces
[
6
]
. Background on this approach to parametric statistics, now usually called information geometry, can be found in the books of Amari
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
and in
[
6
]
which also surveys the relevant literature.

A statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
is
special
if
\nabla
preserves
\det h
. That a cooriented nondegenerate hypersurface in flat affine space acquires a pair of conjugate special statistical structures (see Example
25
) was emphasized by H. Matsuzoe
[
108
,
107
]
and implicitly plays a role in earlier work of T. Kurose
[
91
,
92
]
and H. Amari and collaborators
[
1
,
3
]
, among others. In this context, statistical structures have been called by other names such as
Codazzi structures

[
105
,
126
]
or the author’s terminology
exact AH structures

[
57
,
59
]
. A statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
is
flat
if
\nabla
is flat. There is an extensive literature treating flat statistical structures which are also called
Hessian structures

[
28
,
48
,
124
,
125
,
127
,
131
]
or
Kähler affine structures

[
36
,
60
,
85
]
. Possibly singular flat statistical structures are related to the Frobenius manifolds and WDVV equations studied in mathematical physics
[
34
,
47
,
46
,
77
,
79
]
.

Even without such motivations, statistical structures are of interest simply as a direct generalization of pseudo-Riemannian metrics and in recent years have received more attention in this sense, for example in
[
15
,
64
,
95
,
106
,
118
]
(no attempt is made here at completeness).

A structure arising in a diversity of initially apparently little related contexts is likely to be rich, while the study of its properties from the perspective appropriate to one context may yield unexpected insights in the other contexts.

Here the differential geometric point of view on statistical structures is pursued. This differential geometric perspective leads to focusing on a class of examples different from those that form the center of attention in the main literature on statistical structures. The specific context considered (described in detail later in the introduction) generalizes the classical relation between geometric structures on a domain in projective space and geometric structures on the cone over this domain. It leads to the following notions:

•

A notion of Einstein equations for special statistical structures that generalizes the usual Einstein equations for pseudo-Riemannian metrics and is preserved by conjugacy. For example, the Cheng-Yau metric of a properly convex flat projective structure constitutes with a distinguished affine connection representing the given projective structure an Einstein special statistical structure.

•

A notion of locally statistical structure, called an
AH (affine hypersurface)
structure that extends the usual notion in much the same way that Weyl structures extend pseudo-Riemannian metrics. Einstein equations are defined also for these more general AH structures.

The author studied these notions previously
[
57
,
59
,
60
]
in the more general context of AH structures and without reference to statistical structures as such. It is hoped that the exposition here will be more accessible.

The definition of Einstein equations for special statistical structures is less obvious than might be expected naively. It is not enough to require the vanishing of the trace-free Ricci tensor, nor that this hold for the statistical structure and its conjugate, as this need not imply constancy of some understandable quantity interpretable as a scalar curvature. It appears that such an additional condition must be imposed as part of the definition. Its formulation and motivation, which are both a bit subtle, are treated in Section
9
. They are such that a special statistical structure solves the Einstein equations for statistical structures if and only if its underlying metric solves the Einstein field equations with a stress-energy tensor built from its cubic form (see Corollary
9.13
for a precise statement and (
9.7
) for the definition of the stress-energy tensor).

Statistical structures interact with other geometric structures, such as almost complex or symplectic structures or generalized geometries. See
[
16
,
73
]
for examples.
Here a key role is played by the notion of a
radiant statistical structure
, which is a triple
(\nabla,h,\mathbb{E})
comprising a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
, a pseudo-Riemannian metric
h
, and a vector field
\mathbb{E}
such that
(\nabla,h)
is a statistical structure;
\mathbb{E}
is radiant, meaning
\nabla\mathbb{E}=\operatorname{Id}_{TM}
; and
h
is self-similar, meaning
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}h=2h
. Such structures occur naturally in a variety of contexts: on pointed convex cones as a consequence of deep work of Cheng-Yau
[
36
,
37
,
60
,
78
,
99
]
, total spaces of one-dimensional principal bundles over manifolds carrying particular geometric structures (see sections
8
-
10
), and in the construction of the Fefferman-Graham ambient metric
[
52
,
120
]
. From the point of view of the study of statistical structures the main results of the paper can be framed as extensions of these ideas:

•

Theorem
10.5
that shows that if the curvature of a principal connection on a one-dimensional principal bundle over a manifold equipped with an Einstein special statistical structure is compatible in a certain way with the statistical structure then on the total space of the principal bundle there is an Einstein AH structure built from the downstairs special statistical structure and the curvature of the principal connection. As is explained in more detail later in the introduction, this construction generalizes vastly the classical correspondence between projective space and affine space. It generalizes to statistical structures constructions associating Einstein-Weyl structures with positive scalar curvature Kähler-Einstein metrics
[
24
,
119
]
.

•

Theorem
10.6
applies Theorem
10.5
to show that if a principal circle bundle over a compact orientable surface of genus at least two has Euler number of absolute value less than minus the Euler characteristic of the surface then on the total space of the circle bundle there is a Lorentzian signature Einstein AH structure that, in a certain precise sense, induces on the base surface the Cheng-Yau Einstein special statistical structure of a properly convex projective structure.

The idea of a correspondence between geometric structures on a base manifold and other geometric structures on the total space of a fiber bundle over the base goes back in different forms and specific instances to Kaluza-Klein
[
134
]
, T. Y. Thomas
[
129
]
, and many others. In the context of statistical structures such constructions have been explored in various forms. In
[
109
]
, Matsuzoe and Inoguchi showed that the tangent bundle over a flat statistical manifold admits a natural complex structure compatible with a statistical structure whose underlying metric is the Norden metric. In
[
8
]
there were studied statistical structures with underlying metric a Sasaki metric and there was explored the case where the metric on the base manifold is Kähler. The results in Section
10
are analogous, with a one-dimensional principal bundle in place of the tangent bundle, and an Einstein statistical structure on the base.

The formulations and proofs of Theorems
10.5
and
10.6
are other related results utilize the notion of conelike radiant structure which gives the title of the article. The remainder of the introduction is devoted to motivating this notion, which has independent interest. The reader mainly interested in statistical structures can begin reading in Section
8
, although the results of the earlier sections are necessary for the proofs of the results in Section
10
.

The notion of
conelike radiant structure
formalizes the idea of a not necessarily flat affine connection equipped with a family of surfaces that behave like the intersections with a cone of the planes through the origin in a real vector space. A
radiant structure
on a smooth manifold
M
is a pair
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
comprising a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
and a vector field
\mathbb{E}
such that
\nabla\mathbb{E}
is the identity endomorphism of the tangent bundle
TM
. In a manifold with a radiant structure, a smoothly immersed surface is
planelike
if it is totally geodesic and everywhere tangent to
\mathbb{E}
. A radiant structure is
conelike
if it
admits a complete set of planelike surfaces
in the sense that for every point
p\in M
and every two-dimensional subspace
L\subset T_{p}M
that contains
\mathbb{E}_{p}
, there is a planelike surface
\Sigma\subset M
containing
p
and such that
T_{p}\Sigma=L
. The curvature of a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
means the curvature of
\nabla
. The flat radiant structure on a real vector space is that given by the flat affine structure and the Euler vector field generating dilations. The interior of a cone carries the flat conelike radiant structure generated by this radiant structure and whose planelike surfaces are its intersections with two-dimensional subspaces. The planelike surfaces of a conelike radiant structure generalize the two-dimensional subspaces through the origin in the flat model. Their dilation invariance is replaced by tangency to the radiant vector field, and their flatness is replaced by being totally geodesic.

Conelike radiant structures exist on manifolds with nontrivial topology. For example, by Theorem
6.2
, the total space of any principal
G
-bundle with
\dim G=1
admits conelike radiant structures and, by Theorem
11.4
, on a semisimple Lie group there is a conelike radiant structure associated with each adjoint orbit in its Lie algebra. More generally, by Theorem
11.2
, any Lie group whose Lie algebra is equipped with a structure of a quadratic Lie algebra admits a left-invariant conelike radiant structure. In general these conelike radiant structures are not flat, although their Ricci tensors are antisymmetric.

The projection
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
of the complement of the origin in an
(n+1)
-dimensional real vector space
\mathbb{V}
to the
n
-dimensional projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
is the model for the affine-projective/homogeneous-inhomogeneous paradigm that pervades much of geometry. Together with some additional functional data this setup yields projective and hyperbolic geometry. Work due in its essential points to Cheng-Yau
[
37
,
35
]
, establishing a picture conjectured by Calabi
[
19
,
20
]
and developed also partly in
[
97
,
121
]
, extends this basic picture to the context of affine spheres and the cones over them. This context includes the universal covers of properly convex flat real projective structures. It shows that the interior of any convex cone that is pointed (contains no half space) is foliated in a unique way by complete hyperbolic affine spheres asymptotic to the cone and having center at the vertex of the cone (see also
[
99
,
101
]
). The cone itself supports a related Lorentzian Monge-Ampere metric
[
60
]
(see also
[
21
,
102
]
for closely related constructions). For the Minkowski cone the foliating affine spheres are hyperboloids with Blaschke metrics homothetic to the hyperbolic metric and the associated Lorentzian Monge-Ampere metric is the Minkowski metric. The notion of conelike radiant structure introduced here makes it possible to generalize this picture in several ways - most obviously by dropping the flatness of the affine connection (equivalently the projective flatness of the downstairs projective structure) but also by abandoning convexity and replacing the Euler field generating the group of dilations on a vector space by a more general notion of Euler vector field.

Example
1
indicates in more detail the basic structural features whose generalization to a curved setting, culminating in the results of Section
10
, occupies much of the present article. Example
1
is written using notations that make it straightforward to see how it is a special case of results given later, such as Lemma
10.4
and Theorem
10.5
, that can be understood as extending it to a curved setting.

Example 1
.

Let
\rho:\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to M=\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
be the principal
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-bundle defining the oriented projectivization of the
(n+1)
-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{V}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{+}=GL^{+}(1,\mathbb{R})
is the multiplicative group of positive real numbers, regarded as a topological group.
Let
z^{I}
be coordinates on
\mathbb{V}
so that
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{I}}
is a parallel frame for the flat affine connection
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathbb{V}
. Where
z^{n+1}\neq 0
,
t=z^{n+1}
is a fiber coordinate corresponding with the flat principal
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-connection
\alpha=d\log t=d\log z^{n+1}
and determining local coordinates
x^{i}
on the base by
z^{i}=tx^{i}
, so that
(x^{i},t)
are coordinates adapted to the bundle structure in which
\mathbb{X}=t\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}=z^{I}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{I}}
is the usual Euler field. From
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}=t^{-1}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
and
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{n+1}}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}-t^{-1}x^{p}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{p}}
, it follows that the
\alpha
-horizontal lift of
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
is
z^{n+1}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}
. The connection
D
induced on the base by
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\alpha
is the flat connection with respect to which
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
is a parallel local frame. For a function
u
on the base
M
there holds

(1.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}\rho^{\ast}(du)=\rho^{\ast}(Ddu)-du\otimes d\log t-d\log t\otimes du.

Define
F=t^{2}\rho^{\ast}(u)
. The function
u
is related to
F
in that
u^{2}|dx^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{n}|^{-2/(n+1)}
is the expression in local coordinates of the density on
M
, viewed as a section of a line bundle associated with the principal bundle
\rho:\mathcal{F}\to M
, corresponding with the positive homogeneity
2
function
F
, viewed as an equivariant function on the total space of
\mathcal{F}
. Let
\gamma=-(1/2)d\log u
and define

(1.2)

\displaystyle\beta=\alpha-\rho^{\ast}(\gamma)=d\log t+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{\ast}(d\log u)=\tfrac{1}{2}F^{-1}dF.

When
\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{+}(F^{-1}(0))=u^{-1}(0)
is a submanifold,
\beta
is a principal
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-connection over the connected component
M^{+}=\{[z]\in M:F(z)>0\}
of
M\setminus\Sigma
.
The connection
\nabla
induced on
M^{+}
by
\widehat{\nabla}
and
\beta
equals
D+2\gamma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
and the
\beta
-horizontal lift of
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
is

(1.3)

\displaystyle z^{n+1}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}+\rho^{\ast}\left(\gamma(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}})\right)\mathbb{X}=z^{n+1}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{\ast}(u^{-1}\tfrac{\partial u}{\partial x^{i}})\mathbb{X}.

Because
D
is flat, by (
2.4
), the projective Schouten tensor
P
of
\nabla
is

(1.4)

\displaystyle P=D\gamma-\gamma\otimes\gamma=-\tfrac{1}{2}u^{-1}Ddu+\tfrac{1}{4}d\log u\otimes d\log u=-|u|^{-1/2}Dd|u|^{1/2}.

The tensors
H=\tfrac{1}{2}\widehat{\nabla}dF
and
G=\widehat{\nabla}\beta+2\beta\otimes\beta
are related by
H=FG
. Straightforward computation using (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) shows

(1.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}G&=F^{-1}H=\alpha\otimes\alpha-\rho^{\ast}(\gamma)\otimes\alpha-\alpha\otimes\rho^{\ast}(\alpha)+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{\ast}(u^{-1}Ddu)\\
&=\beta\otimes\beta-\rho^{\ast}(\gamma)\otimes\rho^{\ast}(\gamma)+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{\ast}(u^{-1}Ddu)=\beta\otimes\beta-\rho^{\ast}(P).\end{split}

Consider the volume forms
\mu=dx^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{n}
on
M^{+}
and
\Psi=d\log t\wedge\rho^{\ast}(\mu)=\beta\wedge\rho^{\ast}(\mu)
on
\mathcal{F}
. From (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) there follows

(1.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{\det\widehat{\nabla}dF}{\Psi^{\otimes 2}}&=2^{n+1}F^{n+1}\tfrac{\det G}{\Psi^{\otimes 2}}=(-1)^{n}2^{n+1}t^{2(n+1)}\rho^{\ast}\left(u^{n+1}\tfrac{\det P}{\mu^{\otimes 2}}\right)\\
&=2^{n+1}t^{2(n+1)}\rho^{\ast}\left((|u|^{1/2})^{n+2}\tfrac{\det Dd|u|^{1/2}}{\mu^{\otimes 2}}\right).\end{split}

In particular, there is a nonzero constant
\kappa
such that

(1.7)

\displaystyle\det\widehat{\nabla}dF=\kappa\Psi^{\otimes 2},

if and only if

\displaystyle\det Dd|u|^{1/2}=\kappa(|u|^{1/2})^{-(n+2)}\mu^{\otimes 2}.

In this case the level sets of
F
are affine spheres
[
60
,
99
,
101
]
. The solution of these equations on properly convex cones or domains under appropriate boundary/growth conditions is due to Cheng and Yau
[
35
,
37
,
36
]
. Klartag
[
84
]
has shown how to extend this picture to obtain incomplete affine spheres that are elliptic rather than hyperbolic. (The absolute values can be removed from (
1.7
) at the cost of distracting fussing about signs.) There seems to be no systematic study of (
1.7
) in the absence of convexity, though model solutions can be constructed from relative invariants of irreducible prehomogeneous vector spaces
[
61
]
.

For the simplest concrete example, let
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
and let
u(x)=1+\epsilon x^{i}x^{j}g_{ij}
, where
g_{ij}
is a
D
-parallel metric on the, so that
F(z)=(z^{n+1})^{2}+\epsilon z^{i}z^{j}\rho^{\ast}(g)_{ij}=t^{2}(1+\epsilon x^{i}x^{j}g_{ij})
. Since
F
is a quadratic polynomial,
\widehat{\nabla}H=0
. If
\epsilon=-1
, the level set
F^{-1}(0)
is the null cone of the Minkowski metric, and the submanifold
\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{+}(F^{-1}(0))
is an
(n-1)
sphere identified in the coordinates
x
on the base as the unit Euclidean sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, whereas if
\epsilon=1
then
F^{-1}(0)
is empty. Over the connected component
M^{+}
the one-form
\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}F^{-1}dF=d\log{t}+\epsilon\tfrac{x^{a}g_{ab}dx^{b}}{1+\epsilon|x|^{2}}
is a principal
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-connection. The
\beta
-horizontal lifts of the standard vector fields
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
are

(1.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-\epsilon\tfrac{x^{a}g_{ia}}{1+\epsilon|x|^{2}}\mathbb{X}=z^{n+1}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}-\epsilon\tfrac{z^{n+1}z^{a}g_{ia}}{F}\mathbb{X}.

Let
\nabla
be the unique symmetric representative of the standard flat projective structure on
\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
inducing
\beta
and let
P
be its projective Schouten tensor. Straightforward computation using the formulas just given shows

(1.9)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}=-\epsilon\tfrac{x^{a}g_{ia}}{1+\epsilon|x|^{2}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}-\epsilon\tfrac{x^{b}g_{jb}}{1+\epsilon|x|^{2}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},

\displaystyle P(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})=-\tfrac{\epsilon}{1+\epsilon|x|^{2}}\left(g_{ij}-\epsilon\tfrac{x^{a}x^{b}g_{ia}g_{jb}}{1+\epsilon|x|^{2}}\right).

Further computation shows that (whatever is
\epsilon
),
H=F\left(\beta\otimes\beta-\rho^{\ast}(P)\right)=F(\widehat{\nabla}\beta+2\beta\otimes\beta)
, and that
\nabla P=0
. Hence
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of the projectively flat constant sectional curvature
\epsilon
metric
-\epsilon P
. Note that
\nabla
is projectively equivalent to the flat Euclidean connection
D
and that
\epsilon P(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})=u^{-1}Ddu(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})
.

Other choices of
u
or
F
solving (
1.7
) yield more interesting geometries. A more interesting example, for which
g_{ij}
has indefinite signature is given by
u=v^{2}
where

(1.10)

\displaystyle v(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\left(x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+18x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}-4x_{1}x_{3}^{3}-4x_{2}^{3}-27x_{1}^{2}\right)^{1/4}

which solves
v^{-5}\det Dv=81/16
. Its radial graph is a level set of the discriminant of a binary cubic form and is a homogeneous indefinite signature proper affine sphere. See
[
61
,
60
,
99
,
101
,
102
]
for further examples and references.

The picture of Example
1
can be generalized by relaxing the conditions on the upstairs affine/homogeneous model comprising
\widehat{\nabla}
,
\mathbb{E}
, and the two-dimensional planes through the origin or on the downstairs projective/inhomogeneous model comprising
[\nabla]
and its geodesics. Although related, the two approaches do not necessarily lead to exactly the same constructions or point of view. The author took the latter approach in
[
56
,
57
,
59
]
. Here the first approach is taken, one point of departure being that flat radiant structures have already been studied extensively
[
39
,
63
,
65
]
. Simply allowing the connection to have curvature is the most naive generalization. Another is to view a cone as a principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
bundle and to consider instead principal
S^{1}
-bundles.

To begin, Section
3
explores more general notions of compatibility between a connection
\nabla
and a vector field
X
. Namely,
X
is said to be
dilatative
with respect to
\nabla
if
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}
for some smooth function
f
(this generalizes the notion of a vector field concircular with respect to a Levi-Civita connection). It is shown that off the zero set of
f
,
\nabla
and
X
can be modified to yield a radiant structure. A more general notion of a projectively dilatative vector field is also considered. The conclusion is that if
X
is projectively dilatative with respect to
\nabla
, then off the zero set of
X
it is dilatative with respect to a connection projectively equivalent to
\nabla
. These results motivate an initial focus on radiant structures and this is what is done in the rest of the paper. Further exploration of the more general notions remains as a potentially interesting project.

Section
3
generalizes to general radiant structures basic results about flat radiant structures due to Fried, Goldman, and Hirsch
[
63
,
65
]
. For example, Lemmas
3.8
and
3.10
show that a compact manifold supporting a radiant structure must have nonnegative Euler characteristic and admits no parallel volume form. A compact flat radiant manifold must have positive first Betti number. Since Theorem
12.9
shows that the three sphere admits a radiant structure, this fails for non-flat radiant structures, but Lemma
3.12
shows that a compact manifold with vanishing first Betti number admits no radiant structure with symmetric Ricci tensor. Benzecri
[
12
]
showed that a compact surface admits a flat affine structure if and only if it has vanishing Euler characteristic. Both the torus and Klein bottle admit flat radiant affine structures
[
4
,
5
,
116
,
11
]
. Theorem
3.15
extends these results to non-flat radiant structures, showing that a compact surface admits a radiant structure if and only if it has Euler characteristic zero. This is deduced as follows. Because by Lemma
3.8
the Euler characteristic must be nonnegative, it suffices to rule out positive Euler characteristic. Lemma
3.14
shows that a radiant structure on any surface has symmetric Ricci tensor and Lemma
3.11
shows that on a manifold with vanishing first Betti number a torsion-free affine connection with symmetric Ricci tensor admits a parallel volume density. As Lemma
3.10
precludes a parallel volume form for a radiant structure, this suffices. Note that a
volume density
means a nonvanishing
1
-density, which is a different object from a volume form and makes sense on a nonorientable manifold.
The rest of Section
3
records calculations regarding the curvature of radiant structures that are used throughout the remainder of the paper.

As remarked, the situation in dimension three and higher is different. There are manifolds that admit no flat radiant structure that admit radiant structures with purely antisymmetric Ricci tensor.

The volume form
\Psi
on
\mathbb{V}
in Example
1
is an additional bit of structure that plays a role in particular with respect to the formulation of the Monge-Ampere equations (
1.7
) solved by
F
and
u
. It is compatible with the radiant structure in that it is preserved by the affine connection
\widehat{\nabla}
and has homogeneity
\dim\mathbb{V}
with respect to the radiant vector field
\mathbb{E}
. Both of these conditions generalize in an obvious way to the context of not necessarily flat radiant structures to yield a notion of equiaffine radiant structure. The generalization of the compatibility of
\mathbb{E}
and
\Psi
leads to the notion of Euler structure, a vector field
\mathbb{E}
and volume form
\Psi
on an
n
-manifold
M
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=n\Psi
. Section
4
explores this notion and its relation to the notion of an Euler-like vector field studied in
[
18
,
74
,
111
]
. Although the two notions are independent, a radiant vector field is Euler-like.

Basic examples of Euler manifolds, needed later in Section
7
, are the total spaces of real line bundles whose local sections transform like densities of weight
-1/(n+1)
. A pseudo-hyperplane line bundle is a real line bundle
\mathcal{E}
equipped with an isomorphism
\mathcal{E}^{2n+2}\simeq(\operatorname{Det}TM)^{\otimes 2}
; its dual is called a pseudo-tautological line bundle. Up to isomorphism there is a unique such bundle with a given first Stiefel-Whitney class. The total space of any such line bundle carries in a canonical way a pseudo-Euler structure (
pseudo
means the volume form is replaced by a volume density; see section
4
for the precise definition). Such a pseudo-tautological line bundle is a real analogue of a square-root of the canonical bundle of a complex manifold, and generalizing the construction of the Blaschke metric of a properly convex flat real projective structure from the Cheng-Yau construction of affine spheres requires considering projectively invariant Monge-Ampère equations on sections of such line bundles; this is discussed in the author’s previous
[
56
,
57
,
59
]
and the author intends to discuss it further in future work formulating a projective analogue of the Calabi conjecture. See also
[
26
, Section
4
]
where these notions are discussed in the more general framework of parabolic geometries associated with a
|1|
-grading.

A radiant Euler structure is a radiant structure equipped with volume form
\Psi
that with
\mathbb{E}
constitutes an Euler structure. It is
equiaffine
if
\Psi
is parallel. Section
4
gives the basic properties of such structures.

Section
5
contains the main technical results about conelike radiant structures. The main conclusion of Theorem
5.6
is that given a conelike radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
with nonsingular radiant vector field
\mathbb{E}
and for which the Ricci curvature
\mathsf{Ric}
satisfies that the one-form
\rho=\mathsf{Ric}(\mathbb{E},\,\cdot\,\,)
vanishes, there is a unique
\mathbb{E}
-invariant connection that with
\mathbb{E}
constitutes a conelike radiant connection having the same planelike surfaces as
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, inducing on
|\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M|
the same connection as that induced by
\nabla
, and having antisymmetric Ricci tensor. (Whether the vanishing of
\rho
is merely a technical limitation of the proof or is really necessary is not resolved.)

From the point of view of differential geometry, the flat projective structure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
is determined by its projective geodesics, which are the images in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
of planes through the origin in
\mathbb{V}
. The classical construction of T. Y. Thomas
[
128
,
129
,
133
]
extends this picture to an
n
-manifold
M
equipped with a projective structure
[\nabla]
. It associates with
(M,[\nabla])
a principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-bundle
\rho:\mathcal{F}\to M
equipped with a Ricci-flat affine connection
\widehat{\nabla}
and a
\widehat{\nabla}
-parallel volume form
\Psi
satisfying certain certain conditions expressing compatibility between
\widehat{\nabla}
and the principal bundle structure and the projective structure on the base.
The Thomas construction is essentially local in nature and is usually built following a bottom up perspective, associating with a representative
\nabla\in[\nabla]
a lifted connection
\widehat{\nabla}
satisfying certain conditions, the most of important of which is Ricci-flatness, that a posteriori is seen to be independent of the choice of
\nabla
. The affine connection
\widehat{\nabla}
forms with (a multiple of) the vector field
\mathbb{E}
generating the dilations in the fibers of
\mathcal{F}
an example of a conelike radiant structure.

Specializing the results of Section
5
to the setting of the total space of a principal
G
-bundle over
M
with
\dim G=1
yields an extension of the Thomas construction that is a generalization in several respects. First, the principal bundle fibering over
M
need not be topologically trivial. An additional bit of data is the choice of a principal connection on this bundle, which no longer need be linked a priori to the projective structure
[\nabla]
. Rather the basic data is an
extended projective structure

[\nabla,\beta]
comprising an orbit of pairs
(\nabla,\beta)
where
\nabla
is a connection and
\beta
is a principal connection under the action
\gamma\cdot(\nabla,\beta)=(\nabla+2\gamma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k},\beta-\rho^{\ast}(\gamma))
. This is explained and motivated in Section
6
. It would be interesting to explore further couplings of a projective structure to a principal connection with higher-dimensional or nonabelian structure group, or to some further data on an associated vector bundle. In the context of flat bundles on affine manifolds, something along these lines has been studied in
[
14
]
.

The Thomas connection is closely related to the tractor connection associated with the projective structure in the parabolic geometry formalism
[
27
,
25
]
and from either of these essentially equivalent structures there can be constructed the regular normal Cartan connection associated with the the underlying projective structure
[
88
]
.
Projective structures are a particular kind of parabolic geometry. General theorems associate with a parabolic geometry satisfying certain conditions a regular, normal Cartan connection. The adjectives regular and normal refer to certain normalizations on the curvature of the associated Cartan connection that in the context of projective connections amount in essence to the requirement that the classical Thomas connection be Ricci-flat. The general theory interprets these normalizations in terms of the harmonic Hodge theory due to Kostant, and these normalizations are satisfying from the point of view of representation theory. However, their geometric significance is in general somewhat opaque. In section
6
it is shown that the geometric significance of the Ricci-flat normalization for projective structures is that it links parametrizations of projective geodesics with parametrizations of their lifts. Precisely, without this condition an unnormalized Thomas-like connection determines a projective structure on each projective geodesic, and the Ricci-flat normalization forces this induced projective structure to be the standard one.

Section
7
shows how to recover the classical Thomas connection from the formalism described here. It extends the classical construction to associate with a projective structure on
M
a Thomas connection on the total space of any pseudo-tautological line bundle over
M
. This clarifies the global and functorial properties of the classical construction.

To fully develop the picture corresponding to Example
1
, there remains to consider the relation of metric geometry with a conelike radiant structure. Section
8
treats metrics compatible with a radiant structure and what it means for such structures to be conelike. Given a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, a metric
h
can be compatible with each of
\nabla
and
\mathbb{E}
. Following
[
120
]
, the metric is
self-similar
if
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}h=2h
. A
radiant Hessian structure
is a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
and a self-similar metric
h
such that
\nabla h(\mathbb{E},\,\cdot\,\,)=h
. In this case
h
is the Hessian of
v=\tfrac{1}{2}h(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{E})
and Lemma
8.9
shows that around any point where
v
is not zero there is a neighborhood on which
h
is isometric to a warped product. A triple
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},h)
such that
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is radiant and
\nabla_{[i}h_{j]k}=0
is a
radiant statistical structure
. The metric of a radiant statistical structure is necessarily self-similar, so such a structure is a radiant Hessian structure. The usual involutive notion of conjugacy (also called duality) of statistical structures extends to radiant statistical structures.
The conjugate radiant statistical structure
(\bar{\nabla},\mathbb{E},h)
has the same underlying self-similar metric structure
(h,\mathbb{E})
. A radiant statistical structure with nonsingular radiant vector field and its conjugate are both conelike if and only if the connection of each is
\mathbb{E}
-invariant.
A radiant statistical structure is
special
if
\nabla|\det h|=0
. Theorem
10.3
shows that there is a metric
g
that constitutes with the Thomas connection of a projective structure a special radiant statistical structure if and only if the
g
-conjugate connection of the Thomas connection is also the Thomas connection of a projective structure. For properly convex flat projective structures this recovers the duality of affine spheres, and so it shows how to extend this duality to the nonconvex and nonflat settings.

It is useful to generalize the notion of radiant statistical structure by replacing the statistical condition
\nabla_{[i}g_{j]k}=0
with the condition
\nabla_{[i}g_{j]k}=\chi_{[i}h_{j]k}
for some one-form
\chi_{i}
. Although this condition behaves well with respect to conformal rescaling of the metric
g
, it does not link
\nabla
with the density
\det g
in any way. A pair
(\nabla,[g])
comprising a conformal structure
[g]
and a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
is an
AH structure
if for each
g\in[g]
there is a one-form
\chi_{i}
such that
\nabla_{[i}g_{j]k}=\chi_{[i}h_{j]k}
and there holds the
alignment
condition that
g^{pq}\nabla_{i}g_{pq}=ng^{pq}\nabla_{p}g_{iq}
(this does not depend on the choice of
g\in[g]
). The basic properties of AH structures generalize those of statistical structures, with suitable modifications to accommodate
\chi
. Although these are all given in the author’s
[
56
,
57
,
59
,
60
]
, they are developed from scratch in Section
9
in a manner more streamlined and hopefully more readable than in
[
56
,
57
,
59
]
. AH structures are
locally statistical structures
in a sense made precise by Lemma
9.2
.

The conclusion of Lemma
10.4
illustrates the need for the notion of AH structure. It shows that associated with a special statistical structure and a connection
\beta
on a principal bundle with one-dimensional structure group compatible in an appropriate sense with the metric of the statistical structure there is on the total space of the principal bundle a pair of connections
\hat{D}
and a metric
G_{IJ}
satisfying
\hat{D}_{[I}G_{J]K}=\chi_{[I}G_{J]k}
, where
\chi
is a constant multiple of
\beta
, and such that
\hat{D}
has Ricci tensor equal to a multiple of the metric. More detailed background and motivation for Lemma
10.4
and the other results of Section
10
is given at the beginning of that section.

More fundamentally, the usual Einstein-Weyl equations can be extended to AH structures as well and Theorem
10.5
shows that in the setting of Lemma
10.4
, with suitable additional geometrica hypotheses, there result Einstein AH structures on
N
. Section
9
gives a self-contained introduction to the Einstein equations for special statistical structures and AH structures. The precise notion is Definition
9.7
which is a reformulation (with slightly different terminology) of the Einstein equations for AH structures defined and studied previously in
[
56
,
57
,
59
,
58
,
62
,
60
]
. A special case of the definition yields a notion of Einstein equations for special statistical structures, given in Definition
9.11
, that is of independent interest. The motivating observation is that the statistical structure induced on a cooriented nondegenerate hypersurface in flat affine space is Einstein if and only if the hypersurface is an affine sphere. For a flat ambient connection the generalization of Example
1
for a convex cone is given by the Blaschke metric on the associated affine sphere. The special statistical structure induced on an affine hypersurface is Einstein in the sense defined here if and only if the hypersurface is an affine sphere, so the Einstein equations for special statistical structures, or more generally AH structures, can be regarded as a generalization of the equations for affine spheres and correspondingly as the appropriate equations for generalizing Example
1
.

As explained in Example
30
, a special case of Theorem
10.5
recovers a construction associating with a Kähler-Einstein metric of positive scalar curvature an Einstein-Weyl structure due to
[
119
]
. It also yields the following, detailed in Example
31
and summarized in Theorem
10.6
. Given a properly convex flat real projective structure
[\nabla]
on an oriented surface
M
of genus
g\geq 2
, there is a distinguished representative
\nabla\in[\nabla]
with symmetric Ricci tensor
R_{ij}
such that
g_{ij}=-R_{ij}
is positive definite and satisfies
\nabla_{[i}g_{j]k}=0
and
\nabla_{i}|\det g|=0
. Namely,
g_{ij}
and
\nabla
are induced by the Blaschke metric and induced affine connection on the hyperbolic sphere asymptotic to the cone over the universal cover of
M
. See
[
57
]
for details. For the complex structure
J
on
M
determined by
g_{ij}
and the given orientation, the two-form
\omega=\tfrac{2\pi e}{\mathsf{vol}_{g}(M)^{2}}g(J(\,\cdot\,\,),\,\cdot\,\,)
represents the first Chern class of a principal circle bundle
\rho:N\to M
equipped with a principal connection
\beta
having curvature
d\beta=\rho^{\ast}(\omega)
provided
|e|\leq|\chi(M)|
.
Example
31
shows that in this case there are on
N
a Lorentzian metric
G_{IJ}
and a one-parameter family of connections
\hat{D}
satisfying
\hat{D}_{[I}G_{J]K}=-2s\beta_{[I}G_{J]K}
,
\hat{D}_{I}G_{JK}=-2s\beta_{I}G_{JK}+\rho^{\ast}(\nabla g)_{IJK}
,
G^{PQ}\hat{D}_{I}G_{PQ}-3G^{PQ}\hat{D}_{P}G_{QI}=0
, and
\mathsf{Ric}(\hat{D})_{IJ}=-\tfrac{3}{2}s\rho^{\ast}(\omega)_{IJ}-\tfrac{2\pi^{2}e^{2}}{\mathsf{vol}_{g}(M)^{2}}G_{IJ}
. Moreover,
(\hat{D},[G])
is an Einstein AH structure in the sense of Definition
9.7
. In the special case that
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of the hyperbolic metric
g
, then
(\hat{D},[G])
is a Lorentzian Einstein-Weyl structure.

Section
11
constructs left-invariant conelike radiant structures on Lie groups. By Theorem
11.4
, with the adjoint orbit of a Killing anisotropic element of the Lie algebra of a Lie group there is a conelike radiant structure determined uniquely up to automorphism. This has interest independent of the main thrust of the article, as it associates a canonical geometric structure with every Killing anisotropic adjoint orbit. It should be contrasted with the theorem of Helmstetter
[
76
]
showing that a semisimple Lie group carries no flat affine structure (so in particular no flat radiant structure).

In section
12
the three-dimensional unimodular Lie groups are given a uniform description in terms of real Clifford algebras and the conelike radiant structures on them obtained by applying the results of the preceding section are described in detail. The resulting radiant affine connections have purely antisymmetric Ricci tensor, so are in some sense as close to flat as is possible.
As an example, it is explained how to construct from them the well-known Einstein-Weyl structures on the Berger spheres and their Lorentzian analogues on
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
. This gives a novel approach to constructing these Einstein-Weyl structures that grounds them in the classical affine/projective dichotomy and situates them in the landscape of statistical manifolds.

Notational conventions

Smooth means infinitely differentiable and all manifolds, bundles, sections, etc. are smooth unless otherwise indicated. Throughout
M
is a connected, smooth
n
-manifold. The notation
Tf(u)(X)
indicates the image of a vector
X\in T_{u}M
tangent to
M
at
u\in M
under the differential of a smooth map
f
with domain containing
u
.

Given a free
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
action
R:E\times\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\to E
by diffeomorphisms on a smooth manifold
E
, a tensor
S
on
E
is
homogeneous
(resp.
positively homogeneous
) of degree
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
if
R_{r}^{\ast}(S)=r^{\lambda}S
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
(resp. for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
).

Where convenient, tensors are indicated using the abstract index notation, so that, for instance,
a_{ij}
indicates a covariant two tensor. The indices are lables indicating tensor valencies, types, and symmetries and do not indicate any choice of frame, although were a frame fixed they could be interpreted as indicating components with respect to it. Enclosure of indices in square brackets or parentheses indicates complete antisymmetrization or symmetrization over the enclosed indices. For example,
a^{ij}=a^{(ij)}+a^{[ij]}
indicates the decomposition of a contravariant two-tensor into its symmetric and antisymmetric parts.
The exterior product is defined consistently with the convention that
X\wedge Y=X\otimes Y-Y\otimes X
for vector fields
X
and
Y
, so
(X\wedge Y)^{ij}=2X^{[i}Y^{j]}
.
Inclusion of an index between vertical bars
|\,|
indicates its omission from an indicated symmetrization. For example
2a_{[i|jk|l]}=a_{ijkl}-a_{ljki}
. The summation convention is always in effect in the following form: indices are in either
up
position or
down
and the trace pairing of the corresponding tensor factors is indicated by repeating a label appearing as both an up index and a down index. For example
a_{pij}\,^{p}
indicates the pairing of the contravariant part with the first covariant factor. Since polynomials on the vector space
\mathbb{V}
are tautologically identified with symmetric tensors on the dual vector space
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, the index
i
in
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}
has to be regarded as an
up
index.
Although in common use by relativists and conformal geometers, these conventions sometimes bother those who dislike coordinate expressions, so it bears repeating that abstract indices
do not
refer to coordinates and are simply a notationally compact way of indicating invariantly defined objects. In the same spirit, those who use abstract indices sometimes insist that every tensor be labeled with indices. Here indices are omitted when they communicate little. So a metric is sometimes written
g_{ij}
, sometimes simply
g
(this is no different than writing sometimes
g(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)
and sometimes simply
g
) and
g(X,Y)
and
g_{ij}X^{i}Y^{j}
are notational
synonyms
.

A line-bundle valued tensor is said to be
weighted
. A nondegenerate, possibly weighted, covariant two-tensor
h_{ij}
determines a contravariant two-tensor
h^{ij}
of complementary weight (valued in the dual line bundle) defined uniquely by
h^{ip}h_{jp}=\delta_{j}\,^{i}
, in which here, as always,
\delta_{i}\,^{j}
is the tautological
\binom{1}{1}
-tensor determined by the pairing of vectors with covectors.
The horizontal position of an index is maintained when it is raised or lowered using
h^{ij}
or
h_{ij}
.

2.
Background on projective structures

The basic facts about projective structures go back to H. Weyl, E. Cartan, and T. Y. Thomas and are well assimilated into an extensive literature although it seems no one source collects all the relevant information. What follows summarizes what is needed here. More detail from a similar perspective is available in
[
70
]
.
Terminology is abused in the traditional way (e.g.
[
89
, section III.
3
]
) and
affine connection
is used to mean a linear connection, or the covariant derivative it induces on the tangent bundle or any other tensor bundle.

A
path geometry
on a smooth manifold
M
is a one-dimensional foliation
W\subset T\mathbb{P}(TM)
of the projectivized tangent bundle
\rho:\mathbb{P}(TM)\to M
that is transverse to the vertical subbundle
\ker T\rho
and tangent to the Cartan subbundle
E\subset T\mathbb{P}(TM)
defined by
E_{\ell}=\{X\in T_{\ell}\mathbb{P}(TM):T\rho(\ell)X\in\ell\}
. Its
paths
are the images in
M
of the maximal integral submanifolds of
W
. A path geometry is a
projective structure
if there is on
M
a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
such that the images of the geodesics of
\nabla
are contained in its paths.
Two affine connections are
projectively equivalent
if the image of every geodesic of one is contained in the image of a geodesic of the other. Two torsion-free affine connections
\bar{\nabla}
and
\nabla
are projectively equivalent if and only if there is a one-form
\sigma_{i}
such that
\bar{\nabla}-\nabla=2\sigma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
. A projective structure is identified with the equivalence class
[\nabla]
of projectively equivalent connections determining its paths. Here, a representative
\nabla\in[\nabla]
is always assumed to be torsion-free.

Let
\mathbb{V}
be a vector space with projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
and let
\operatorname{Gr}(2,\mathbb{V})
be the Grassmannian of two-dimensional subspaces. The flat model path geometry is given by the foliation of the flag manifold
\{([\ell,K]\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\times\operatorname{Gr}(2,\mathbb{V}):\ell\subset K\}
by the fibers of the projection onto the second factor. The paths in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
are the images in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
of the two-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{V}
. This path geometry is a projective structure, and a projective structure is
flat
if it is locally equivalent to this model. In this case it can be described in terms of an atlas of charts modeled on open domains in
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
with transition functions in
PGL(\mathbb{V})
, and a projective structure is flat if and only if it can be covered by coordinate charts in which the images of its geodesics are contained in straight lines. See
[
66
]
for background on flat projective structures.

A projective structure determines and is determined by a regular normal Cartan connection that can be constructed via the Thomas connection
[
129
,
7
]
, via the tractor formalism
[
25
]
, working with jet bundles
[
88
]
, or via the method of equivalence
[
72
]
and the claims about local flatness that follow can all be proved using this correspondence; see
[
27
]
for a modern approach in a more general setting.

The curvature
R_{ijk}\,^{l}
and torsion
\tau_{ij}\,^{k}
of an affine connection
\nabla
are defined by
2\nabla_{[i}\nabla_{j]}X^{k}=R_{ijp}\,^{k}X^{p}-\tau_{ij}\,^{p}\nabla_{p}X^{k}
for a vector field
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. The
Ricci curvature
of
\nabla
is
R_{ij}=R_{pij}\,^{p}
. The curvature of the covariant derivative induced on
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M
by a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
is
-R_{ijp}\,^{p}=2R_{[ij]}
, and the
projective Weyl
and
projective Cotton
tensors
B_{ijk}\,^{l}
and
C_{ijk}
of
\nabla
are defined by

(2.1)

\displaystyle B_{ijk}\,^{l}=R_{ijk}\,^{l}+2\delta_{[i}\,^{l}P_{j]k}-2\delta_{k}\,^{l}P_{[ij]},

\displaystyle C_{ijk}=2\nabla_{[i}P_{j]k},

in which the
projective Schouten tensor
is defined by

(2.2)

\displaystyle P_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{1-n}R_{(ij)}-\tfrac{1}{n+1}R_{[ij]}=\tfrac{1}{1-n}\left(R_{ij}-\tfrac{2}{n+1}R_{[ij]}\right).

Tracing the differential Bianchi identity yields
\nabla_{p}R_{ijk}\,^{p}=2\nabla_{[i}R_{j]k}
and so the algebraic Bianchi identity yields
0=\nabla_{p}R_{[ijk]}\,^{p}=2\nabla_{[i}R_{jk]}=2(n+1)\nabla_{[i}P_{jk]}=(n+1)C_{[ijk]}
, showing that
C_{[ijk]}=0
. In a similar fashion the trace-free parts of the Bianchi identities for
\nabla
yield:

(2.3)

\displaystyle B_{[ijk]}\,^{l}=0,

\displaystyle C_{[ijk]}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{[i}B_{jk]l}\,^{p}=-\delta_{[i}\,^{p}C_{jk]l},

\displaystyle\nabla_{p}B_{ijk}\,^{p}=(2-n)C_{ijk}.

The Ricci identity and the algebraic Bianchi identity yield
\nabla_{[i}C_{jk]l}=2\nabla_{[i}\nabla_{j}P_{k]l}=-R_{[ij|l|}\,^{p}P_{k]p}=-B_{[ij|l|}\,^{p}P_{k]p}
, or
\nabla_{[i}C_{jk]l}+P_{[i|p|}B_{jk]l}\,^{p}=0
.
The corresponding tensors associated with
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+2\sigma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
are indicated
\tilde{R}_{ijk}\,^{l}
,
\tilde{P}_{ij}
, etc., and satisfy

(2.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{R}_{ijk}\,^{l}&=R_{ijk}\,^{l}+(\nabla_{i}\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{i}\sigma_{k})\delta_{j}\,^{l}-(\nabla_{j}\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k})\delta_{i}\,^{l}+d\sigma_{ij}\delta_{k}\,^{l},\\
\tilde{R}_{ij}&=R_{ij}+(1-n)(\nabla_{i}\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})-d\sigma_{ij},\\
\tilde{P}_{ij}&=P_{ij}+\nabla_{i}\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j},\qquad\tilde{C}_{ijk}=C_{ijk}-B_{ijk}\,^{p}\sigma_{p}.\end{split}

By (
2.4
), the projective Weyl tensor satisfies
\tilde{B}_{ijk}\,^{l}=B_{ijk}\,^{l}
, so does not depend on the choice of
\nabla\in[\nabla]
. When
n=2
the projective Weyl tensor is identically zero and, by (
2.4
), the projective Cotton tensor does not depend on the choice of
\nabla\in[\nabla]
.
A projective structure is flat if and only if its projective Cotton and Weyl tensors vanish. By (
2.3
), when
n>2
this is the case if and only if the projective Weyl tensor vanishes.

The remainder of this section details the well known fact that a projective geodesic of a projective structure acquires in a canonical manner a projective structure
[
75
,
87
,
136
]
. Although this material is well known, it is hard to find it written in the form needed here, so precise statements are given (although proofs are only indicated). The key point, that is needed in the proof of Lemma
6.12
, is the definition of the projective parametrization of a projective geodesic.
This uses some elementary facts relating initial value problems for second order linear ordinary differential equations, Ricatti differential equations, and the Schwarzian derivative.

Let
M
be equipped with a projective structure
[\nabla]
. If
\tilde{\nabla},\nabla\in[\nabla]
and
f\neq 0
, then
fX\wedge\tilde{\nabla}_{fX}(fX)=f^{3}(X\wedge\nabla_{X}X)
for any local vector field
X
, so the condition
X\wedge\nabla_{X}X=0
is preserved if
\nabla
is varied within
[\nabla]
or
X
is rescaled. A
C^{1}
immersed one-dimensional submanifold
C\subset M
is a
projective geodesic
of
[\nabla]
if
X\wedge\nabla_{X}X=0
for any local vector field tangent to
C
along
C
and any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
. Equivalently,
\dot{\gamma}\wedge\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}=0
for any local
C^{2}
parametrization
\gamma:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to C
and any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
. Locally any projective geodesic can be parametrized as a geodesic of a given
\nabla\in[\nabla]
, but in general such a parametrization is not possible globally. The image of a geodesic of any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
is called a
path
or
projective geodesic
of
[\nabla]
.

The Schwarzian derivative
S(f)
of a
C^{3}
diffeomorphism
f:I\to J
between subintervals
I
and
J
of
\mathbb{R}
is defined by
S(f)=\dddot{f}/\dot{f}-\tfrac{3}{2}\left(\ddot{f}/\dot{f}\right)^{2}
, where dots denote derivatives.
If
\phi
is a
C^{3}
diffeomorphism, then, where the composition is defined, there holds the cocycle identity

(2.5)

\displaystyle S(f\circ\phi)=\dot{\phi}^{2}S(f)\circ\phi+S(\phi),

which in part reflects that
S(f)(t)
is the coordinate expression of the quadratic differential
S(f)(t)dt^{2}
.

Lemma
2.1
follows straightforwardly from the standard existence and regularity theorem for a first order linear system of ordinary differential equations
[
41
, Theorem 5.1 of Chapter
1
]
, so its proof is omitted.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open interval containing
0
and let
r\in C^{k}(I)
for some
k\geq 0
. For
a,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
, there are an open interval
J\subset I
containing
0
and a unique
f\in C^{k+3}(J)
solving the initial value problem

(2.6)

\displaystyle S(f)=2r,

\displaystyle f(0)=a,

\displaystyle\dot{f}(0)=b\neq 0,

\displaystyle\ddot{f}(0)=c,

and such that
\dot{f}
does not vanish on
J
. Moreover:

(1)

The solution
f
has the form
f=x_{1}/x_{2}
where, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in C^{k+2}(J)
are the uniquely determined solutions of the initial value problem

(2.7)

\displaystyle\ddot{x}+rx=0,

\displaystyle x(0)=x_{0},

\displaystyle\dot{x}(0)=y_{0},

satisfying
x_{1}(0)=2ab\lambda
,
\dot{x}_{1}(0)=(2b^{2}-ac)\lambda
,
x_{2}(0)=2b\lambda
, and
\dot{x}_{2}(0)=-c\lambda
.

(2)

Given
f\in C^{k+3}(J)
solving (
2.6
) and with
\dot{f}
not vanishing on
J
, the functions
x_{1}=f|\dot{f}|^{-1/2}
and
x_{2}=|\dot{f}|^{-1/2}
solve (
2.7
) on
J
with initial conditions
x_{1}(0)=2ab\lambda
,
\dot{x}_{1}(0)=(2b^{2}-ac)\lambda
and
x_{2}(0)=2b\lambda
,
\dot{x}_{2}(0)=-c\lambda
, where
\lambda=|b|^{-1/2}b^{-1}
.

(3)

Given
f
solving (
2.6
) and
x_{1}=f|\dot{f}|^{-1/2}
and
x_{2}=|\dot{f}|^{-1/2}
, the functions
u=\dot{x}_{2}/x_{2}=-(1/2)(\ddot{f}/\dot{f})
and
v=\dot{x}_{1}/x_{1}=u+\dot{f}/f
are the unique solutions in
C^{k+1}(J)
of the initial value problem

(2.8)

\displaystyle\dot{w}+w^{2}+r=0,

\displaystyle w(0)=w_{0}

with initial conditions
u(0)=-c/(2b)
and
v(0)=(2b^{2}-2ac)/2ab=b/a-c/(2b)
.

Example 2
.

For constants
a,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
, the unique solution of the initial value problem

(2.9)

\displaystyle S(f)=0,

\displaystyle f(0)=a,

\displaystyle\dot{f}(0)=b\neq 0,

\displaystyle\ddot{f}(0)=c.

is the linear fractional transformation
f(t)=\tfrac{(ac-2b^{2})t-2ab}{ct-2b}
.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open interval. Functions
f,g\in C^{3}(I)
satisfy
S(f)=S(g)
on
I
if and only if there is a linear fractional transformation
\phi
mapping
g(I)
to
f(I)
such that
f=\phi\circ g
.

Proof.

This follows from the cocycle identity (
2.5
), Lemma
2.1
, and Example
2

∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
[\nabla]
be a projective structure on a smooth manifold
M
. Let
C\subset M
be a projective geodesic. For any
p\in C
there are an open interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
containing
0
and a
C^{2}
immersion
\gamma:I\to M
such that
\gamma(0)=p
,
\gamma(I)\subset C
, and such that the function
q\in C^{1}(I)
defined by
\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}=q\dot{\gamma}
satisfies

(2.10)

\displaystyle\dot{q}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}-2P(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})=0.

The immersion
\gamma
is determined uniquely up to precomposition with a linear fractional transformation. As a consequence
C
acquires in a canonical way a projective structure.

Proof.

Let
C\subset M
be a projective geodesic. Let
I
and
\bar{I}
be open subintervals of
\mathbb{R}
and let
\phi:I\to\bar{I}
be a
C^{3}
diffeomorphism. Let
\gamma:I\to M
and
\bar{\gamma}:\bar{I}\to M
be
C^{2}
immersions such that
\gamma(I),\bar{\gamma}(\bar{I})\subset C
and
\gamma=\bar{\gamma}\circ\phi
. Consider
I
and
\bar{I}
with the flat affine structures induced from that on
\mathbb{R}
and let
t
and
\bar{t}
be affine coordinates in
I
and
\bar{I}
. Write derivatives with respect to
t
using
\dot{\,}
and derivatives with respect to
\bar{t}
using
\prime
. For example,
\dot{\gamma}=\dot{\phi}(\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\circ\phi)
. Let
\nabla
and
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+2\sigma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
be torsion-free representatives of
[\nabla]
. By (
2.4
), their projective Schouten tensors satisfy
\tilde{P}_{(ij)}=P_{(ij)}+\nabla_{(i}\sigma_{j)}-\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}
. Since
C
is a projective geodesic there are functions
q,\tilde{q}\in C^{1}(I)
and
\bar{Q}\in C^{1}(\bar{I})
such that
\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}=q\dot{\gamma}
,
\nabla_{\frac{d}{d\bar{t}}}\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}=\bar{q}\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}
, and
\tilde{\nabla}_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}=\tilde{q}\dot{\gamma}
. From

(2.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{\phi}q(\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\circ\phi)&=q\dot{\gamma}=\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}=\dot{\phi}^{2}\left(\nabla_{d/d\bar{t}}\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\right)\circ\phi+\ddot{\phi}(\bar{\gamma}^{\prime})\circ\phi=\dot{\phi}^{2}\left(\bar{q}\circ\phi+\ddot{\phi}/\dot{\phi}\right)\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}\circ\phi,\end{split}

there follows
q=\dot{\phi}\bar{q}\circ\phi+\ddot{\phi}/\dot{\phi}
, so that

(2.12)

\displaystyle\dot{q}=\dot{\phi}^{2}(\bar{q}^{\prime}\circ\phi)+\ddot{\phi}(\bar{q}\circ\phi)+\left(\ddot{\phi}/\dot{\phi}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle q^{2}=(\bar{q}\circ\phi)^{2}+2\ddot{\phi}(\bar{q}\circ\phi)+\left(\ddot{\phi}/\dot{\phi}\right)^{2},

from which there follows

(2.13)

\displaystyle\dot{q}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}-2P(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})=\dot{\phi}^{2}\left(\bar{q}^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{q}^{2}-2P(\bar{\gamma}^{\prime},\bar{\gamma}^{\prime})\right)\circ\phi+S(\phi).

From
\tilde{q}\dot{\gamma}=\tilde{\nabla}_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma}=\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}+2\sigma(\dot{\gamma})\dot{\gamma}=(q+2\sigma(\dot{\gamma}))\dot{\gamma}
, it follows that
\tilde{q}=q+2\sigma(\dot{\gamma})
, so that

(2.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{\tilde{q}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\tilde{q}^{2}-2\tilde{P}(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})&=\dot{q}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}+2\tfrac{d}{dt}\sigma(\dot{\gamma})-2P(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})-2(\nabla_{d/dt}\sigma)(\dot{\gamma})-2\sigma(\dot{\gamma})=\dot{q}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}-2P(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma}),\end{split}

so that the function
\kappa=\dot{q}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}-2P(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})\in C^{1}(I)
does not depend on the choice of
\nabla\in[\nabla]
. Likewise the function
\bar{\kappa}=\bar{q}^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{q}^{2}-2P(\bar{\gamma}^{\prime},\bar{\gamma}^{\prime})\in C^{1}(\bar{I})
does not depend on the choice of
\nabla\in[\nabla]
. By (
2.13
),

(2.15)

\displaystyle\kappa=\dot{\phi}^{2}\bar{\kappa}\circ\phi+S(\phi).

By (
2.5
),
\bar{f}\in C^{3}(I)
solves
S(\bar{f})=\bar{\kappa}
if and only if
f=\bar{f}\circ\phi
solves
S(f)=\kappa
.

Given
\gamma
, and so
q
and
\kappa
, replacing
I
by a subinterval if necessary, by Lemma
2.1
there can be found a
C^{4}
diffeomorphism
\phi
such that
S(\phi)=\kappa
on
I
. In this case
\bar{\kappa}=0
. Regard
U=\gamma(I)=\bar{\gamma}(\bar{I})
as an open set in
C
and
\bar{\gamma}^{-1}:U\to\mathbb{R}
as a chart. In this chart,
\bar{\kappa}=0
. Such a chart can be constructed around any point of
C
, and on the overlap the transition functions must have vanishing Schwarzian derivative, so are restrictions of elements of
PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
, hence these charts determine a projective structure on
C
.
∎

Lemma
2.3
motivates the following definition. A smooth parametrization
\gamma:I\to M
of a projective geodesic is
projective parametrization
if the associated function
\kappa=\dot{q}-\tfrac{1}{2}q^{2}-2P(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})
vanishes on
I
.

A more geometric description of the projective structure induced on a projective geodesic is given by Lemmas
3.7
and
6.12
.

The infinitesimal variation of a projective geodesic,
C\subset M
, through projective geodesics is described by a section of the normal bundle
\nu(C)=TM_{|C}/TC
of
C
. Precisely, let
\bar{\gamma}(t,s):(a,b)\times(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to M
be a
C^{2}
immersion such that for each
s
the image of
\bar{\gamma}_{s}=\bar{\gamma}(\,\cdot\,\,,s)
is contained in a projective geodesic, and so that
\bar{\gamma}(t,0)=\gamma(t)
is a parametrization of
\bar{\gamma}((a,b)\times\{0\})\subset C
. Let
\nabla\in[\nabla]
,
\bar{v}=\frac{\partial\bar{\gamma}}{\partial t}
,
\bar{Y}=\frac{\partial\bar{\gamma}}{\partial s}
and let
Y(t)=\bar{Y}(t,0)
. The assumption
C
is a projective geodesic means
\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}=0
for each
s\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. The vector field
Y
depends on the chosen parametrization of
C
, but changing this parametrization modifies
Y
only by the addition of a vector field tangent to
C
.

From
\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}=0
it follows that
\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{Y}=\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}}\bar{Y}
. Differentiating
\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}=0
and using the preceding observation and also
\nabla_{\bar{Y}}\bar{v}=\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{Y}
to simplify the result gives

(2.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\nabla_{\bar{Y}}\left(\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}\right)=-\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{Y}+\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\bar{Y}}\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}=-\bar{v}\wedge\nabla_{\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}}\bar{Y}+\bar{v}\wedge\left(\nabla_{\bar{v}}\nabla_{\bar{Y}}\bar{v}+R(\bar{Y},\bar{v})\bar{v}\right)\\
&=\bar{v}\wedge\left(\nabla_{\bar{v}}\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{Y}-\nabla_{\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{v}}\bar{Y}+R(\bar{Y},\bar{v})\bar{v}\right)=\bar{v}\wedge(\mathfrak{L}_{\bar{Y}}\nabla)(\bar{v},\bar{v}),\end{split}

where the Lie derivative
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\nabla
of an affine connection
\nabla
along a vector field
X
is defined by differentiating the difference tensor of the pullback of
\nabla
via the flow of
X
with
\nabla
. Define the difference tensor
[\bar{\nabla}]-[\nabla]
of the projective structures
[\bar{\nabla}]
and
[\nabla]
to be the trace-free part of the difference tensor
[\bar{\nabla}]-[\nabla]
of any representatives
\bar{\nabla}\in[\bar{\nabla}]
and
\nabla\in[\nabla]
, which does not depend on the choices of representatives. The Lie derivative
\mathfrak{L}_{X}[\nabla]
is defined to be the derivative at
t=0
of the difference tensor
\phi_{t}^{\ast}[\nabla]-[\nabla]
where
\phi_{t}
is the local flow generated by
X\in\Gamma(TM)
. For any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
,
(\mathfrak{L}_{X}[\nabla])_{ij}\,^{k}
equals the trace-free part
(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}-\tfrac{2}{n+1}\delta_{(i}\,^{k}\nabla_{j)}\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)
of
(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}X^{k}+X^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}
.
If
\nabla
is replaced in (
2.16
) by
\tilde{\nabla}\in[\nabla]
having curvature
\tilde{R}
then
\bar{Y}\wedge\bar{v}\wedge(\tilde{R}(\bar{Y},\bar{v})\bar{v}-R(\bar{Y},\bar{v})\bar{v})=0
. Since also
\bar{Y}\wedge\bar{v}\wedge(\tilde{\nabla}_{\bar{v}}\bar{Y}-\nabla_{\bar{v}}\bar{Y})=0
, it follows from (
2.16
) that a variation of
C
through projective geodesics is represented by a section
[Y]
of
\nu(C)
such that for any parametrization of
C
and any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
, any vector field
Y(t)
representing
[Y]
satisfies

(2.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y\wedge\dot{\gamma}\wedge(\mathfrak{L}_{Y}[\nabla])(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})&=Y\wedge\dot{\gamma}\wedge(\nabla_{d/dt}\nabla_{d/dt}Y-\nabla_{\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}}Y+B(Y,\dot{\gamma})\dot{\gamma})\\
&=Y\wedge\dot{\gamma}\wedge(\nabla_{d/dt}\nabla_{d/dt}Y-\nabla_{\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}}Y+R(Y,\dot{\gamma})\dot{\gamma})=0.\end{split}

A section
[Y]
of the normal bundle of a projective geodesic solving (
2.17
) is a
projective Jacobi field
. This notion is needed in the proof of Lemma
5.2
.

3.
Radiant structures

This section defines the radiant structures that are the focus of this paper.
It is convenient to introduce them in a more general context for which it is helpful to describe some additional geometric notions.

A section of the normal bundle
\nu(C)
of a projective geodesic
C
can be viewed also as a rank two subbundle
K
of the restriction
TM_{|C}
to
C
of
TM
such that
K
contains
TC
. A pair
(C,K)
such that
C
is an immersed one-dimensional submanifold and
K
is a rank two subbundle of
TM_{|C}
containing
TC
is a
flag
with
base path

C
. A flag
(C,K)
is a
geodesic flag
if the base path
C
is a projective geodesic. A flag
(C,K)
is a
Jacobi flag
if it is a geodesic flag and
K/TC
is a projective Jacobi field.

Given a projective structure
[\nabla]
on
M
, a flag
(C,K)
is
parallel
if for any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
, any parametrization
\gamma:(a,b)\to C
, and any vector field
X
defined along
C
such that
\dot{\gamma}
and
X
span
K
, there holds

(3.1)

\displaystyle X\wedge\dot{\gamma}\wedge\nabla_{d/dt}X=0.

It is straightforward to check that the equations (
3.1
) do not depend on the choice of
\nabla
, the choice of parametrization of
C
, or the choice of
X
. A parallel flag
(C,K)
such that the base path
C
is a projective geodesic is a
parallel geodesic flag
. This amounts to adding to (
3.1
) the equation
\dot{\gamma}\wedge\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\gamma}=0
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. For a vector field
X\in\Gamma(TM)
and a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
the following two conditions are equivalent:

(1)

Y\wedge\nabla_{Y}X=0
for all
Y\in\Gamma(TM)
.

(2)

There is
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}
.

In this case
nf=\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)
where
\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)=\nabla_{p}X^{p}
.

Proof.

That (
2
) implies (
1
) is immediate. If
X
and
\nabla
satisfy (
1
), then
\delta_{(i}\,^{[k}\nabla_{j)}X^{l]}=0
. Tracing this in
j
and
l
shows
n\nabla_{i}X^{k}=\nabla_{p}X^{p}\delta_{i}\,^{k}
, so
X
satisfies (
2
) with
f=n^{-1}\nabla_{p}X^{p}
.
∎

A vector field
X
is
dilatative
with respect to a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
if it satisfies either of the equivalent conditions of Lemma
3.1
.

Remark 1
.

A vector field
X
dilatative with respect to the Levi-Civita connection
D
of a pseudo-Riemannian metric
g_{ij}
is
concircular

[
33
, Section
1.10
]
. In the equivalent form
D_{i}X_{j}=fg_{ij}
, where
X_{i}=X^{p}g_{pi}
, this condition was studied in
[
54
]
. A Riemannian manifold that admits a nowhere vanishing concircular vector field is locally a warped product
[
32
, Theorem
3.1
]
. Lemma
8.9
extends this statement to the context of radiant Hessian structures.

A vector field
X\in\Gamma(TM)
is
projectively dilatative
with respect to the torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
if
X\wedge Y\wedge\nabla_{Y}X=0
for all
Y\in\Gamma(TM)
.
If
\dim M=2
, then any vector field is projectively dilatative, so the notion is vacuous, but in higher dimensions it is not automatic.
The terminology
projectively dilatative
is justified by Lemma
3.2
, that can be proved by straightforward computation.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
X\in\Gamma(TM)
be projectively dilatative with respect to the torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
.

(1)

For
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)
and
Q_{ij}\in\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M)
,
X
is projectively dilatative with respect to the torsion-free affine connection
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+2\gamma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}+Q_{ij}X^{k}
.

(2)

For
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the vector field
\tilde{X}=gX
is projectively dilatative with respect to
\nabla
.

A
line field
on
M
is a one-dimensional subbundle of
TM
. A compact manifold admits a line field if and only if its Euler characteristic
\chi(M)
is zero, but an open manifold always admits a smooth line field.

By Lemma
3.2
, it makes sense to define
X\in\Gamma(TM)
to be
projectively dilatative
with respect to the projective structure
[\nabla]
if it is projectively dilatative with respect to any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
, and it make sense to say that a line field
L\subset TM
is projectively dilatative with respect to a torsion-free connection
\nabla
(or projective structure
[\nabla]
) if any section
X\in\Gamma(L)
is projectively dilatative with respect to
\nabla
(or any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
n=\dim M>2
and let
X\in\Gamma(TM)
be such that
M_{\ast}=M_{\ast}(X)=\{p\in M:X_{p}\neq 0\}
is nonempty. For a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
the following two conditions are equivalent:

(1)

X
is projectively dilatative with respect to
\nabla
.

(2)

There is
\sigma_{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M_{\ast})
such that
\nabla_{i}X^{j}-\tfrac{1}{n}\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)\delta_{i}\,^{j}=\sigma_{i}X^{j}-\tfrac{1}{n}\sigma_{p}X^{p}\delta_{i}\,^{j}
on
M_{\ast}
.

If there hold (
1
)-(
2
) then

(3)

X\wedge\nabla_{X}X=0
on
M
.

(4)

On
M_{\ast}
there hold

(3.2)

\displaystyle X^{p}B_{ijp}\,^{k}

\displaystyle=(d\sigma_{ij}+\tfrac{2}{n+1}R_{[ij]})X^{k}+\tfrac{1}{n-1}\left(\delta_{j}\,^{k}(d\sigma_{ip}+\tfrac{2}{n+1}R_{[ip]})-\delta_{i}\,^{k}(d\sigma_{jp}+\tfrac{2}{n+1}R_{[jp]})\right)X^{p},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1-n}{2}X^{p}C_{ijp}

\displaystyle=f(R_{[ij]}+\tfrac{n+1}{2}d\sigma_{ij})+\tfrac{1}{n+1}X^{p}\nabla_{p}(R_{[ij]}+\tfrac{2}{n+1}d\sigma_{ij}).

where
f=\tfrac{1}{n}(\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)-\sigma(X))
.

Moreover:

(5)

If
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)
,
Q_{ij}\in\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M)
, and
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+2\gamma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}+Q_{ij}X^{k}
, then
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=\sigma_{i}+\gamma_{i}+Q_{ip}X^{p}
in place of
\sigma_{i}
in (
2
) and
d\tilde{\sigma}_{ij}+\tfrac{2}{n+1}\tilde{R}_{[ij]}=d\sigma_{ij}+\tfrac{2}{n+1}R_{[ij]}+\tfrac{n}{n+1}dq_{ij}
, where
q_{i}=Q_{ip}X^{p}
and
\tilde{R}_{ij}
is the Ricci curvature of
\tilde{\nabla}
.

(6)

If
c\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then
\tilde{X}=cX
is projectively dilatative with respect to
\nabla
, and on
M_{\ast}(cX)\subset M_{\ast}(X)
,
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=\sigma_{i}+d\log{c}_{i}
in place of
\sigma_{i}
in (
2
).

Proof.

Condition (
1
) can be rewritten as the tensorial identity

(3.4)

\displaystyle X^{[a}\delta_{(i}\,^{b}\nabla_{j)}X^{c]}=0.

It is immediate that (
2
) implies (
3.4
) on
M_{\ast}
. Because
\nabla_{i}X^{j}
is smooth on
M
, that (
3.4
) holds on
M_{\ast}
implies it holds on
M
as well. Thus (
2
) implies (
1
). Suppose
X
and
\nabla
satisfy (
1
). Tracing (
3.4
) in
j
and
c
gives

(3.5)

\displaystyle 0=(1-n)X^{[a}\nabla_{i}X^{b]}+X^{[a}\delta_{i}\,^{b]}\nabla_{p}X^{p}-X^{p}\nabla_{p}X^{[a}\delta_{i}\,^{b]}.

Contracting (
3.5
) with an arbitrary vector field
Y^{i}
gives

(3.6)

\displaystyle 0=((n-1)\nabla_{Y}X-\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)Y)\wedge X+Y\wedge\nabla_{X}X.

Taking
Y=X
in (
3.6
) shows
(n-2)X\wedge\nabla_{X}X=0
, so
X\wedge\nabla_{X}X=0
, since
n>2
. This shows (
1
) implies (
3
). It also implies that there is
g\in C^{\infty}(M_{\ast})
such that
\nabla_{X}X=gX
on
M_{\ast}
. Substituting this into (
3.6
) shows that on
M_{\ast}
there holds
0=((n-1)\nabla_{Y}X+(g-\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X))Y)\wedge X
for all
Y\in\Gamma(TM)
. Consequently, for each
Y\in\Gamma(TM)
there is
b(Y)\in C^{\infty}(M_{\ast})
such that
\nabla_{Y}X+\tfrac{1}{n-1}(g-\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X))Y)=b(Y)X
on
M_{\ast}
. From this last expression it is evident that the assignment
Y\to b(Y)
is a
C^{\infty}(M_{\ast})
-module map, so that there is
\sigma_{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M_{\ast})
such that
b(Y)=\sigma_{p}Y^{p}
on
M_{\ast}
. This shows
\nabla_{i}X^{j}-\tfrac{1}{n}\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)\delta_{i}\,^{j}=\sigma_{i}X^{j}-\tfrac{1}{n}\sigma_{p}X^{p}\delta_{i}\,^{j}
on
M_{\ast}
, so shows (
2
).

Suppose
X
is projectively dilatative with respect to
\nabla
so that
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=\sigma_{i}X^{j}+f\delta_{i}\,^{j}
where
f=\tfrac{1}{n}(\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)-\sigma(X))
. Antisymmetrizing
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}X^{k}=df_{i}\delta_{j}\,^{k}+X^{k}\nabla_{i}\sigma_{j}+\sigma_{j}\nabla_{i}X^{k}=df_{i}\delta_{j}\,^{k}+f\sigma_{j}\delta_{i}\,^{k}+\left(\nabla_{i}\sigma_{j}+\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\right)X^{k}
gives

(3.7)

\displaystyle X^{p}R_{ijp}\,^{k}

\displaystyle=2\nabla_{[i}\nabla_{j]}X^{k}=2df_{[i}\delta_{j]}\,^{k}-2f\sigma_{[i}\delta_{j]}\,^{k}+d\sigma_{ij}X^{k}.

Tracing (
3.7
) gives

(3.8)

\displaystyle R_{ip}X^{p}

\displaystyle=(1-n)(df_{i}-f\sigma_{i})+d\sigma_{pi}X^{p}.

Combining (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) gives (
3.2
).
Differentiating (
3.8
) and using
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}+\sigma_{i}X^{j}
to simplify the result gives

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(R_{ij}+d\sigma_{ij}&+(1-n)\nabla_{j}\sigma_{j})=-X^{p}(\nabla_{j}R_{ip}+\nabla_{j}d\sigma_{ip})+(n-1)\left(2\sigma_{(i}df_{j)}-f\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}-\nabla_{j}df_{i}\right).\end{split}

Antisymmetrizing (
3.9
) and using the traced differential Bianchi identity to simplify the result yields

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(R_{[ij]}&+\tfrac{n+1}{2}d\sigma_{ij})=X^{p}(\nabla_{[i}R_{j]p}+\nabla_{[i}d\sigma_{j]p})=\tfrac{1}{2}X^{p}(\nabla_{q}R_{ijp}\,^{q}-\nabla_{p}d\sigma_{ij}).\end{split}

Since
C_{ijk}=\tfrac{2}{1-n}\nabla_{[i}R_{j]k}-\tfrac{2}{n^{2}-1}\nabla_{k}R_{[ij]}
, the identity (
3.10
) can be rewritten as (
3.3
).

The claims (
5
) and (
6
) about how
\sigma_{i}
transforms when
\nabla
and
X
are replaced by
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\tilde{X}
follow from straightforward computations, with the exception of the final claim of (
5
), which is a bit more involved. Write
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=2\gamma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}+Q_{ij}X^{k}
, so that
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}
. Then
\Pi_{ip}\,^{p}=(n+1)\gamma_{i}+q_{i}
, where
q_{i}=Q_{ip}X^{p}
. From
2\tilde{R}_{[ij]}-2R_{[ij]}=-2\nabla_{[i}\Pi_{j]p}\,^{p}=-(n+1)d\gamma_{ij}-dq_{ij}
and
d\tilde{\sigma}_{ij}=d\sigma_{ij}+d\gamma_{ij}+dq_{ij}
there results (
5
).
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

On a manifold,
M
, of dimension
n>2
, if
X\in\Gamma(TM)
is projectively dilatative with respect to the projective structure
[\nabla]
, there is a unique
\nabla\in[\nabla]
such that
X
is dilatative with respect to
\nabla
on
M_{\ast}=\{p\in M:X_{p}\neq 0\}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.3
, for any
\nabla\in[\nabla]
there are
f\in C^{\infty}(M_{\ast})
and
\sigma_{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M_{\ast})
such that
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}+\sigma_{i}X^{j}
on
M_{\ast}
. The unique
\tilde{\nabla}\in[\nabla]
such that
X
is dilatative with respect to
\tilde{\nabla}
on
M_{\ast}
is
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla-2\sigma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
.
∎

Example 3
.

This example shows that the
\sigma_{i}
in (
2
) need not extend smoothly to all of
M
. Let
D
be the flat connection determined by the affine structure on the
n
-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{V}
and let
h_{ij}
be a Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{V}
, meaning that it is
D
-parallel and positive definite. The Euler vector field
\mathbb{X}^{i}
generating the flow by dilations around the origin satisfies
D_{i}\mathbb{X}^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
. Let
X^{i}=|\mathbb{X}|^{2\alpha}_{h}\mathbb{X}^{i}
.
For
\alpha>-1/2
, the vector field
X
has a unique zero at the origin, so that
\mathbb{V}_{\ast}=\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
. When
\alpha\leq-1/2
,
X
is defined and nonvanishing on
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
. On
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
,
D_{i}X^{j}=|\mathbb{X}|^{2\alpha}_{h}\delta_{i}\,^{j}+2\alpha|\mathbb{X}|^{-2}_{h}\mathbb{X}^{p}h_{pi}X^{j}
, so
D_{p}X^{p}=(n+2\alpha)|\mathbb{X}|^{2\alpha}_{h}
and
\sigma_{i}=2\alpha|\mathbb{X}|^{-2}_{h}\mathbb{X}^{p}h_{pi}
.
In the case
\alpha>-1/2
,
\sigma_{i}
does not extend smoothly to the origin, but in both cases
\sigma_{p}X^{p}=2\alpha|\mathbb{X}|^{2\alpha}_{h}
is smooth on the entire domain of definition of
X
. There holds
X\wedge Y\wedge D_{Y}X=0
for all vector fields
Y
on the domain of definition of
X
. Note also that
d\sigma_{ij}=0
where
\sigma_{i}
is defined. When
\alpha=-n/2
,
X
is an example of a divergence free projectively dilatative vector field.

By (
3
) of Lemma
3.3
a vector field
X
projectively dilatative with respect to
[\nabla]
is projectively geodesic in the sense that
X\wedge\nabla_{X}X=0
for all
\nabla\in[\nabla]
, so every integral manifold of the line field
L
spanned by
X
is a projective geodesic of
[\nabla]
. Consequently, by Lemma
3.3
, that a vector field
X
be projectively dilatative with respect to a projective structure
[\nabla]
means that every integral manifold of the line field
L
spanned by
X
is a projective geodesic of
[\nabla]
, and that every flag
(C,K)
, the base curve of which is an integral manifold of
L
, is a parallel geodesic flag for
[\nabla]
.

If a line field
L
is dilatative with respect to connections
\nabla
and
\bar{\nabla}
then it makes sense to declare
\nabla
and
\bar{\nabla}
equivalent if every parallel geodesic flag for
\nabla
with base curve an integral manifold of
L
is a parallel geodesic flag for
\bar{\nabla}
, and similarly with the roles of
\bar{\nabla}
and
\nabla
interchanged. Define a
projective dilatation structure
to be a pair
(\{\nabla\},L)
comprising a line field
L\subset TM
and an equivalence class
\{\nabla\}
of torsion-free connections such that
L
is projectively dilatative with respect to each
\nabla\in\{\nabla\}
and any two representatives of
\{\nabla\}
are equivalent in the preceding sense.

If
\nabla\in\{\nabla\}
then
\{\nabla\}
contains the projective structure
[\nabla]
generated by
\nabla
, so it makes sense to say that a projective structure
[\nabla]
is
subordinate
to a projective dilatation structure
(\{\nabla\},L)
if
[\nabla]\subset\{\nabla\}
. It makes sense to ask for conditions distinguishing a projective structure subordinate to a projective dilatation structure. Theorem
5.6
can be viewed as accomplishing this in a particular setting.

If
(\{\nabla\},L)
is a projective dilatation structure, then the integral manifolds of
L
are projective geodesics for any
\nabla\in\{\nabla\}
. As the parallel flags the base paths of which are the integral manifolds of
L
do not depend on the choice of
\nabla\in\{\nabla\}
, it makes sense to call these parallel flags the
radial flags
of the projective dilatation structure
(\{\nabla\},L)
.

A
radiant structure
on a manifold
M
is a pair
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
comprising a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
and a vector field
\mathbb{E}\in\Gamma(TM)
such that
\nabla_{i}\mathbb{E}^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
. The vector field
\mathbb{E}
is said to be
radiant
with respect to
\nabla
. The curvature of a radiant structure means the curvature of
\nabla
