, and a radiant structure is flat if
\nabla
is flat. A flat affine manifold admits a radiant vector field if and only if its affine holonomy has a fixed point. For back ground on flat radiant manifolds see
[
39
,
63
,
65
]
. The simplest examples of radiant flat affine manifolds are cones in a vector space and their cocompact quotients by subgroups of dilations, such as affine Hopf manifolds.

Example 4
.

Let
\mathbb{V}
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional vector space and let
\mathbb{E}
be the radial vector field generating dilations by
e^{t}
and let
\widehat{\nabla}
be the flat affine connection determined by the vector space structure.
For
\lambda>1
let
\langle\lambda\rangle
denote the cyclic subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
generated by the powers of
\lambda
. As
\mathbb{E}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
are invariant under
\langle\lambda\rangle
, they descend to the quotient
\mathscr{H}^{n}(\lambda)
of
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
by
\langle\lambda\rangle
, which is therefore a radiant flat affine manifold. The manifold
\mathscr{H}^{n}(\lambda)
is called an
affine Hopf manifold
. It is diffeomorphic to
S^{n-1}\times S^{1}
.

More generally, the following construction due to Benzecri
[
12
, Section
2
]
shows that if an
n
-manifold
M
carries a flat projective structure, then
M\times S^{1}
carries an affine structure. The presentation here follows Y. Benoist
[
11
, Section
1.3
]
. Suppose
n>1
so that
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
is simply-connected. The idea comes from viewing the affine structure on
\mathscr{H}(\lambda)
as a lifting of the projective structure on the space of rays in
\mathbb{V}
,
\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
via the projection
\bar{\rho}:\mathscr{H}(\lambda)\to\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
induced by the canonical projection
\rho:\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
. The group of projective transformations of
\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
is isomorphic to the group
SL^{\pm}(\mathbb{V})
defined as the subgroup of
GL(\mathbb{V})
acting unimodularly on
\mathop{\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n+1}}\mathbb{V}
. Let
\pi:\tilde{M}\to M
be a universal cover of
M
, let
\mathsf{dev}:\tilde{M}\to\mathbb{P}^{+}(\mathbb{V})
and
\mathsf{hol}:\pi(M)\to SL^{\pm}(\mathbb{V})
be the corresponding developing and holonomy maps of the projective structure. Let
\hat{M}=\{(x,v)\in\tilde{M}\times\mathscr{H}^{n}(\lambda):\mathsf{dev}(x)=\bar{\rho}(v)\}
. Define an action of
\gamma\in\pi(M)
on
\hat{M}
by
\gamma(x,v)=(\gamma x,\mathsf{hol}(\gamma)v)
and let
\bar{M}
be the quotient of
\hat{M}
by this action of
\pi_{1}(M)
. A universal cover of
\bar{M}
is given by
N=\{(x,v)\in\tilde{M}\times\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}:\mathsf{dev}(x)=\sigma(v)\}
and the developing map
\bar{\mathsf{dev}}:N\to\mathbb{V}
of the resulting affine structure on
\bar{M}
is given by
\bar{\mathsf{dev}}(x,v)=\sigma(v)
. Since
\bar{\mathsf{dev}}(N)\subset\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
, this affine structure is radiant.

In this example,
\bar{M}
is diffeomorphic to
M\times S^{1}
. Example
21
generalizes this example by allowing the projective structure to be nonflat (in this case the resulting radiant structure is also nonflat).

Example 5
.

If
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of a pseudo-Riemannian metric and
X
is radiant with respect to
\nabla
, then
X
is said to be
concurrent
. See
[
33
, Section
1.10
]
.

Example 6
.

A dilatative vector field
X
satisfies that
\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)
is a nonzero constant if and only if
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}X^{k}=0
. In this case, a constant multiple of
X
is radiant.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. If
X\in\Gamma(TM)
is dilatative with respect to
\nabla
and
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}
with
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
nowhere vanishing, then
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+\tfrac{1}{(1-n)}f^{-1}R_{ij}X^{k}
and
\tilde{X}^{i}=f^{-1}X^{i}
satisfy
\tilde{\nabla}_{i}\tilde{X}^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
, so constitute a radiant structure.

Proof.

By (
3.8
),
R_{ip}X^{p}=(1-n)df_{i}
, so
\tilde{\nabla}_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}
, so that

(3.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{\nabla}_{i}\tilde{X}^{j}

\displaystyle=-f^{-2}df_{i}X^{j}+f^{-1}(\nabla_{i}X^{j}+\tfrac{1}{1-n}f^{-1}R_{ip}X^{p}X^{j})=f^{-1}\nabla_{i}X^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}.\qed

Corollary 3.6
.

On a manifold,
M
, of dimension
n>2
, suppose
X\in\Gamma(TM)
is projectively dilatative with respect to torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
and
\nabla_{i}X^{j}=f\delta_{i}\,^{j}+\sigma_{i}X^{j}
for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\sigma_{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M_{\ast})
where
M_{\ast}=\{p\in M:X_{p}\neq 0\}
. On the subset
M_{\ast\ast}
of
M_{\ast}
where
f-\sigma_{p}X^{p}\neq 0
, the connection
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla-2\sigma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}+\tfrac{1}{1-n}(f-\sigma_{p}X^{p})^{-1}(R_{ij}+(n-1)(\nabla_{i}\sigma_{j}+\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})+d\sigma_{ij})X^{k}
and
\tilde{X}=(f-\sigma_{p}X^{p})^{-1}X^{i}
satisfy
\tilde{\nabla}_{i}\tilde{X}^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
so constitute a radiant structure generating on
M_{\ast\ast}
the same projective dilatation structure as that generated by
(\nabla,X)
.

Proof.

Combine successively Corollary
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
making use of (
2.4
).
∎

Corollary
3.6
shows that if
(\{\nabla\},L)
is a projective dilatation structure on
M
that admits a globally defined projectively dilatative representative pair
(\nabla,X)
, then there is an open subset of
M
on which
(\{\nabla\},L)
is represented by a radiant structure
(\nabla,X)
. In general there is no reason to think a projective dilatation structure will admit a globally defined representative
(\nabla,X)
, and less still to expect that when it does that
f-\sigma_{p}X^{p}
will be everywhere nonzero, but nonetheless the corollary provides motivation for studying radiant structures as local avatars of projective dilatation structures.

Although the remainder of the paper focuses on radiant structures, projective dilatation structures are a priori more general objects whose existence is in principle less limited by topological restrictions than is the existence of radiant structures (as will be shown next). In some imprecise sense radiant structures are to projective dilatation structures as affine structures are to projective structures or tori are to hyperbolic surfaces.

Let
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be a radiant structure on
M
. If
M
is compact then
\mathbb{E}
is complete, but
\nabla
need not be complete. In the case
\nabla
is flat, if
\nabla
is complete then its affine holonomy cannot have a fixed point unless it is trivial, and so
\nabla
cannot admit a radiant vector field unless it is the standard flat connection on a vector space. Radiant flat affine structures on compact manifolds are the simplest examples of incomplete flat affine connections on compact manifolds.

Lemma
3.7
relates the images of geodesics of
\nabla
with the integral curves of
\mathbb{E}
. In considering Lemma
3.7
it is helpful to consider the following example.
If
\sigma(t)
is an integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
, then the reparametrization
\gamma(t)=\sigma(\log|1-t|)
is a geodesic of
\nabla
passing through
\sigma(0)
. Whether
\gamma(t)
blows up or not when
t
approaches
1
depends on the behavior of
\sigma(s)
as
s\to-\infty
. If
\sigma(s)
approaches a zero of
\mathbb{E}
as
s\to-\infty
then in general the geodesic
\gamma(t)
continues through the apparent singularity. For example this happens on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Consider the radial vector field
\mathbb{X}=x^{i}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
generated by dilations by
e^{s}
. If
0\neq v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
then
\mathbb{X}_{v}\neq 0
. The maximal integral curve of
\mathbb{X}
passing through
v
is
\sigma(s)=e^{s}v
, defined for
s\in\mathbb{R}
. The curve
\gamma(t)=\sigma(\log|1-t|)
is the geodesic of the standard flat affine connection such that
\gamma(0)=v
and
\tfrac{d}{dt}\gamma(0)=-\mathbb{X}_{v}
. It is defined for all
t\in(-\infty,1)
, but in fact, as
\sigma(\log|1-t|)=(1-t)v
for
t\in(-\infty,1)
, it is not a maximal geodesic with these initial conditions, such being given by
\tau(t)=(1-t)v
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The image of this maximal geodesic is the union of three images of integral curves of
\mathbb{X}
, namely the origin and the two rays leaving the origin in the
v
and
-v
directions. Lemma
3.7
shows that a similar relation between the images of the integral curves of
\mathbb{E}
and the images of geodesics of
\nabla
holds for any radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
.

Lemma 3.7
.

For a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
there hold

(1)

The image of the maximal integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
passing through a nonsingular point
p
of
\mathbb{E}
is contained in the image of the maximal geodesic of
\nabla
passing through
p
and having at
p
tangent
\mathbb{E}_{p}
.

(2)

The image of a maximal geodesic of
\nabla
passing through a singular point of
\mathbb{E}
contains the union of the images of the maximal integral curves of
\mathbb{E}
the closures of which contain the singular point.

(3)

If
\mathbb{E}
is tangent to a maximal totally geodesic submanifold
\Sigma\subset M
at a point
p\in M\cap\Sigma
then
\Sigma
contains every image of an integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
the closure of which contains
p
. Moreover, if
p\in\Sigma
is a singular point of
\mathbb{E}
then
\mathbb{E}
is tangent to
\Sigma
along some open neighborhood of
p
in
\Sigma
.

Proof.

First it is shown that if
\mathbb{E}_{p}\neq 0
then the image of the maximal integral curve
\sigma:J\to M
of
\mathbb{E}
passing through
p
is contained in the image of the maximal geodesic through
p
tangent to
\mathbb{E}_{p}
at
p
. Here
J
is an open interval in
\mathbb{R}
containing
0
,
\sigma(0)=p
, and
\tfrac{d}{ds}\sigma(s)=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma(s)}
for
s\in J
. It is convenient to write
J=(a,b)
, allowing
a=-\infty
and
b=\infty
. From
\mathbb{E}\wedge\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{E}=0
it follows that the curve
\gamma(t)=\sigma(\log|1-t|)
is the unique
\nabla
geodesic such that
\gamma(0)=p
and
\tfrac{d}{dt}\gamma(0)=-\mathbb{E}_{p}
. Since
a<0
,
(1-e^{b},1-e^{a})\subset(-\infty,1)
, where
e^{a}
has to be interpreted as
0
in the case
a=-\infty
, and
1-e^{b}
has to be interpreted as
-\infty
in the case
b=\infty
. It follows that the image of
\gamma
contains the image of
\sigma
. The point is that
\gamma
is defined on
(1-e^{a},1-e^{b})
, but could extend to a larger interval.

Let
x^{i}
be normal coordinates in a neighborhood of a singular point
p\in M
such that the origin corresponds to
p
and write
\mathbb{E}=A^{i}(x)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
and
X_{i}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
. By assumption
X_{i}=\nabla_{X_{i}}\mathbb{E}=\left(dA^{j}(X_{i})+A^{p}\Gamma_{ip}\,^{j}\right)X_{j}
in which
\Gamma_{ij}\,^{k}
are the Christoffel symbols of
\nabla
with respect to the frame
X_{i}
. Because
\mathbb{E}_{p}=0
is assumed, there holds at the origin
\tfrac{\partial A^{j}}{\partial x^{i}}(0)=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
and so
A^{i}=x^{i}+c^{i}
for some constants
c^{i}
. Moreover, since
A^{i}(0)=0
, these constants are
0
, and so
A^{i}=x^{i}
and
\mathbb{E}
has the form
x^{i}X_{i}
of the usual Euler vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the integral curves of which are the rays leaving the origin and the origin itself. Because the coordinates are normal the image of the geodesic of
\nabla
passing through the origin with velocity
v
at the origin is the line passing through the origin in the direction
v
, which is contained in a union of images of integral curves of
\mathbb{E}
, namely the origin and the rays with directions
\pm v
. This shows that there is an open neighborhood
U
of
p
such that the intersection with
U
of the image of a maximal geodesic passing through
p
is contained in the intersection with
U
of a union of images of integral curves of
\mathbb{E}
. By the preceding paragraph and the fact that the singular points of
\mathbb{E}
are isolated, these images are themselves contained in the maximal geodesic. If the singular point
p
is contained in the closure of the image
L
of a maximal integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
then in the normal coordinates considered above, either
L
is
p
itself, or the intersection
L\cap U
corresponds in coordinates to part of a ray limiting to the origin. In either case, this shows
L
is contained in a maximal geodesic passing through
p
.

Suppose
\Sigma\subset M
is a maximal totally geodesic submanifold. If
p\in\Sigma
is not a singular point of
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}_{p}\in T_{p}\Sigma
then the image of the maximal integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
passing through
p
is contained in the image of a maximal geodesic of
\nabla
, so is contained in
\Sigma
. If
p
is a singular point then
\mathbb{E}_{p}=0
and so trivially
\mathbb{E}_{p}\in T_{p}\Sigma
. In this case, by the preceding paragraph, the image of any geodesic passing through
p
contains a union of images of integral curves of
\mathbb{E}
the closure of each of which contains
p
. Since the image of the geodesic is contained in
\Sigma
, it contains these images too. In particular there is an open neighborhood
U
of
p
in
\Sigma
such that for
q\in U
different from
p
the intersection with
U
of the image of the maximal integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
passing through
q
contains
p
in its closure, and so is contained in
\Sigma
; hence
\mathbb{E}_{q}\in T_{q}\Sigma
.
∎

Remark 2
.

A consequence of
[
63
, Theorem
3.3
]
is that the radiant vector field of a flat radiant structure on a compact manifold is nonsingular. More readable proofs are given in
[
66
, Corollary
6.5.3
]
and
[
42
, Theorem
3
]
. The idea of the proofs is to show that the flow of
\mathbb{E}
retracts the manifold onto the singular set of
\mathbb{E}
. All the proofs make essential use of the fact that the developing map is defined globally on the universal cover and it is not clear to the author how to modify them when flatness is not assumed.

If a compact orientable manifold,
M
, admits a nonsingular radiant structure, then
\chi(M)=0
. It is proved in
[
63
, p.
502
]
that a radiant vector field on a compact flat affine manifold
M
is nonsingular, and so
\chi(M)=0
for such a manifold. In general it is not clear whether a radiant vector field on a compact manifold
M
can have zeroes. Lemma
3.8
shows that if it does, then
M
must be even-dimensional.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is a radiant structure, a zero of
\mathbb{E}
is nondegenerate, so isolated, and its index is
+1
.

(1)

If a compact manifold
M
admits a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, then
\chi(M)\geq 0
, with equality if and only if
\mathbb{E}
is nonsingular.

(2)

If a compact and odd-dimensional manifold admits a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, then
\mathbb{E}
is nonsingular.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathsf{Zero}(\mathbb{E})
. Let
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}\in\Gamma(TU)
be a local frame over an open neighborhood
U\subset M
of
p
, let
\nabla_{E_{i}}E_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Gamma_{ij}\,^{k}E_{k}
, and write
\mathbb{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f^{i}E_{i}
. Then
E_{i}=\nabla_{E_{i}}\mathbb{E}=\left(df^{j}(E_{i})+f^{q}\Gamma_{iq}\,^{j}\right)E_{j}
, so on
U\cap\mathsf{Zero}(\mathbb{E})
there holds
df^{j}(E_{i})=\delta_{j}\,^{i}
. Hence
p
is nondegenerate, and so is isolated. Since
\det\nabla\mathbb{E}
is positive at
p
, the index of
\mathbb{E}
at
p
is
1

[
114
, Lemma
6.4
]
. If
M
is compact and orientable, then by the Poincarè-Hopf Index Theorem,
\chi(M)
equals the sum of the indices of any vector field with isolated zeroes, in particular the sum of the indices of
\mathbb{E}
, which is positive unless
\mathbb{E}
is nonsingular, in which case it is
0
. If
M
is compact and nonorientable, then the radiant structure lifts to its connected oriented double cover and the same conclusion follows. If
M
is compact and odd-dimensional then
\chi(M)=0
, and so
\mathbb{E}
is nonsingular.
∎

Remark 3
.

Lemma
3.8
implies that neither a compact surface of genus at least two, nor the product of any such surface with an even-dimensional sphere, admits a radiant structure.
In higher dimensions it is not clear how limiting is the conclusion of Lemma
3.8
. It rules out radiant structures on sphere bundles over higher genus surfaces. On the other hand, the affirmative resolution of question attributed to Thurston
[
83
, Problem
4.10
]
asserts that an aspherical compact four manifold has nonnegative Euler characteristic. The simplest example of a simply-connected, compact manifold which by Lemma
3.8
admits no radiant structure seems to be the connected sum of
S^{3}\times S^{3}
with itself.

Remark 4
.

In this paper there is not given any example of a manifold with positive Euler characteristic admitting a radiant structure. It would be interesting to know if such examples exist. The natural place to look is compact homogeneous spaces, which have nonnegative Euler characteristic
[
115
]
.
By a conjecture attributed to Chern, a compact manifold admitting a flat affine structure has Euler characteristic zero, and this would imply no compact manifold with positive Euler characteristic can admit a flat radiant structure. Can such a manifold admit a radiant structure? In addressing such questions it may be interesting to ask them for radiant structures with symmetric Ricci tensor. For example, Lemma
3.12
shows that compact manifold with vanishing first Betti number admits no Ricci symmetric radiant structure, while Theorem
12.9
shows that the three sphere does admit a radiant structure (with purely antisymmetric Ricci tensor).

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be a radiant structure on an
n
-manifold. For
S_{i_{1}\dots i_{r}}^{j_{1}\dots j_{s}}\in\Gamma(\otimes^{r}T^{\ast}M\otimes\otimes^{s}TM\otimes|\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M|^{\lambda})
there holds
\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}S=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}S+(s-r-n\lambda)S
.

Proof.

For a torsion-free connection
\nabla
and
X\in\Gamma(TM)
there holds

(3.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathfrak{L}_{X}S)_{i_{1}\dots i_{r}}^{j_{1}\dots j_{s}}&-X^{p}\nabla_{p}S_{i_{1}\dots i_{r}}^{j_{1}\dots j_{s}}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}S_{i_{1}\dots p\dots i_{r}}^{j_{1}\dots\,\,\dots j_{s}}\nabla_{i_{a}}X^{p}-\sum_{a=1}^{s}S_{i_{1}\dots\,\,\dots i_{r}}^{j_{1}\dots p\dots j_{s}}\nabla_{p}X^{j_{a}}+\lambda(\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X))S_{i_{1}\dots i_{r}}^{j_{1}\dots j_{s}},\end{split}

in which
\operatorname{div}_{\nabla}(X)=\nabla_{p}X^{p}
.
Taking
X=\mathbb{E}
in (
3.12
) gives the claim.
∎

Lemma
3.10
generalizes
[
63
, Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
]
to the nonflat setting.

Lemma 3.10
.

If
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is a radiant structure on a compact manifold then there is no
\nabla
-parallel volume form and a
\nabla
-parallel one-form is identically zero.

Proof.

If
\Psi
is an
n
-form on a radiant
n
-manifold then
d(i(\mathbb{E})\Psi)=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi+n\Psi
by Lemma
3.9
. If
\Psi
is a parallel volume form, then
\Psi
is exact and
M
is orientable, and so by Stokes’s theorem
M
must be noncompact.
A one-form parallel with respect to a torsion-free connection is closed, so if
\beta
is a parallel one-form then, by Lemma
3.9
there holds
\beta=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\beta-\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}\beta=d(\beta(\mathbb{E}))=dg
, where
g=\beta(\mathbb{E})
. Since
dg
is parallel, if it vanishes at one point it vanishes identically. If
M
is compact then
g
must have a minimum, at which
dg
vanishes, and so
\beta=dg
is identically zero.
∎

Example 7
.

Because the affine Hopf manifold
\mathscr{H}^{n}(\lambda)
of example
4
is compact it admits no parallel (nor any exact) volume form.

Lemma 3.11
.

On a manifold with vanishing first Betti number, a torsion-free affine connection with symmetric Ricci tensor admits a parallel volume density.

Proof.

If a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
has symmetric Ricci tensor, then in a neighborhood of every point it admit a parallel volume density. Let
\mu
be a global nonvanishing volume density and define a one-form
\gamma
by
\nabla\mu=\gamma\otimes\mu
. Then
d\gamma_{ij}\mu=2\nabla_{[i}\nabla_{j]}\mu=-2R_{ijp}\,^{p}\mu=2R_{[ij]}\mu=0
, so
\gamma
is closed and hence locally exact. If
f
is local primitive of
\gamma
then
\nabla(e^{-f}\mu)=0
. Let
\{U_{\alpha}\}
be an open cover such that on
U_{\alpha}
there is a
\nabla
-parallel nonvanishing volume density
\mu_{\alpha}
. There is
f_{\alpha\beta}\in C^{\infty}(U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta})
such that
\mu_{\alpha}=e^{f_{\alpha\beta}}\mu_{\beta}
on
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}
. Because
0=\nabla\mu_{\alpha}=df_{\alpha\beta}\otimes\mu_{\beta}
, refining the open cover if necessary,
f_{\alpha\beta}
is constant on
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}
. Because the first Betti number is zero, the Cech cocycle
\{f_{\alpha\beta}\}
is exact, so there is a
0
-chain
\{g_{\alpha}\}
with values in the constant sheaf
\mathbb{R}
(again, replacing the given cover with a refinement if necessary) such that
f_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\alpha}-g_{\beta}
on
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}
, and
e^{-g_{\alpha}}\mu_{\alpha}
patch together to give a global parallel volume density.
∎

For flat radiant structures Lemma
3.12
recovers a special case of
[
65
, Proposition
2.7
]
.

Lemma 3.12
.

A compact manifold with vanishing first Betti number admits no radiant structure with symmetric Ricci tensor.

Proof.

If there were a radiant structure with symmetric Ricci tensor, by Lemma
3.11
it would admit a parallel volume form, contrary to Lemma
3.10
.
∎

In constrast with Lemma
3.12
, Theorem
12.9
shows that the three sphere admits a radiant structure with skew-symmetric Ricci tensor.

Some issues arise when the curvature of a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is not identically zero that are absent in the case that
\nabla
is flat. The condition
\nabla_{i}\mathbb{E}^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
implies
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}
, so that a radiant vector field need not be an infinitesimal affine automorphism. Geometrically this corresponds to the nonexistence, even locally, of totally geodesic surfaces to which
\mathbb{E}
is tangent.
On the other hand, it is an important feature of a radiant vector field on a flat affine manifold that it is necessarily an infinitesimal affine automorphism.

For a torsion-free affine connection,
\nabla
, and a vector field,
X
, differentiating
(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}X^{k}+X^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}
, tracing the result, and using the Ricci identity, the differential Bianchi identity, and (
3.12
) yields

(3.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{p}&=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{p}X^{p}+2\nabla_{(i}X^{p}R_{j)p}+X^{p}\nabla_{p}R_{ij}+2X^{p}\nabla_{i}R_{[jp]}\\
&=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{p}X^{p}+(\mathfrak{L}_{X}\mathsf{Ric})_{ij}+2R_{[jp]}\nabla_{i}X^{p}+2X^{p}\nabla_{i}R_{[jp]},\end{split}

which is needed in the proof of Lemma
3.13
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be a radiant structure and define a one-form
\rho_{i}
by
\rho_{i}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pi}
.
There hold

(3.14)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{(ij)}=\nabla_{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{p}+\nabla_{(i}\rho_{j)}=\mathbb{E}^{q}\nabla_{p}R_{qij}\,^{p}+R_{ij}+\nabla_{(i}\rho_{j)},

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{[ij]}=\nabla_{[i}\rho_{j]}=\tfrac{1}{2}d\rho_{ij},

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{pi}=\rho_{i}+\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{p}\rho_{i}=(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\rho)_{i},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{ip}=0.

In particular, if
R_{ij}
is
\lambda
-positively homogeneous, then
\rho_{i}
is
\lambda
-positively homogeneous.

Proof.

Antisymmetrizing, tracing, and differentiating the identity
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathbb{E}^{k}=0
yields

(3.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &R_{ijp}\,^{k}\mathbb{E}^{p}=0,&&R_{ip}\mathbb{E}^{p}=0,&\\
&R_{ji}=-\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{i}R_{jp},&&R_{ijk}\,^{l}=-\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{k}R_{ijp}\,^{l},&&2R_{[ij]}=2\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{[i}R_{j]p}=\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{q}R_{ijp}\,^{q}.\end{aligned}

In particular,
\rho_{i}
vanishes if
\nabla
has symmetric Ricci tensor.
That
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\mathbb{E}^{k}=0
implies immediately the first of

(3.17)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k},

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ip}\,^{p}=-\rho_{j},

while the second follows from the first and (
3.16
). From (
3.16
) it follows that
\nabla_{i}\rho_{j}=2R_{[ij]}+2\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{i}R_{[pj]}
.
In (
3.13
) this yields
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{ij}=\nabla_{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{p}+\nabla_{i}\rho_{j}
, and decomposing this into its symmetric and antisymmetric parts and using again (
3.16
) yields (
3.14
). Contracting
\mathbb{E}^{i}
with (
3.14
) and simplifying yields (
3.15
).
∎

Remark 5
.

Example
19
exhibits a radiant structure for which
\rho
is not identically zero. Example
34
exhibits a radiant structure for which
\rho
is nowhere vanishing and not closed. In neither of these examples is the underlying manifold compact, and it would be interesting to know if there is a radiant structure on a compact manifold having nonzero
\rho
.

Remark 6
.

The identity
R_{ijp}\,^{k}\mathbb{E}^{p}=0
means that the affine connection
\nabla
of a radiant structure has
Weyl nullity
in the sense of
[
69
]
. See in particular
[
69
, Proposition
2.5
]
.

Lemma 3.14
.

A radiant structure on a surface has symmetric Ricci tensor.

Proof.

By (
2.1
), in dimension
2
,
R_{ijk}\,^{l}=-2R_{k[i}\delta_{j]}\,^{l}
, so by (
3.17
),
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}=R_{ji}\mathbb{E}^{k}
. Hence
0=(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{[ij]}\,^{k}=-R_{[ij]}\mathbb{E}^{k}
. Because the zeros of
\mathbb{E}
are nondegenerate, so isolated, this implies
R_{[ij]}=0
.
∎

By
[
12
]
a compact surface admits a flat affine structure if and only if it is a torus or a Klein bottle. These are classified in
[
4
,
5
,
116
]
and some of them are radiant. See also
[
9
, Section
4
]
and
[
11
]
. For radiant structures, Theorem
3.15
extends this result to non-flat structures.

Theorem 3.15
.

A compact surface admits a radiant structure if and only if it is a torus or a Klein bottle.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.8
, a compact surface that admits a radiant structure has nonnegative Euler characteristic, while by Lemmas
3.12
and
3.14
the two-sphere admits no radiant structure. Were the projective plane to admit a radiant structure, it could be lifted to the two-sphere. A two-dimensional affine Hopf manifold is a torus, so tori admit radiant structures, as do Klein bottles
[
5
]
and
[
66
, Section
6.5.2.2
]
.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

A radiant structure on a compact surface is nonsingular.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.15
, the surface has Euler characteristic zero, so this follows from Lemma
3.8
.
∎

4.
Euler manifolds and equiaffine radiant structures

The differential of a vector field,
X
, is well defined at a point
p\in\mathsf{Zero}(X)
as the value at
p
of
\nabla_{i}X^{j}
for any torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
, for if
\bar{\nabla}=\nabla+\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}
is any other torsion-free affine connection, then, at
p
there holds
\bar{\nabla}_{i}X^{k}=\nabla_{i}X^{j}+\Pi_{ip}\,^{j}X^{p}=\nabla_{i}X^{j}
. Let
N\subset M
be a connected smoothly embedded submanifold of
M
. The case where
N
is
0
-dimensional, so a point, is allowed, and is the case of principal interest here. Following
[
18
,
74
,
111
]
, a vector field
X\in\Gamma(TM)
is
Euler-like along
N
if, for every
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
that vanishes to first order along
N
,
df(X)-f
vanishes to second order along
N
. In
[
18
]
it is also required that
X
be complete, but here this condition is omitted, as also in
[
74
]
, although in fact in the examples of interest considered here, the Euler-like vector field is complete. That
df(X)-f
vanish to second order along
N
for any
f
vanishing to first order along
N
implies that
X
vanishes along
N
as well.

A vector field
X
is Euler-like along a submanifold
N
of codimension
n-r
if and only if for every
p\in N
and every choice of local coordinates
x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r}
centered on
p
such that
\{x^{1}=0,\dots,x^{r}=0\}
defines the part of
N
contained in the coordinate neighborhood of
p
, the vector field
X
has the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n-r}a^{i}(x,y)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}(x^{i}+b^{i}(x,y))\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
with
a^{i}(x,y)
vanishing when
x^{1}=0,\dots,x^{r}=0
, and
b^{i}(x,y)
vanishing to second order when
x^{1}=0,\dots,x^{r}=0
. In particular, along
N
the differential of
X
is a projection of rank
r
onto the normal bundle of
N
, and the linear approximation to
X
along
N
is the usual Euler field on the normal bundle of
N
.

A vector field
X\in\Gamma(TM)
is
Euler-like
if each connected component of its zero set
\mathsf{Zero}(X)
is a smoothly embedded submanifold of
M
and
X
is Euler-like along each connected component of
\mathsf{Zero}(X)
.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is a radiant structure on a smooth manifold
M
, then
\mathbb{E}
is Euler-like.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.8
,
\mathsf{Zero}(\mathbb{E})
is a discrete set of points. That
\mathbb{E}
be radiant means that its differential at
p\in\mathsf{Zero}(\mathbb{E})
equals
\nabla_{i}\mathbb{E}^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
. It follows that in geodesic normal coordinates
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
centered at
p
,
\mathbb{E}
has the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+G
where
G
is a smooth vector field vanishing to second order at
p
.
∎

On an
n
-manifold
M
, a
volume form
means a nowhere-vanishing
n
-form
\Psi\in\Gamma(\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M)
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
M
be an
n
-manifold.

(1)

A vector field
\mathbb{E}
on
(M,\Psi)
is
Euler
if
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=n\Psi
.

(2)

An
Euler structure
on
M
is a pair
(\Psi,\mathbb{E})
comprising a volume form
\Psi\in\Gamma(\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M\setminus\mathsf{0}(M))
and an Euler-like Euler vector field
\mathbb{E}\in\Gamma(TM)
.

By Stokes’ Theorem, a manifold admitting an Euler vector field is noncompact, because a volume form admitting an Euler vector field is exact, for
\Psi=d\mu
where
\mu=\tfrac{1}{n}i(\mathbb{E})\Psi
. Because
\Psi
is nowhere-vanishing, prescribing
\mu
determines
\mathbb{E}
, so the form
\mu
and the Euler field are equivalent data. (The form
\mu
of an Euler structure satisfies some nontrivial condition corresponding with the Euler-like condition on the Euler vector field. This condition is not elucidated because it is not used.)

Remark 7
.

Note that
Euler
and
Euler-like
are
not
synonyms. The Euler-like condition refers to the vector field alone, while the Euler condition depends on a choice of volume form. Even when both make sense, neither condition implies the other. With respect to the volume form
\Psi=dx\wedge dy
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the vector field
X=(x+y)\partial_{x}+(y-x)\partial_{y}
is Euler but is not Euler-like (for
f(x,y)=x
,
df(X)-f=y
does not vanish to second order at the origin) while the vector field
Y=x\partial_{x}
is Euler-like but not Euler.

Remark 8
.

In some contexts it might be desirable to impose further conditions as part of the definition of an Euler structure. First, the Euler vector field could be required to be complete; this is generally the case in the examples considered later. Second, if
M
were allowed to have boundary
\partial M
and
M
were oriented, then there could be required additionally that, along
\partial M
,
\mathbb{E}
point to the exterior of
M
, or that
\mu
restrict to a volume form on
\partial M
consistent with the induced orientation.

Lemma 4.3
.

For a volume form
\Psi
and a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
on an
n
-manifold
M
the following are equivalent:

(1)

\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=0
;

(2)

\mathbb{E}
is an Euler vector field with respect to
\nabla
;

(3)

(\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is an Euler structure.

Proof.

Any volume form
\Psi
and any radiant vector field
\mathbb{E}
satisfy
\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi-n\Psi
, so
\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=n\Psi
. Since, by Lemma
4.1
, a radiant vector field is Euler-like, the equivalent conditions (
1
)-(
2
) imply
(\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is an Euler structure. That (
3
) implies (
2
) is immediate.
∎

Definition 4.4
.

(1)

A
radiant Euler structure
is a triple
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
such that
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is a radiant structure and
(\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is an Euler structure.

(2)

An
equiaffine radiant structure
is a radiant Euler structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
such that
\nabla\Psi=0
.

By Lemma
4.3
, if
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is a radiant structure and
\nabla\Psi=0
, then
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is an equiaffine radiant structure.

For any equiaffine radiant structure there holds
\nabla i(\mathbb{E})\Psi=\Psi
.

Remark 9
.

If
\mathbb{E}
is Euler with respect to
\Psi
then it is Euler with respect to any nonzero constant multiple of
\Psi
. If
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is a radiant Euler structure, so is
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},c\Psi)
for any
c\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
, so it is the homothety class of
\Psi
that matters in Definition
4.4
, rather than
\Psi
.

If
(\Psi,\mathbb{E})
is a pair comprising a volume form
\Psi
and a vector field
\mathbb{E}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=\lambda\Psi
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
, then
\tfrac{n}{\lambda}\mathbb{E}
is an Euler vector field. However, if
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is radiant, rescaling
\mathbb{E}
destroys the property that
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be radiant. Requiring that
\lambda
be
n
in the definition of Euler vector field is motivated by the condition of compatibility with a radiant connection in Definition
4.4
and by Example
10
.

Example 8
.

Products of each of radiant structures, Euler structures, and equiaffine radiant structures are again structures of the same kind.
Identify a vector field
X
on
M
with the vector field
X\oplus\mathsf{0}_{M^{\prime}}
on
M\times M^{\prime}
generated by the flow given by the product of the flow of
X
with the identity map on the
M^{\prime}
factor, and do similarly for a vector field on
M^{\prime}
. If
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
and
(\nabla^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime})
are radiant structures on
M
and
M^{\prime}
, respectively, then
\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
means the vector field on
M\times M^{\prime}
generated by the flow
\phi^{t}\times\phi^{\prime\,t}
where
\phi^{t}
and
\phi^{\prime\,t}
are the flows of
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
, respectively. If
\nabla
and
\nabla^{\prime}
are connections on
M
and
M^{\prime}
, the product connection
D
is defined by
D_{X\oplus Y}A\oplus B=\nabla_{X}A\oplus\nabla^{\prime}_{Y}B
.

It follows from the definition that if
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
and
(\nabla^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime})
are radiant structures on
M
and
M^{\prime}
then
(D,\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime})
is a radiant structure on
M\times M^{\prime}
. Similarly, it is straightforward to check that if
(\Psi,\mathbb{E})
and
(\Psi^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime})
are Euler structures on
M
and
M^{\prime}
respectively, then
(\pi^{\ast}(\Psi)\wedge(\pi^{\prime})^{\ast}(\Psi^{\prime}),\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime})
is an Euler structure on
M\times M^{\prime}
, where
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are the projections from
M\times M^{\prime}
onto its factors. Likewise, if
(\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
and
(\nabla^{\prime},\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})
are equiaffine radiant structures on
M
and
M^{\prime}
, then
(D,\mathbb{E}\oplus\mathbb{E}^{\prime},\pi^{\ast}(\Psi)\wedge(\pi^{\prime})^{\ast}(\Psi^{\prime}))
is an equiaffine radiant structure on
M\times M^{\prime}
.

Since the curvature of the product connection is the sum of the curvatures of the component connections, these constructions preserve the subclasses of the radiant, Euler, or equiaffine radiant structures for which the connection is flat or Ricci-flat.

Example 9
.

Let
\mathbb{V}
be an
n
-dimensional real vector space.
The affine structure determined by the vector space structure on
\mathbb{V}
determines a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
on
\mathbb{V}
such that if
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{V}
and
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}e_{i}
are the corresponding coordinates on
\mathbb{V}
, then
dx^{1},\dots,dx^{n}
is a
\nabla
-parallel coframe. Coordinates
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
with this property are
affine coordinates
on
\mathbb{V}
. In this case the volume form
\Psi=dx^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{n}
is
\nabla
-parallel and any
\nabla
-parallel volume form is a constant multiple of
\Psi
. In any choice of affine coordinates the vector field
\mathbb{E}
generated by the flow
\tau_{t}(p)=e^{t}p
by dilations around the origin has the form
\mathbb{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}
, so is Euler-like. Since
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=n\Psi
,
\mathbb{E}
is an Euler vector field. With
\Psi
it constitutes the
standard
Euler structure on
\mathbb{V}
, and with
\nabla
constitutes the
standard
radiant structure on
\mathbb{V}
. All these structures together constitute the
standard
equiaffine radiant structure
(\mathbb{V},\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
. The automorphisms of the radiant structure
(\mathbb{V},\nabla,\mathbb{E})
are
GL(\mathbb{V})=GL(n,\mathbb{R})
. The following stronger result is true, because a diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{V}
that preserves
\mathbb{E}
necessarily preserves
\nabla
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{E}
be the Euler vector field of real vector space
\mathbb{V}
. If a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}
satisfies
\phi^{\ast}(\mathbb{E})=\mathbb{E}
, then
\phi\in GL(\mathbb{V})
.

Proof.

Let
z=\sum_{i=0}^{n}x^{i}e_{i}
be the affine coordinates on
\mathbb{V}
corresponding with a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
. Write
\phi(p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{i}(p)e_{i}
. The component functions
\phi^{i}
are in
C^{2}(\mathbb{V})
. That
\phi^{\ast}(\mathbb{E})=\mathbb{E}
means
T\phi(p)(\mathbb{E}_{p})=\mathbb{E}_{\phi(p)}
for all
p\in\mathbb{V}
. In coordinates
\sum_{j=1}^{n}x^{j}\tfrac{\partial\phi^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}=\phi^{i}
. Differentiating this shows
\sum_{k=1}^{n}x^{k}\tfrac{\partial^{2}\phi^{i}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{k}}=0
. If
f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{V})
satisfies
df(\mathbb{E})=0
, then
f
is constant on rays emanating from the origin, so
f(x)=\lim_{t\to-\infty}f(e^{t}x)=f(0)
, showing
f
is constant. Hence
\tfrac{\partial\phi^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}
is constant for all
i
and
j
. This shows
\phi\in GL(\mathbb{V})
.
∎

The automorphism group of the equiaffine radiant structure
(\mathbb{V},\nabla,\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is the special linear subgroup
SL(\mathbb{V})=SL(n,\mathbb{R})

Lemma 4.6
.

On an
n
-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{V}
, let
X
be an Euler-like vector field having an isolated zero at
0\in\mathbb{V}
.

(1)

X=\mathbb{E}+B
where
B
is a smooth vector field vanishing to second order at
0
.

(2)

If
X
is also Euler with respect to the volume form of the standard equiaffine structure
(\nabla,\Psi,\mathbb{E})
on
\mathbb{V}
, then
X=\mathbb{E}+B
, where
B
is a smooth divergence-free vector field vanishing to second order at
0
.

(3)

There is a diffeomorphism
\phi
defined on an open neighborhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{V}
such that
\phi^{\ast}(X)=\mathbb{E}
on
U
.

Proof.

Let
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
be coordinates such that
dx^{1},\dots,dx^{n}
is a
\nabla
-parallel coframe and
\Psi=dx^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{n}
. In such coordinates,
\mathbb{E}=x^{i}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
. That
X
have an isolated zero at
0
means there are constants
a_{j}\,^{i}
and functions
b^{i}(x)
vanishing to second order at
0
such that
X=a_{p}\,^{i}x^{p}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+B
where
B=b^{i}(x)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
. Because
X
is Euler-like,
dx^{i}(X)-x^{i}=a_{p}\,^{i}x^{p}-x^{i}+b^{i}(x)
must vanish to second order at
x=0
for
1\leq i\leq n
. This forces
a_{i}\,^{j}=\delta_{i}\,^{j}
, so that
X=\mathbb{E}+B
. This shows (
1
). Since
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\Psi-n\Psi=(a_{p}\,^{p}+\tfrac{\partial b^{p}}{\partial x^{p}}-n)\Psi=\mathfrak{L}_{B}\Psi
, that
X
be also Euler forces that
B
be divergence free. This shows (
2
).

The remainder of the argument follows closely the proof of Lemma
2.4
in
[
18
]
.
Let
\tau_{t}(p)=e^{t}p
be the flow generated by
\mathbb{E}
. Define a time-dependent vector field
W:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{V}\to T\mathbb{V}
by
W(t,p)=t^{-1}\tau_{\log{t}}^{\ast}(B)_{p}
for
t>0
. In affine coordinates,
W(t,p)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}t^{-2}b^{i}(tx)\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}e_{i}
. Because the
b^{i}
vanish to second order at the origin,
W(t,p)
extends smoothly to
t=0
.

Let
\phi_{t}
be the flow of the time-dependent vector field
W
, equal to the identity when
t=0
and satisfying
\tfrac{d}{dt}\phi_{t}(p)=W(t,\phi_{t}(p))
.
Since
W(t,0)=0
for all
t
, the integral curve
\phi_{t}(0)=0
is defined for all
t
and there is an open neighborhood
U
of
0
such that, for
p\in U
, the integral curve
\phi_{t}(p)
is defined for all
t\in[0,1]
. By definition of
W
, for
a\geq 0
,
\tau_{\log{a}}^{\ast}(W)(t,p)=aW(at,p)
and it follows that
a\phi_{at}(p)=\phi_{t}(ap)
when both sides make sense, with the consequence that
\tau_{\log{a}}(U)\subset U
for
a\in(0,1]
.

Consider the vector field
Y(t,p)=tW(t,p)+\mathbb{E}(p)
. When
t=0
,
Y=\mathbb{E}
, and when
t=1
,
Y=B+\mathbb{E}=X
.
In general if
P
and
Q
are vector fields and
\psi_{t}
is the flow of
P
, there holds
\tfrac{d}{dt}\psi_{t}^{\ast}(Q)=\psi_{t}^{\ast}([P,Q])
. Using this observation and
\tau_{\log{t}}^{\ast}(\mathbb{E})=\mathbb{E}
there results that, on
U
,

(4.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{d}{dt}\phi_{t}^{\ast}(tW+\mathbb{E})&=\tfrac{d}{dt}\phi_{t}^{\ast}(\tau_{\log{t}}^{\ast}(B)+\tau_{\log{t}}^{\ast}(\mathbb{E}))\\
&=\phi_{t}^{\ast}\left([W,\tau_{\log{t}}^{\ast}(B+\mathbb{E})]+t^{-1}\tau_{\log{t}}^{\ast}([\mathbb{E},B+\mathbb{E}])\right)=\phi_{t}^{\ast}\left([W,tW+\mathbb{E}]+[\mathbb{E},W]\right)=0.\end{split}

Thus
\phi_{t}^{\ast}(tW+\mathbb{E})
is constant in
t
. At
t=0
it equals
\mathbb{E}
and at
t=1
it equals
\phi_{1}^{\ast}(X)
, so
\phi_{1}^{\ast}(X)=\mathbb{E}
on
U
.
∎

Example 10
.

The standard equiaffine radiant structure on
\mathbb{V}
restricts to the submanifold
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
be the projectivization of
\mathbb{V}
and let
\pi:\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
be the canonical projection, so that
\pi(u)=[u]
is the line generated by
u\in\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
.
The
tautological line bundle

\pi:\mathbb{O}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
has total space
\mathbb{O}=\{(L,v)\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\times\mathbb{V}:v\in L\}
and projection
\pi(L,v)=L
, so that the fiber
\mathbb{O}_{L}
over
L\in\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
is
L
. The group
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
acts on the right on
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
and this action is principal for the projection
\pi:\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
, so the latter can be viewed as a principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-bundle.
The map sending the element
[v,t]
of the bundle
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\times_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
associated with
\pi:\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
via the representation
\sigma(r)=r
of
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
to the element
[[v],tv]\in\mathbb{O}
is a line bundle isomorphism.
A section
s
of
\mathbb{O}
corresponds with a function
\tilde{s}
on
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
equivariant with respect to
\sigma
in the sense that
\tilde{s}(rv)=\sigma(r^{-1})\tilde{s}(L,v)=r^{-1}\tilde{s}(v)
for
v\in\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
. That is
\tilde{s}
has homogeneity
-1
. Consequently homogeneous degree
k
polynomials on
\mathbb{V}
determine sections of the
k
-fold tensor power
(\mathbb{O}^{\ast})^{k}=\mathbb{O}^{-k}
of the dual line bundle
\mathbb{O}^{\ast}
. Because sections are smooth, the converse claim is false; in general there are smooth sections of
\mathbb{O}^{-k}
for which the corresponding homogeneity
k
function on
\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}
is not polynomial. A simple example is
\tilde{s}(x,y)=\sqrt{x^{4}+y^{4}}
.

There holds
T\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\simeq\hom(\mathbb{O},\mathbb{V}/\mathbb{O})\simeq\mathbb{O}^{\ast}\otimes\mathbb{V}/\mathbb{O}
. Here notation is slightly abused, and
\mathbb{V}
is used to denote the trivial vector bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\times\mathbb{V}
as well as the vector space
\mathbb{V}
. Dualizing and taking determinants yields
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\simeq\mathbb{O}^{\otimes n}\otimes\operatorname{Det}(\mathbb{V}/\mathbb{O})^{\ast}
. On the other hand,
\mathbb{V}\simeq\mathbb{O}\oplus\mathbb{V}/\mathbb{O}
and taking determinants yields
\operatorname{Det}\mathbb{V}\simeq\mathbb{O}\otimes\operatorname{Det}(\mathbb{O}/\mathbb{V})
. Combining the preceding shows
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\simeq\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\otimes\operatorname{Det}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}\simeq\mathbb{O}^{n+1}
, where
n=\dim\mathbb{V}
and the first isomorphism results because
\operatorname{Det}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is a trivial line bundle. However, the trivialization is not canonical; fixing the standard volume form on
\mathbb{V}
determines an isomorphism
\mathbb{O}\simeq\operatorname{Det}(\mathbb{V}/\mathbb{O})^{\ast}
, that fixes a particular isomorphism of
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
with
\mathbb{O}^{\otimes n+1}
.

Since sections of
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
have weight
1
in the sense that they correspond to functions on the complement of the zero section,
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})\setminus\mathsf{0}(\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V}))
, homogeneous of degree
1
, sections of
\mathbb{O}
should be considered to have weight
1/(n+1)
.

Example 11
.

There is an Euler manifold
(\mathcal{V}\to M,\Psi^{\mathcal{V}},\mathbb{E})
canonically associated with an
n
-dimensional smooth manifold
M
. Its definition is motivated by Example
10
.
Let
\mathcal{V}=\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M\setminus\mathsf{0}(M)\to M
be the bundle of
n
-forms with the image of the zero section
\mathsf{0}:M\to\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M
deleted, regarded as a principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-bundle with principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-action
R_{r}
. Let
\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}
be the vector field generating the fiber dilations
R_{e^{t}}(u)=e^{t}u
on
\mathcal{V}
. The
tautological
n
-form

\mu\in\Gamma(\mathop{\bigwedge\nolimits^{\!n}}(T^{\ast}\mathcal{V}))
, defined by

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{s}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=s(T\rho(s)(X_{1}),\dots,T\rho(s)(X_{n}))

for
X_{i}\in T_{s}\mathcal{V}
, has the following properties, which determine it uniquely:

(1)

s^{\ast}(\mu)=s
for any local section
s
of
\mathcal{V}
;

(2)

R_{r}^{\ast}(\mu)=r\mu
for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
;

(3)

i(\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}})\mu=0
.

For any principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-connection
\beta
on
\mathcal{V}
there holds
d\mu=\beta\wedge\mu
as follows from evaluating
i(\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}})d\mu=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}}\mu=\mu
on the
\beta
-horizontal lift of a local frame on
M
. As
\beta\wedge\mu
is evidently a volume form, this shows that
\Psi^{\mathcal{V}}=d\mu
is a volume form on
\mathcal{V}
satisfying
i(\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}})\Psi^{\mathcal{V}}=\mu
.
Although
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}},\Psi)
is not an Euler structure, because
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}}\Psi
equals
\Psi
rather than
(n+1)\Psi
, replacing
\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}
with
\mathbb{E}=(n+1)\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}
yields the desired Euler structure
(\mathcal{V}\to M,\Psi^{\mathcal{V}},\mathbb{E})
. The triple
(\mathcal{V}\to M,\Psi^{\mathcal{V}},\mathbb{E})
could be called the
volumnification
of
M
by analogy with the symplectification of a contact manifold as in Example
12
.

Local coordinates
x^{1},\dots,x^{n}
on
U\subset M
determine a trivialization
dx^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{n}
of
\mathcal{V}
over
U
and a coordinate
t:U\to\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
on the fiber such that
\mu=t\rho^{\ast}(dx^{1})\wedge\dots\wedge\rho^{\ast}(dx^{n})
and
\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}
has the expression
t\partial_{t}
. The given trivialization is determined by a local section parallel with respect to the (locally defined) principal connection
\beta=d\log t
.
The vertical vector field
\mathbb{E}=(n+1)\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}
corresponds to the modified
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
action
R_{\operatorname{sgn}(r)|r|^{n+1}}
on
\mathcal{V}
(in the sense that its flow is
R_{e^{(n+1)t}}
), for which the given trivialization corresponds to the principal connection
\tfrac{1}{n+1}d\log t
corresponding with the fiber coordinate
\operatorname{sgn}(t)|t|^{1/(n+1)}
.

Example 12
.

The notion of Euler structure is partly modeled on the notion of a Liouville structure on a symplectic manifold
[
40
,
49
,
50
]
.
A vector field
X
on a symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
is
Liouville
if
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\Omega=\Omega
. A
Liouville structure
on a symplectic manifold
(M,\Omega)
is a Liouville vector field,
X
, usually also required to be complete and sometimes also required to satisfy some additional convexity condition.

From the point of view taken here, in the definition of a Liouville vector field it would be better to require
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\Omega=2\Omega
, the constant
2
playing the role of
n=\dim M
in the definition of an Euler vector field. If the definition of a Liouville vector field is modified to be that
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\Omega=2\Omega
, then every Liouville vector field gives rise to an Euler vector field. If the symplectic manifold has dimension
2n
, let
\Psi=\Omega^{n}
. Since
\iota(X)\Omega^{n}=n\iota(X)\Omega\wedge\Omega^{n-1}
,
\mathfrak{L}_{X}\Psi=d\iota(X)\Omega^{n}=n\mathfrak{L}_{X}\Omega\wedge\Omega^{n-1}=2n\Psi
, so
(X,\Omega^{n})
is an Euler pair.

For an
n
-manifold
N
, endow
M=T^{\ast}N
with the the vector field
\mathbb{E}
equal to the generator of the dilations by
e^{2t}
in the fibers and
\Psi=\Omega^{n}
, in which
\Omega
is the canonical symplectic form. Then
(\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is an Euler structure on
M
. This is a special case of the following observation that the symplectification of a contact manifold carries an Euler structure.
Let
C
be a contact distribution on a
(2n-1)
-dimensional manifold
M
and let
\text{Ann}\,C\subset T^{\ast}M
be the line subbundle local sections of which annihilate
C
. On the total space of the complement
\rho:\mathcal{F}=\text{Ann}\,C\setminus\mathsf{0}(M)\to M
of the zero section
\mathsf{0}(M)
of
T^{\ast}M
there is defined a tautological one-form
\theta
by
\theta_{u}(X)=u(T\rho(u)(X))
for
u\in\mathcal{F}
and
X\in T_{u}\mathcal{F}
, and
\Omega=d\theta
is a symplectic structure on
M
. Let
\mathbb{E}
generate dilations by
e^{2t}
in the fibers of
\mathcal{F}
and let
\Psi=\Omega^{n}
. Then
(\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E},\Psi)
is an Euler manifold.

Many examples of Euler structures arise on the total spaces of line bundles. Before giving the examples, some background related to line bundles is recalled.

For a real Lie group,
G
, the right action of
g\in G
on the principal
G
-bundle
\rho:\mathcal{F}\to M
is written
R_{g}(u)=u\cdot g
for
u\in\mathcal{F}
. The vector bundle
V=\mathcal{F}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{V}
associated with the representation
\sigma:G\to\text{End}(\mathbb{V})
is the quotient of
\mathcal{F}\times\mathbb{V}
by the equivalence relation
(u,v)\sim(u\cdot g,\rho(g^{-1})v)
. The image in
\mathcal{F}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{V}
of
(u,v)\in\mathcal{F}\times\mathbb{V}
is written
[u,v]
.
The
frame bundle

\pi:\mathcal{F}{\left(E\right)}\to M
of a vector bundle
E\to M
with fiber
\mathbb{V}
is the principal
GL(\mathbb{V})
-bundle such that the fiber
\pi^{-1}(p)
over
p\in M
comprises invertible linear maps
u:\mathbb{V}\to E_{p}
and
GL(\mathbb{V})
acts on the right by precomposition.
By construction,
V=\mathcal{F}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{V}\to M
is recovered from
\mathcal{F}{\left(V\right)}
as the bundle associated with the standard representation of
GL(\mathbb{V})
.
Sections
s\in\Gamma(M,V)
are in canonical bijection with functions
\tilde{s}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{V}
which are
G
-equivariant in the sense that
\tilde{s}(R_{g}(u))=\rho(g^{-1})\tilde{s}(u)
. The equivariant function
\tilde{s}\in C^{\infty}(\rho^{-1}(U))
corresponding with the local section
s\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{F}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{V})
is defined by
s(p)=[u,\tilde{s}(u)]
for
p\in M
and any
u\in\pi^{-1}(p)
.
If
G
acts on the right on a space by
u\to u\cdot g
, then
G
acts on the right on a space of functions
s
on the space by
(g\cdot s)(u)=s(u\cdot g^{-1})
. Thus the equivariance says that
g\cdot\tilde{s}=\rho(g)\tilde{s}
.

The integer power
\mathcal{L}^{k}
of a real line bundle
\mathcal{L}\to M
means the
k
-fold tensor product of
\mathcal{L}
with itself if
k
is positive, and the
k
-fold tensor product with itself of the dual line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{-1}=\mathcal{L}^{\ast}
if
k
is negative (when
k=0
it means the trivial line bundle).
The map
[u,t]\to tu(1)
establishes a vector bundle isomorphism
\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}\times_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{L}
where
\sigma(r)=r
is the standard representation of
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
. Conversely, the complement
\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathsf{0}(M)
of the zero section is identified with
\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}
via the map sending
l\in\mathcal{L}_{p}\setminus\{0\}
to
\hat{l}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{L}_{p}
defined by
\hat{l}(t)=tl
. Note that this means that sections
u
of
\mathcal{L}^{k}
correspond with functions
\tilde{u}
on
\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}
of homogeneity
-k
.

The
O(1)=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
reduction of
\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundle
\mathcal{O}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}\to M
. It is a double cover of
M
that is connected if and only if
\mathcal{L}
is orientable. The line bundle
\mathcal{L}
is recovered as the associated bundle
\mathcal{O}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)}\times_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}
where
\sigma
is the standard representation of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
on
\mathbb{R}
.
By the preceding, the spaces of isomorphism classes of real line bundles on
M
, isomorphism classes of principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-bundles on
M
, and isomorphism classes of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundles on
M
are in pairwise bijection in canonical ways. The transition functions of a real line bundle
\mathcal{L}\to M
over a good cover determine a closed Cech
1
-cocyle so an element of the Cech cohomology
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R}^{\ast})
of the locally constant sheaf
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
. The short exact sequence of locally constant sheaves
0\to\mathbb{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t\to e^{t}}}{{\to}}\mathbb{R}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\to 0
determines a long exact sequence in cohomology and because
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
is a fine sheaf, there results
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R}^{\ast})\simeq H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. The image in
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
of the Cech cocycle associated with
\mathcal{L}
is the first Stiefel-Whitney characteristic class
w_{1}(\mathcal{L})\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. The usual classifying theory for bundles establishes that any class in
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
is the first Stiefel-Whitney class of some real line bundle and that two real line bundles are isomorphic if and only if their first Stiefel-Whitney classes are equal. There are written also
w_{1}(\mathcal{L})=w_{1}(\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)})=w_{1}(\mathcal{O}{\left(\mathcal{L}\right)})
. The bundle
\mathcal{L}
is orientable if and only if
w_{1}(\mathcal{L})=0
.

Examples
10
and
11
motivate Definition
4.7
. These notions are are used in Sections
7
and
8
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
M
be an
n
-manifold.

(1)

A
hyperplane line bundle
on
M
is a real line bundle
\mathcal{E}\to M
equipped with a line bundle isomorphism
\mathcal{E}^{n+1}\simeq\operatorname{Det}TM
.

(2)

A
pseudo-hyperplane line bundle
on
M
is a real line bundle
\mathcal{E}\to M
equipped with a line bundle isomorphism
\mathcal{E}^{2n+2}\simeq(\operatorname{Det}TM)^{\otimes 2}
.

The line bundle dual to a (pseudo)-hyperplane line bundle is called a
(pseudo)-tautological line bundle
.

Although in either case it forms part of the structure, the isomorphism is not indicated explicitly in the notation. By definition, a hyperplane line bundle is a pseudo-hyperplane line bundle. If
M
admits an orientable hyperplane line bundle, then
M
is orientable, and, similarly, if
n
is odd the existence of a hyperplane bundle forces
M
to be orientable, for
w_{1}(T^{\ast}M)=(n+1)w_{1}(\mathcal{E})=0
. The notion of pseudo-hyperplane bundle is convenient because it accommodates the case of a nonorientable odd-dimensional manifold.

Example 13
.

Example
10
shows that the hyperplane line bundle
\mathbb{O}^{\ast}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
and tautological line bundle
\mathbb{O}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
are a hyperplane and tautological line bundle in the sense of Definition
4.7
.

A pseudo-hyperplane line bundle is determined by two requirements. Its tensor square is trivial as real line bundle and sections of its
(n+1)
st power transform locally like sections of
\operatorname{Det}TM
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
M
be a
n
-manifold and let
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. Up to isomorphism of real line bundles there is a unique pseudo-hyperplane line bundle
\mathcal{E}\to M
such that
w_{1}(\mathcal{E})=\omega
. In particular, the orientable pseudo-hyperplane line bundle
|\operatorname{Det}TM|^{1/(n+1)}
represents the isomorphism class of real line bundles having vanishing first Stiefel-Whitney class.

Proof.

Given
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
there is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-principal bundle
\mathcal{P}\to M
such that
w_{1}(\mathcal{P})=\omega
. Define the total space of
\mathcal{E}
to be the quotient of
\mathcal{P}\times|\operatorname{Det}TM|^{1/(n+1)}
by the action of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
given by
-(p,\mu)=(-p,-\mu)
for
(p,\mu)
in a fiber of
\mathcal{P}\times|\operatorname{Det}TM|^{1/(n+1)}
. Alternatively, let
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{P}\times_{\sigma}\mathbb{R}
where
\sigma
is the standard representation of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
on
\mathbb{R}
, and define
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{L}\otimes|\operatorname{Det}TM|^{1/(n+1)}
. This second description makes apparent that
\mathcal{E}^{2(n+1)}\simeq(\operatorname{Det}TM)^{2}
. Since
|\operatorname{Det}TM|^{1/(n+1)}
is orientable, its first Stiefel-Whitney class is trivial, so
w_{1}(\mathcal{E})=w_{1}(\mathcal{L})=\omega
, and the uniqueness follows from the fact that the first Stiefel-Whitney class determines a real line bundle up to line bundle isomorphism.
∎

Example 14
.

The notions of (pseudo-)tautological bundle of Definition
4.7
extend the notion of tautological bundle on a flat real projective manifold introduced by Loftin in
[
98
,
100
,
99
]
.
Although a manifold necessarily admits an orientable pseudo-hyperplane line bundle, in general it need not admit an orientable hyperplane line bundle. However,
[
98
, Proposition
2.2.1
]
shows that a manifold equipped with a properly convex flat real projective structure admits an orientable hyperplane line bundle.

Example 15
.

Viewed as the real projective line,
S^{1}
admits two pseudo-tautological line bundles, the topologically trivial bundle
\mathcal{L}_{1}
of
1/2
-densities and the usual tautological line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{2}
. View the
n
-dimensional torus as the
n
fold product
\mathbb{T}_{n}=S^{1}\times\dots\times S^{1}
. By the Künneth formula, any pseudo-tautological bundle on
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is the tensor product of the bundle of
1/(n+1)
-densities
|\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}\mathbb{T}_{n}|^{1/(n+1)}
with a direct sum of pullbacks
\oplus_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}^{\ast}(\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon_{i}})
,
\epsilon_{i}\in\{1,2\}
, where
p_{i}:\mathbb{T}_{n}\to S^{1}
is the projection onto the
i
th factor.

A
volume density
is a nonvanishing
1
-density. A
pseudo-Euler structure
on the
n
-manifold
M
is a pair
(\mathbb{E},\Psi)
such that
\Psi
is a volume density and
\mathbb{E}
is an Euler-like vector field satisfying
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\Psi=n\Psi
. What distinguishes a pseudo-hyperplane line bundle
\mathcal{E}
among arbitrary real line bundles is that the total space of
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{E}^{-1}\right)}
is equipped in a canonical way with a volume density compatible with the fundamental vector field generating the principal action.

Lemma 4.9
.

For a pseudo-hyperplane bundle
\mathcal{E}\to M
, let
\rho:\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}{\left(\mathcal{E}^{-1}\right)}\to M
be the frame bundle of
\mathcal{E}^{-1}
and let
\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}}
be the fundamental vector field generating dilations by
e^{t}
in the fibers of
\mathcal{F}
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{E}\to M
is a hyperplane line bundle, then there is a canonically determined volume form
\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}
on the total space
\mathcal{F}
such that
(\mathcal{F},\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}},\Psi^{\mathcal{F}})
is an Euler manifold satisfying
R_{r}^{\ast}\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}=r^{n+1}\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}
for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{E}\to M
is a pseudo-hyperplane line bundle, then there is a canonically determined volume density
|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|
on the total space
\mathcal{F}
such that
(\mathcal{F},\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}},|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|)
is a pseudo-Euler manifold satisfying
R_{r}^{\ast}|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|=|r|^{n+1}|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|
for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a hyperplane line bundle.
The frame bundle
\mathcal{F}
can be viewed as a
(n+1)
st root of the principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-bundle
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{F}{\left(\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M\right)}\simeq\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M\setminus\mathsf{0}(M)
discussed in Example
11
and so acquires a structure of an Euler manifold as follows. The isomorphism
\mathcal{E}^{-n-1}\simeq\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M
induces a principal
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
-bundle morphism
\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{V}
that satisfies
\mathcal{Q}(ru)=r^{n+1}\mathcal{Q}(u)
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
and is either a diffeomorphism onto its image or a local diffeomorphism that is
2-1
onto its image, as
n
is even or odd. Because
T\mathcal{Q}(\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}})=(n+1)\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{V}}
, the pullback
\mu^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ast}(\mu)
of the tautological
n
-form
\mu
on
\mathcal{V}
satisfies
\iota(\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}})d\mu^{\mathcal{F}}=(n+1)\mu^{\mathcal{F}}
, so
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}}}\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}=(n+1)\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}
where
\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}=d\mu^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ast}(\Psi^{\mathcal{V}})
. This shows that
(\mathcal{F},\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}},\Psi^{\mathcal{F}})
is an Euler manifold.
Explicitly, if
u\in\mathcal{F}
and
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in T_{u}\mathcal{F}
,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}_{u}(\mathbb{E},X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\langle u^{n+1},T\rho(u)(X_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge T\rho(u)(X_{n})\rangle,

where
\langle\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,\rangle
indicates the pairing of the volume form
u^{n+1}\in\operatorname{Det}T_{\rho(u)}^{\ast}M
with the
n
-vector
T\rho(u)(X_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge T\rho(u)(X_{n})\in\operatorname{Det}T_{\rho(u)}M
.
If
\mathcal{E}
is only a pseudo-hyperplane line bundle, then the preceding discussion applies on an open neighborhood of
M
over which
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
are trivial with the proviso that the pulled-back forms
\mu^{\mathcal{F}}
and
d\mu^{\mathcal{F}}
are determined only up to sign. Taking the absolute value of
d\mu^{\mathcal{F}}
yields the volume density
|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|
. Concretely, in this case the right-hand side of (
4.3
) is defined up to a sign, and taking its absolute value defines
|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|_{u}(\mathbb{E},X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
.
That
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{X}^{\mathcal{F}}}|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|=(n+1)|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|
follows from the hyperplane line bundle case because this identity is a purely local claim.
That
R_{r}^{\ast}\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}=r^{n+1}|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|
and
R_{r}^{\ast}|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|=|r|^{n+1}|\Psi^{\mathcal{F}}|
for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
are immediate from (
4.3
).
∎

5.
Conelike radiant structures

Let
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be a radiant structure on
M
. (The reader is reminded that by the definition of a radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, its underlying connection
\nabla
is torsion-free.) A smoothly immersed surface
\Sigma
in
M
is
planelike
if it is totally geodesic and
\mathbb{E}_{p}\in T_{p}\Sigma
for all
p\in\Sigma
. A planelike surface
\Sigma
is a
plane
if, moreover, the connection induced on
\Sigma
by
\nabla
is flat. A radiant structure is
conelike
if it
admits a complete set of planelike surfaces
in the sense that for every
p\in M
and every two-dimensional subspace
L\subset T_{p}M
containing
\mathbb{E}_{p}
there is a smoothly immersed planelike surface
\Sigma\subset M
containing
p
and such that
T_{p}\Sigma=L
.

A planelike surface inherits a radiant structure, so, by Theorem
3.15
, a compact immersed planelike surface is topologically a torus or a Klein bottle.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=\Pi_{(ij)}\,^{k}
and
X^{i}
be given and suppose that
X^{p}\Pi_{ip}\,^{j}=0
. Let
S_{ij}\,^{kl}=\Pi_{ij}\,^{[k}X^{l]}
. If
S_{(ij}\,^{[ab}\delta_{k)}\,^{c]}=0
, then there exists a smooth tensor
Q_{ij}=Q_{(ij)}
on
M_{\ast}=\{p\in M:X_{p}\neq 0\}
such that

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}X^{k}-2\delta_{(i}\,^{k}Q_{j)p}X^{p}=X^{p}\left(Q_{ij}\delta_{p}\,^{k}-2Q_{p(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}\right)

on
M_{\ast}
. Moreover,
\Pi_{ip}\,^{p}=-nQ_{ip}X^{p}
and
X^{p}X^{q}Q_{pq}=0
.

Proof.

Tracing
S_{(ij}\,^{[ab}\delta_{k)}\,^{c]}=0
in
c
and
k
yields
nS_{ij}\,^{kl}=-4\delta_{(i}\,^{[k}S_{j)p}\,^{l]p}
. Substituting into this the definition of
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}
yields
n\Pi_{ij}\,^{[k}X^{l]}-2\delta_{(i}\,^{[k}X^{l]}\Pi_{j)p}\,^{p}=-2\delta_{(i}\,^{[k}\Pi_{j)p}\,^{l]}X^{p}=0
, the last equality because
X^{p}\Pi_{ip}\,^{j}=0
. This shows that for any vector field
Y^{i}
there vanishes the wedge product with
X
of the vector field
Y^{p}Y^{q}(n\Pi_{pq}\,^{i}-2\delta_{(p}\,^{i}\Pi_{q)a}\,^{a})
. Hence there is
Q_{ij}\in\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M_{\ast})
such that
n\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}-2\delta_{(i}\,^{k}\Pi_{j)p}\,^{p}=nQ_{ij}X^{k}
on
M_{\ast}
. Tracing this shows that
\Pi_{ip}\,^{p}=-nQ_{ip}X^{p}
, and (
5.1
) and
X^{p}X^{q}Q_{pq}=0
follow.
∎

A radiant structure is
nonsingular
if
\mathbb{E}
is nonsingular. By Lemma
3.8
this is automatic if
M
is compact and odd-dimensional. Because, by Lemma
3.8
, the zeros of a radiant vector field are isolated, results about nonsingular radiant structures apply to arbitrary radiant structures over the open submanifold
M_{\ast}=\{p\in M:\mathbb{E}_{p}\neq 0\}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

A nonsingular radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
on an
n
-manifold is conelike if and only if there is
Q_{ij}\in\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M)
such that
n\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{ip}=\rho_{i}
and
\mathbb{E}^{p}\mathbb{E}^{q}Q_{pq}=0
and satisfying the equivalent identities

(5.2)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}-2\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{p(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}.

In this case,

(5.3)

\displaystyle(n-2)Q_{ij}+(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}+\tfrac{n-2}{n}\nabla_{(i}\rho_{j)}=(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{(ij)},

\displaystyle d\rho_{ij}=2(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{[ij]}.

If, moreover,
\nabla
has symmetric Ricci tensor then
\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{ip}=0
and
\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is conelike. Let
p\in M
, let
L\subset T_{p}M
contain
\mathbb{E}_{p}
, and let
\Sigma
be a smoothly immersed planelike surface containing
p
and tangent to
L
.
Choose an open neighborhood
U\subset M
of
p
and a vector field
A
such that over
\Sigma\cap U
the vector fields
\mathbb{E}
and
A
span
T(\Sigma\cap U)
. Since
\Sigma
is totally geodesic
\nabla_{A}A
and
\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}A
are tangent to
\Sigma\cap U
, from which it follows that
\mathbb{E}\wedge A\wedge R(\mathbb{E},A)A=0
on
U\cap\Sigma
. Since
p
and
L
are arbitrary (except that
L
must contain
\mathbb{E}_{p}
) this shows that
\mathbb{E}\wedge A\wedge R(\mathbb{E},A)A=0
on
M
for all
A\in\Gamma(TM)
.

By (
3.16
) and the algebraic Bianchi identity, the tensor
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}^{p}\Pi_{ip}\,^{j}=0
and
\Pi_{[ij]}\,^{k}=0
. The preceding paragraph shows that the tensor
S_{ij}\,^{kl}=\Pi_{ij}\,^{[k}\mathbb{E}^{l]}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{[k}\mathbb{E}^{l]}
satisfies
S_{(ij}\,^{[ab}\delta_{k)}\,^{c]}=0
. By Lemma
5.1
there is a tensor
Q_{ij}
satisfying (
5.2
). Tracing (
5.2
) in
i
and
k
shows
n\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{pj}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pj}=\rho_{j}
.

Suppose
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is a nonsingular radiant structure and there is a tensor
Q_{ij}
as in the statement of the lemma. Fix
p\in M
and
\bar{X}\in T_{p}M
. Let
\sigma(t)
be the maximal integral curve of
\mathbb{E}
such that
\sigma(0)=p
, and let
X(t)
be a parallel vector field along
\sigma
such that
X(0)=\bar{X}
. By (
5.2
),

(5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}nR(X,\dot{\sigma})\dot{\sigma}&=-nR(\mathbb{E},X)\mathbb{E}=n(Q(X,\mathbb{E})\mathbb{E}-Q(\mathbb{E},X)\mathbb{E}-Q(\mathbb{E},\mathbb{E})x)=0.\end{split}

By Lemma
3.7
the maximal integral curve
C
of
\mathbb{E}
passing through
p
is contained in the image of the maximal geodesic of
\nabla
passing through
p
, which is parametrized by
\sigma
since
\dot{\sigma}\wedge\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\sigma}=0
. Together with (
5.4
) and (
2.17
) this shows that
X
is a projective Jacobi field along
C
.
It follows that
X
generates a deformation of the image of
\sigma
through projective geodesics, and that the submanifold swept out by these geodesics is a totally geodesic submanifold passing through
p
and tangent to
\text{Span}\,\{\mathbb{E}_{p},X\}
at
p
. Since this argument can be applied at any
p\in M
, it shows
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is conelike.

Suppose there is
Q_{ij}
as in the statement of the lemma and satisfying (
5.2
). Rewriting (
5.2
) yields

(5.5)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{ij}\,^{k}-\tfrac{2}{n+1}\delta_{(i}\,^{k}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla)_{j)p}\,^{p}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}+\tfrac{2}{n}\rho_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}.

Differentiating the second equality of (
5.5
) yields

(5.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{ijk}\,^{l}+\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{i}R_{pjk}\,^{l}+\tfrac{2}{n}\nabla_{i}\rho_{(j}\delta_{k)}\,^{l}=nQ_{jk}\delta_{i}\,^{l}+\mathbb{E}^{l}\nabla_{i}Q_{jk}.\end{split}

The differential Bianchi identity yields

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{q}R_{pjk}\,^{q}=\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{p}R_{jk}-\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{j}R_{pk}=(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{jk}-R_{jk}-\nabla_{j}\rho_{k}.

Tracing (
5.6
) in
i
and
l
and substituting (
5.7
) in the result yields

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{ij}=(n-2)Q_{ij}+(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}+\tfrac{n-1}{n}\nabla_{i}\rho_{j}+\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla_{j}\rho_{i}.\end{split}

Decomposing (
5.8
) by symmetries yields (
5.3
). If
\nabla
is Ricci symmetric, then
\rho_{j}=0
, and in (
5.5
) this yields the final claim.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

A nonsingular radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
satisfying
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\nabla=0
is conelike with
\rho=0
and admits a complete set of planes.

Proof.

That
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
is conelike with
\rho=0
is immediate from Lemma
5.2
. Because
\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}=0
, the connection induced on a planelike surface
\Sigma
is flat, so the given radiant structure admits a complete set of planes.
∎

Two conelike radiant structures with the same radiant vector field
have the same planelike surfaces
if each planelike surface of one radiant structure is a planelike surface of the other radiant structure.

Lemma 5.4
.

Conelike nonsingular radiant structures
(M,\nabla,\mathbb{E})
and
(M,\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
have the same planelike surfaces if and only if their difference tensor
\Pi=\tilde{\nabla}-\nabla
has the form
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}-2\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{p(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
with
Q_{[ij]}=0
and
\mathbb{E}^{p}\mathbb{E}^{q}Q_{pq}=0
.
In this case, if
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\nabla
induce the same connection on some, and hence any, bundle of densities of nontrivial weight, then
\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{ip}=0
, so that
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}
.

Proof.

Fix
p\in M
and
L\in T_{p}M
containing
\mathbb{E}_{p}
, and let
\Sigma
be a smoothly immersed
\nabla
-planelike surface passing through
p
and tangent to
L
. Let
A
be a vector field tangent to
\Sigma
near
p
. Then
\tilde{\nabla}_{A}A=\nabla_{A}A+Q(A,A)\mathbb{E}+2Q(\mathbb{E},A)A
is tangent to
\Sigma
because
\nabla_{A}A
is tangent to
\Sigma
. Since
\tilde{\nabla}_{\mathbb{E}}A=\nabla_{\mathbb{E}}A
and
\tilde{\nabla}\mathbb{E}=\nabla\mathbb{E}
this suffices to show that
\Sigma
is
\tilde{\nabla}
-totally geodesic, and it follows that
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\nabla
have the same planelike surfaces.

Suppose the given radiant structures have the same planelike surfaces. The difference tensor
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=\tilde{\nabla}-\nabla
satisfies
\Pi_{[ij]}\,^{k}=0
, because both connections are torsion-free, and
\mathbb{E}^{p}\Pi_{ip}\,^{j}=\tilde{\nabla}_{i}\mathbb{E}^{j}-\nabla_{i}\mathbb{E}^{j}=0
. Let
\gamma(t)
be a
\nabla
-geodesic such that
\gamma(0)\in M
and
\dot{\gamma}(0)\neq\mathbb{E}_{\gamma(0)}
, let
L=\text{Span}\,\{\mathbb{E}_{\gamma(0)},\dot{\gamma}(0)\}
, and let
\Sigma
be a planelike surface through
\gamma(0)
tangent to
L
. By assumption the
\tilde{\nabla}
-geodesic
\tilde{\gamma}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=\gamma(0)
and
\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}(0)=\dot{\gamma}(0)
lies on
\Sigma
. Hence
0=\tilde{\nabla}_{d/dt}\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}=\nabla_{d/dt}\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}+\Pi(\dot{\tilde{\gamma}},\dot{\tilde{\gamma}})
. At
t=0
this gives
\Pi(\dot{\gamma}(0),\dot{\gamma}(0))\wedge\mathbb{E}_{\gamma(0)}\wedge\dot{\gamma}(0)=0
. Since
\gamma
is an arbitrary geodesic, this shows that at every point of
M
there holds
\Pi(A,A)\wedge\mathbb{E}\wedge A=0
for all vectors
A
transverse to
\mathbb{E}
. The preceding sentence shows
S_{ij}\,^{ab}=\Pi_{ij}\,^{[a}\mathbb{E}^{b]}
satisfies
S{(ij}\,^{[ab}\delta_{k)}\,^{c]}=0
. By Lemma
5.1
there is a tensor
Q_{ij}
with the claimed properties. If
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\nabla
induce the same connection on some bundle of densities of nontrivial weight, then
-n\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{ip}=\Pi_{ip}\,^{p}=0
, which shows the final claim.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be a radiant structure on the
n
-manifold
M
. Suppose
Q_{ij}\in\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M)
is such that
\mathbb{E}^{p}\mathbb{E}^{q}Q_{pq}=0
and define
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+\Pi
where
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}-2q_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
with
q_{i}=\mathbb{E}^{p}Q_{pi}
. Then
(\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
is a radiant structure and the curvature tensors of
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\nabla
are related by

(5.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{R}_{ijk}\,^{l}&=R_{ijk}\,^{l}+\delta_{i}\,^{l}\left(Q_{jk}+\nabla_{j}q_{k}+q_{j}q_{k}\right)-\delta_{j}\,^{l}\left(Q_{ik}+\nabla_{i}q_{k}+q_{i}q_{k}\right)\\
&\qquad+\mathbb{E}^{l}\left(2\nabla_{[i}Q_{j]k}+2q_{[i}Q_{j]k}\right)-dq_{ij}\delta_{k}\,^{l},\end{split}

(5.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{ij}=R_{ij}+(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}+(n-2)\left(Q_{ij}+\nabla_{i}q_{j}+q_{i}q_{j}\right)+dq_{ij},

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{[ij]}=R_{[ij]}+\tfrac{n}{2}dq_{ij},

(5.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{i}

\displaystyle-\rho_{i}=n\mathbb{E}^{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{pi}=n(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}q)_{i}.

The connections
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\nabla
induce the same connection on a line bundle of densities of nontrivial weight over
M
if and only if
q_{i}=0
.
In this case
\tilde{\rho}_{i}=\rho_{i}
.

Proof.

Calculations using
\tilde{R}_{ijk}\,^{l}-R_{ijk}\,^{l}=2\nabla_{[i}\Pi_{j]k}\,^{l}+2\Pi_{p[i}\,^{l}\Pi_{j]k}\,^{p}
yield (
5.9
) and (
5.10
).
Since
q_{p}\mathbb{E}^{p}=0
,
\mathbb{E}^{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{pi}=(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}q)_{i}=\mathbb{E}^{p}dq_{pi}=\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{p}q_{i}-\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{i}q_{p}=\mathbb{E}^{p}\nabla_{p}q_{i}+q_{i}
. Combining this with (
5.9
) yields (
5.11
). Since
\Pi_{ip}\,^{p}=-nq_{i}
, the connections
\tilde{\nabla}
and
\nabla
induce the same connection on any line bundle of densities of nontrivial weight, e.g.
\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M
, if and only if
q_{i}=0
.
∎

Example 16
.

Suppose the radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
on an
n
-manifold satisfies
\rho_{i}=0
and its Ricci tensor has positive homogeneity
\lambda\neq 2-n
, so that
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{ij}=\lambda R_{ij}
. By Lemma
5.5
, the connection
\bar{\nabla}=\nabla+\tfrac{1}{2-n-\lambda}R_{(ij)}\mathbb{E}^{k}
forms with
\mathbb{E}
a radiant structure with Ricci tensor
\bar{R}_{ij}
satisfying
\bar{R}_{(ij)}=0
and
\bar{R}_{[ij]}=R_{[ij]}
. In particular, if the Ricci tensor of
\nabla
is symmetric and has positive homogeneity
\lambda\neq 2-n
, so that
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{ij}=\lambda R_{ij}
, then the connection
\bar{\nabla}=\nabla+\tfrac{1}{2-n-\lambda}R_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}
forms with
\mathbb{E}
a Ricci-flat radiant structure.

By Lemma
5.4
, the difference tensor of conelike nonsingular radiant structures
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
and
(\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
having the same planelike surfaces satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
5.5
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
M
be a manifold of dimension
n>2
. Let
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
be a conelike nonsingular radiant structure on
M
such that
\rho_{i}=0
.

(1)

There is a unique
\mathbb{E}
-invariant connection
\tilde{\nabla}
such that
(\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
is a conelike nonsingular radiant structure having antisymmetric Ricci tensor, having the same planes as has
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, and inducing on
|\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M|
the same connection as that induced by
\nabla
.

(2)

If a Lie group
G
acts on
M
by automorphisms of
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
, then
G
acts by automorphisms of
(\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
.

(3)

If
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
has symmetric Ricci tensor, then there is a unique Ricci-flat,
\mathbb{E}
-invariant connection
\tilde{\nabla}
such that
(\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
is a conelike nonsingular radiant structure having the same planes as has
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
and inducing on
|\operatorname{Det}T^{\ast}M|
the same connection as that induced by
\nabla
.

Proof.

Fix a conelike nonsingular radiant structure
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
on
M
. By Lemma
5.4
, the connection
\tilde{\nabla}
of the most general conelike radiant structure
(\tilde{\nabla},\mathbb{E})
having the same planes as has
(\nabla,\mathbb{E})
has the form
\tilde{\nabla}=\nabla+\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}
with
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=Q_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}-2q_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}
for
Q_{ij}
and
q_{i}
satisfying
Q_{[ij]}=0
,
\mathbb{E}^{j}Q_{ij}=q_{i}
, and
\mathbb{E}^{p}q_{p}=0
. By Lemma
5.2
,
\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}+\tfrac{2}{n}\rho_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}=T_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}
for a tensor
T_{ij}
satisfying
T_{[ij]}=0
,
n\mathbb{E}^{p}T_{pi}=\rho_{i}
, and
\mathbb{E}^{p}\mathbb{E}^{q}T_{pq}=0
. By (
5.3
) of Lemma
5.2
,

(5.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}T)_{ij}&-(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\mathsf{Ric})_{ij}=(2-n)T_{ij}+\tfrac{1-n}{n}\nabla_{i}\rho_{j}+\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla_{j}\rho_{j}.\end{split}

From (
3.17
) and (
5.9
) there follows

(5.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}\tilde{\nabla})_{ij}\,^{k}&=\mathbb{E}^{p}\tilde{R}_{pij}\,^{k}=\mathbb{E}^{p}R_{pij}\,^{k}+(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}-2(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}q)_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}\\
&=(T_{ij}+(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij})\mathbb{E}^{k}-\delta_{i}\,^{k}\left((\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}q)_{j}+\tfrac{1}{n}\rho_{j}\right)-\delta_{j}\,^{k}\left((\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}q)_{i}+\tfrac{1}{n}\rho_{j}\right).\end{split}

If
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}=-T_{ij}
then
-n(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}q)_{i}=-n\mathbb{E}^{p}(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ip}=n\mathbb{E}^{p}T_{pi}=\rho_{i}
, and so it follows from (
5.13
) that
\tilde{\nabla}
is
\mathbb{E}
-invariant if and only if
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}=-T_{ij}
. If this is the case, then by (
5.10
) it must be that

(5.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{ij}

\displaystyle=R_{ij}-T_{ij}+(n-2)\left(Q_{ij}+\nabla_{i}q_{j}+q_{i}q_{j}\right)+dq_{ij},

but it is not clear that there can always be found a tensor
Q_{ij}
so that (
5.14
) yields antisymmetric
\tilde{R}_{ij}
.

Now suppose that
\rho_{i}=0
. It follows from (
5.12
) that if
Q_{ij}
is defined by
(n-2)Q_{ij}=T_{ij}-R_{(ij)}
, then
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathbb{E}}Q)_{ij}=-T_{ij}
, so that
\tilde{\nabla}
is
\mathbb{E}
-invariant by (
5.13
). In this case,
