is the cone connection determined by the extended projective structure
[\bar{\nabla},\theta]
on
\mathbb{V}
.

The connection
\nabla
on
G
obtained in the case where
A_{ij}\,^{k}=0
, so that
\bar{\nabla}_{e_{i}}e_{j}=0
for all
i
and
j
, is the special case of Theorem
11.2
where the invariant symmetric tensor
g
is taken to be
g=\theta\otimes\theta
, so that
\nabla
is the left-invariant connection associated with the tensor
\Pi+\tfrac{1}{2}[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,]
where
\Pi=\theta\otimes\delta+\delta\otimes\theta-\theta\otimes\theta\otimes Z
.

Let
G=\operatorname{Heis}_{2n+1}
. Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{V}
be a lattice, let
H=\Gamma\oplus\mathbb{R}\subset G
, and let
K=\Gamma\oplus\mathbb{Z}\subset G
. Then
G/H\simeq\mathbb{V}/\Gamma=\mathbb{T}_{2n}
is a
2n
-dimensional torus,
H/K\simeq S^{1}
, and the fibration
\rho
descends to give a fiber bundle
S^{1}=H/K\to G/K\to\mathbb{T}_{2n}=G/H
. The radiant structure
(\nabla,\theta)
descends to
G/K
and is the cone connection of the extended projective structure determined by
\theta
and the descent of
\bar{\nabla}
to
\mathbb{T}_{2n}
.

Example 34
.

This example constructs a left-invariant radiant structure on a nonunimodular solvable Lie group for which
\rho
is neither vanishing nor closed.
The simplest example of a Lie algebra admitting an exact symplectic form is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{A}(1,\mathbb{C})
of affine transformations of the complex affine line. To make computations in the
\mathfrak{g}
there is fixed a basis
\{e_{i}\}
. The dual basis in
\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
is written
\{e^{i}\}
. Write
[e_{i},e_{j}]=c_{ij}\,^{k}e_{k}
, so that
de^{k}=-\tfrac{1}{2}c_{ij}\,^{k}e^{i}\wedge e^{j}=-c_{ij}\,^{k}e^{i}\otimes e^{j}
. Here
d
can be understood either as the Lie algebra cohomology codifferential, or as the exterior differential of the left-invariant one-form on
G
corresponding to
e^{i}
(see
[
117
]
). An element
x\in\mathfrak{g}
is written as
\sum_{i}x_{i}e_{i}
.
With respect to the basis
e_{1}=(1,0)
,
e_{2}=(\mathsf{i},0)
,
e_{3}=(0,1)
, and
e_{4}=(0,\mathsf{i})
of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{A}(1,\mathbb{C})
, the Lie bracket is

(11.31)

\displaystyle[x,y]

\displaystyle=(x_{1}y_{3}-x_{3}y_{1}-x_{2}y_{4}+x_{4}y_{2})e_{3}+(x_{1}y_{4}-x_{4}y_{1}+x_{2}y_{3}-x_{3}y_{2})e_{4}.

The commutator is
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]=\text{Span}\,\{e_{3},e_{4}\}
, which is abelian, but stable under the adjoint action, showing that
\mathfrak{g}
is solvable but not nilpotent. Since
\mathsf{tr}\,\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\,\cdot\,\,)=2e^{1}
,
\mathfrak{g}
is not unimodular.
Calculation using (
11.31
) shows
B(x,y)=2(x_{1}y_{1}-x_{2}y_{2})
. Using (
11.26
), it can be checked that the connection associated by Theorem
11.4
with
t=-e_{1}
is given by

(11.32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(x,y)&=(-x_{1}y_{1}+\tfrac{1}{4}x_{2}y_{2})e_{1}-(x_{1}y_{2}+x_{2}y_{1})e_{2}\\
&\quad-(x_{3}y_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{2}y_{4}-x_{4}y_{2}))e_{3}-(x_{4}y_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{2}y_{3}-x_{3}y_{2}))e_{4},\end{split}

and constitutes with
E^{t}=E^{-e_{1}}
a left-invariant radiant structure on a group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Because
de^{1}=0
, by (
11.25
) its Ricci curvature vanishes. Consider the connection
\bar{\nabla}
corresponding with the tensor

(11.33)

\displaystyle\bar{A}=A+e^{4}\otimes\delta+\delta\otimes e^{4}-(e^{4}\otimes e^{1}+e^{1}\otimes e^{4})\otimes e_{1}.

Because
\bar{A}(\,\cdot\,\,,t)=A(\,\cdot\,\,,t)
,
(\nabla,E^{t})
is again radiant. The skew-symmetric part of its Ricci tensor can be calculated using (
11.4
). Because
\bar{A}_{ip}\,^{p}=4e^{4}-2e^{1}
, there results that the skew-symmetric part of
\bar{\mathsf{ric}}
equals the symplectic form
-2de^{4}=2(e^{1}\wedge e^{4}+e^{2}\wedge e^{3})
. Consequently,
\rho=-de^{4}(t,\,\cdot\,\,)=-e^{4}
is nonvanishing and is not closed.

Because a Lie group that admits a finite-volume quotient by a discrete subgroup is unimodular
[
113
, Lemma
6.2
]
, this example cannot yield a compact radiant manifold with
\rho
nonvanishing. Moreover, the construction uses the nonunimodularity in an essential way; a multiple of the radiant field is Killing dual to the trace of the adjoint representation.

12.
Left-invariant conelike radiant structures on three-dimensional unimodular Lie groups

Among the simplest interesting examples of conelike radiant structures are the left-invariant conelike radiant structures on three-dimensional unimodular Lie groups.
The main result of this section is that the isomorphism classes of left-invariant conelike radiant structures on a three-dimensional unimodular nonnilpotent Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
are in bijection with the
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
orbits of semisimple elements of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Three-dimensional unimodular Lie groups were classified by L. Bianchi
[
13
]
. Modern references include
[
113
]
and
[
110
, Section
2
]
.

The claims about
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie algebras stated in Lemmas
12.1
and
12.2
are closely related to and could be deduced from the results of
[
81
,
104
]
, which describe simple three-dimensional Lie algebras over arbitrary fields.

Lemma 12.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie algebra over a field
\mathbb{k}
of characteristic not dividing
6
and having Killing form
B_{\mathfrak{g}}\in S^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
. For
0\neq t\in\mathfrak{g}
there holds

(12.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)(\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\operatorname{Id}_{\mathfrak{g}})=0,

\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is semisimple or nilpotent as
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
or
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=0
, and
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{k}
if and only if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)/2
is a square in
\mathbb{k}
.

Proof.

Let
0\neq t\in\mathfrak{g}
and write
T=\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
. Because
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{k}\notin\{2,3\}
, there holds
6\det A=(\mathsf{tr}\,A)^{3}-3(\mathsf{tr}\,A)(\mathsf{tr}\,A^{2})+2\mathsf{tr}\,A^{3}
for any
A\in\text{End}(\mathfrak{g})
. Since
T(t)=0
and
\mathfrak{g}
is unimodular, with
T
in place of
A
this yields
0=6\det T=2\mathsf{tr}\,T^{3}
, so
\mathsf{tr}\,T^{3}=0
. If
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=0
, then
\mathsf{tr}\,T^{k}=0
for
1\leq k\leq 3
, and, because
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{k}\notin\{2,3\}
, this implies
T
is nilpotent, so
T^{3}=0
and there holds (
12.1
). Suppose
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
. Since
T(t)=0
, by the Cayley-Hamilton theorem there are
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{k}
such that
T^{3}+\alpha T^{2}+\beta T=0
. Tracing this yields
0=\mathsf{tr}\,T^{3}+\alpha B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)+\beta\mathsf{tr}\,T=\alpha B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
, so
\alpha=0
, since
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t,)\neq 0
. Hence
T(T^{2}+\beta\operatorname{Id})=0
. If
\beta=0
, then
T^{3}=0
, so
T
is nilpotent,
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=0
, and there holds (
12.1
). If
\beta\neq 0
, then the characteristic polynomial of
T
is
x(x^{2}+\beta)
so
T
is semisimple and there is a
2
-dimensional subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
on which
T^{2}+\beta\operatorname{Id}
restricts to
0
. It follows that
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=\mathsf{tr}\,T^{2}=-2\beta
, and this shows (
12.1
) holds for all
t\in\mathfrak{g}
. If
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)/2
is a square in
\mathbb{k}
, then the characteristic polynomial of
T
has three distinct factors over
\mathbb{k}
, so
T
is diagonalizable.
∎

Lemma 12.2
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is a
3
-dimensional unimodular real Lie algebra that is not abelian, then there is a basis
\{t,a,b\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying

(12.2)

\displaystyle[t,a]=2b,

\displaystyle[t,b]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)a,

\displaystyle[a,b]=\epsilon t,

where
\epsilon=0
if
B_{g}(t,t)=0
,
\epsilon\in\{0,1,-1\}
if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,-1\}
if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)>0
. There hold
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,a)=0
,
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,b)=0
,
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(a,b)=0
,
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(a,a)=-4\epsilon
, and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(b,b)=\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
.

(1)

If
\epsilon=0
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=0
, then
\mathfrak{g}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{heis}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.

(2)

If
\epsilon=1
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
, then
\mathfrak{g}
is simple and compact.

(3)

If
\epsilon=-1
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
, then
\mathfrak{g}
is simple and noncompact.

(4)

If
\epsilon=0
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
, then
\mathfrak{g}
is solvable but not nilpotent.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent, and its center has dimension at least
2
, then it is abelian. Otherwise, by Lemma
11.5
it is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{heis}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
. In this case the proof of Lemma
11.5
shows that there is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
having the form (
12.2
) where
b
spans
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})
. By Lemma
12.1
, if an element
x
of a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is Killing isotropic, then
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(x)
is nilpotent, so if every element of
\mathfrak{g}
is Killing isotropic, then every element of
\mathfrak{g}
is
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}
-nilpotent, so
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent. Hence if
\mathfrak{g}
is not nilpotent, there is
t\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
.
By (
12.1
) of Lemma
12.1
, if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)>0
, then
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{R}
and there are nonzero
u_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
Tu_{\pm}=\pm\alpha u_{\pm}
where
\alpha=\sqrt{(B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)/2)}
, and
a=\alpha^{-1}(u_{+}+u_{-})
and
b=\tfrac{1}{2}(u_{+}-u_{-})
constitute with
t
a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and satisfy
[t,a]=2b
and
[t,b]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)b
.
If
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
, then
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is diagonalizable over
\mathbb{C}
but not over
\mathbb{R}
, and there are nonzero
u,v\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
Tu=-\alpha v
and
Tv=\alpha u
where
\alpha=\sqrt{-(B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)/2)}
, and
a=\alpha^{-1}(u+v)
and
b=\tfrac{1}{2}(u-v)
constitute with
t
a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and satisfy
[t,a]=2b
and
[t,b]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)b
.
In either case,
[a,b]=pa+qb+rt
for
p,q,r\in\mathbb{R}
. Because
\mathfrak{g}
is unimodular,
0=\mathsf{tr}\,\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(a)=q
and
0=\mathsf{tr}\,\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(b)=-p
, so
[a,b]=rt
. Replacing
a
and
b
by
\bar{a}=ca
and
\bar{b}=cb
with
c=\sqrt{|r|}
yields a basis
\{t,\bar{a},\bar{b}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying
[t,\bar{a}]=2\bar{b}
,
[t,\bar{b}]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\bar{a}
, and
[\bar{a},\bar{b}]=\epsilon t
with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,-1\}
. If
\epsilon=1
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)>0
then taking
\hat{a}=(2/\alpha)\bar{b}
and
\hat{b}=(\alpha/2)a
the basis
\{t,\hat{a},\hat{b}\}
satisfies
[t,\hat{a}]=2\hat{b}
,
[t,\hat{b}]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\hat{a}
, and
[\hat{a},\hat{b}]=-t
, so if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)>0
it can be assumed
\epsilon\in\{0,-1\}
.
This proves there is a basis as in (
12.2
).

That the Killing pairings of the elements of the basis as in (
12.2
) are as indicated follows from (
12.2
) by direct computation. If
\epsilon=0
then
\text{Span}\,\{a,b\}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
is a proper ideal but is not nilpotent, so
\mathfrak{g}
is solvable and not nilpotent. If
\epsilon\neq 0
, then
B
is nondegenerate, so
\mathfrak{g}
is simple. If
\epsilon=-1
then
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(b,b)
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
have opposite signs, so
\mathfrak{g}
is not compact, whereas if
\epsilon=1
, then
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(b,b)
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
have the same sign while
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(a,a)<0
, so
\mathfrak{g}
is compact if and only if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
too.
∎

Remark 30
.

In the nonnilpotent case of Lemma
12.2
there are possible four Lie algebras and five cases. The case
\epsilon=1
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
corresponds to a
2
-dimensional adjoint orbit in
\mathfrak{so}(3)
, the cases
\epsilon=-1
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
of either sign corresponds to the two types of
2
-dimensional adjoint orbits in
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
, and the cases
\epsilon=0
correspond to the
2
-dimensional adjoint orbits in the groups of motions of
2
-dimensional Euclidean and
2
-dimensional Minkowski space.

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
G
be a
3
-dimensional Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and Killing form
B_{\mathfrak{g}}\in S^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
. Suppose the radiant vector field
\mathbb{X}=E^{t}
of a left-invariant conelike radiant structure on
G
is generated by
t\in\mathfrak{g}
.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is unimodular and
B_{g}(t,t)\neq 0
, then
\rho=0
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{g}
is simple and
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is semisimple, then
\rho=0
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is unimodular and
B_{g}(t,t)\neq 0
, then by Lemma
12.2
there is a basis
\{t,a,b\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying (
12.2
). In particular
\mathfrak{g}=\text{Span}\,\{t\}+\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
. By Lemma
11.1
this suffices to show that
\rho=0
. This shows (
1
).

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is simple and
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is semisimple.
Suppose there is
a\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
\rho(a)\neq 0
. Consider the Fitting decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{k}\right)\oplus\left(\cap_{k\geq 1}\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{k}\right)
. By Lemma
11.1
,
\cap_{k\geq 1}\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{k}\subset\ker\rho
, so it may be supposed that
a\in\sum_{k\geq 1}\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{k}
. Since
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is semisimple,
\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{2}=\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
, so
a\in\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
. Because
\mathfrak{g}
is simple,
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is not the zero endomorphism. Since
\dim\mathfrak{g}=3
,
\dim\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)=3-\text{rank\,}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
equals
1
or
2
. Were
\dim\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)=2
, then there would be a basis
\{t,u,v\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
[t,u]=0
and
[t,v]\neq 0
. In this case
[t,v]
and
[u,v]
span
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
, but that
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
be a proper ideal contradicts that
\mathfrak{g}
is simple. Hence
\dim\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)=1
, so
a
is a multiple of
t
, which contradicts
\rho(t)=0
.
∎

Remark 31
.

The nonoverlap between claims (
2
) and (
1
) of Lemma
12.3
is slight. If
\mathfrak{g}
is simple and noncompact, so is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
, then there exists
t\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=0
and
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is semismple. In this case (
2
) applies but (
1
) does not. On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{g}
is unimodular, and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
, Lemma
12.1
implies
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is semisimple, and so in this case the conclusion of (
2
) follows from (
1
).

Theorem 12.4
.

Let
G
be a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and Killing form
B_{\mathfrak{g}}\in S^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
. Given
t\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
, there is a unique left-invariant affine connection on
G
having antisymmetric Ricci tensor and constituting with the vector field
\mathbb{E}=E^{t}
a left-invariant conelike radiant structure, and it is the connection associated with
t
as in (
11.24
) of Theorem
11.4
.

Proof.

Let
t\in\mathfrak{g}
satsify
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
and let
\{t,a,b\}
be the basis of
\mathfrak{g}
given by (
12.2
) of Lemma
12.2
. Suppose
\nabla
is a left-invariant affine connection that with
E^{t}
constitutes a left-invariant conelike radiant structure. The associated tensor
\Pi
satisfies
\Pi(t,t)=t
and

(12.3)

\displaystyle\Pi(t,a)=\Pi(a,t)=a+\tfrac{1}{2}[t,a]=a+b,

\displaystyle\Pi(t,b)=\Pi(b,t)=b+\tfrac{1}{2}[t,b]=b+\tfrac{1}{8}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)a.

By (
12.1
),
2\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{3}=B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
. Combining this with (
11.24
) or the equivalent expression (
11.26
) shows that if
\nabla
is the connection of Theorem
11.4
, then

(12.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Pi(a,a)&=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(a,\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathfrak{g}}+\tfrac{2}{B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{2}\right)a\right)t=\tfrac{1}{2}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(a,a)t=-2\epsilon t,\\
\Pi(b,a)&=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(b,\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathfrak{g}}+\tfrac{2}{B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{2}\right)a\right)t=\tfrac{1}{2}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(b,a)t=0,\\
\Pi(b,b)&=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(b,\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathfrak{g}}+\tfrac{2}{B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)^{2}\right)b\right)t=\tfrac{1}{2}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(b,b)t=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\epsilon t,\end{aligned}

and the Ricci curvature of the associated left-invariant conelike radiant structure satisfies
\mathsf{ric}(a,b)=\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon
,
\mathsf{ric}(a,a)=0
,
\mathsf{ric}(b,b)=0
, and
\mathsf{ric}(t,\,\cdot\,\,)=0
.

Now suppose
\nabla
is any left-invariant affine connection that with
E^{t}
constitutes a left-invariant conelike radiant structure. It will be shown that the associated tensor
\Pi
is as in (
12.4
). By Lemma
12.3
,
\rho=0
. Write
T=\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)\in\text{End}(\mathfrak{g})
. By Lemma
5.2
, (
11.5
), and (
11.6
), there is
Q\in S^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
such that
Q(t,\,\cdot\,\,)=0
, and

(12.5)

\displaystyle Q(u,v)=Q(Tu,v)+Q(u,Tv),

\displaystyle T\Pi(u,v)=\Pi(Tu,v)+\Pi(u,Tv)+Q(u,v)t,

for
u,v\in\mathfrak{g}
. Define
n\in\mathbb{R}
by
-8n=B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
so that
[t,b]=-2na
. Substituting
a
and
b
in (
12.5
) and using (
12.2
) yields

(12.6)

\displaystyle Q(a,a)=4Q(a,b),

\displaystyle Q(b,b)=-4nQ(a,b),

(12.7)

\displaystyle T\Pi(a,a)=4\Pi(a,b)+Q(a,a)t,

\displaystyle T\Pi(b,b)=-4n\Pi(a,b)+Q(b,b)t,

(12.8)

\displaystyle T\Pi(a,b)=2\Pi(b,b)-2n\Pi(a,a)+Q(a,b)t,

Combining (
12.6
) shows
Q(a,b)=2Q(b,b)-2nQ(a,a)=-16nQ(a,b)
. Hence, if
1\neq-16n
then
Q(a,b)=0
, so, by (
12.6
),
Q
vanishes identically.

The following consequence of (
12.2
) is used several times in what follows: if
Tx\in\text{Span}\,\{t\}
then
x\in\text{Span}\,\{t\}
and
Tx=0
.
Applying
T
to (
12.8
) and using
T\Pi(b,b)=-nT\Pi(a,a)
and (
12.7
) yields

(12.9)

\displaystyle T^{2}\Pi(a,b)

\displaystyle=2T\Pi(b,b)-2nT\Pi(a,a)=4T\Pi(b,b)=-16n\Pi(a,b)+4Q(b,b)t.

Applying
T
to (
12.9
) and using (
12.1
) yields
-4nT\Pi(a,b)=T^{3}\Pi(a,b)=-16nT\Pi(a,b)
, so that
T\Pi(a,b)=0
and
\Pi(a,b)\in\text{Span}\,\{t\}
. In (
12.8
) this implies
2n\Pi(a,a)-2\Pi(b,b)=Q(a,b)t
. Because
n\Pi(a,a)+\Pi(b,b)\in\text{Span}\,\{t\}
, this yields
\Pi(a,a),\Pi(b,b)\in\text{Span}\,\{t\}
, so there are
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\Pi(a,a)=\alpha t
and
\Pi(b,b)=\beta t
and, hence,
Q(a,b)=2n\alpha-2\beta
. With (
12.7
), this yields

(12.10)

\displaystyle\Pi(a,b)=-\tfrac{1}{4}Q(a,a)t=\tfrac{1}{4n}Q(b,b)t=-Q(a,b)t=(2\beta-2n\alpha)t.

There follow
\mathsf{r}(a,b)a=(-\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon+2\beta-2n\alpha)a-(\alpha+2\epsilon)b
, and
\mathsf{r}(a,b)b=(\beta+2n\epsilon)a+(-\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon-2\beta+2n\alpha)b
, from which it follows that

(12.11)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ric}(a,a)=\alpha+2\epsilon,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ric}(b,b)=\beta+2n\epsilon,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ric}(a,b)=\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon+2\beta-2n\alpha,

\displaystyle\mathsf{ric}(b,a)=-\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon+2\beta-2n\alpha.

For
\nabla
to have antisymmetric Ricci tensor it must be
\alpha=-2\epsilon
,
\beta=-2n\epsilon
, and
\beta=n\alpha
. Hence
Q(a,b)=2n\alpha-2\beta=0
, and, by (
12.6
), this implies that
Q
is identically zero.
This shows that
Q
is identically zero in all cases. By (
12.10
),
\Pi(a,b)=0
, and by the preceding,
\Pi(b,b)=\beta t=-2n\epsilon t
and
\Pi(a,a)=\alpha t=-2\epsilon t
, so that
\Pi
is determined completely and uniquely in terms of
n
and
\epsilon
alone. Comparing with (
12.4
) shows that the unique connection
\Pi
found here is the connection associated with
t
as in (
11.24
) of Theorem
11.4
.
∎

Corollary 12.5
.

Let
G
be a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and Killing form
B_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let
t\in\mathfrak{g}
be such that
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
and let
\{t,a,b\}
be a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying
[a,b]=\epsilon t
,
[t,a]=2b
,
[t,b]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)a
where
\epsilon\in\{0,1,-1\}
if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
and
\epsilon\in\{0,-1\}
if
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)>0
. Let
A=E^{a}
,
B=E^{b}
, and
T=E^{r}=\mathbb{E}
be the corresponding left-invariant frame on
G
and let
\{\alpha,\beta,\tau\}
be the left-invariant coframe dual to
\{A,B,T\}
.

The unique left-invariant affine connection
\nabla
on
G
having antisymmetric Ricci tensor and constituting with the vector field
\mathbb{E}=E^{t}
a left-invariant conelike radiant structure given by Theorem
12.4
has the form

(12.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\nabla_{A}T=A,&&\nabla_{B}T=B,&&\nabla_{T}T=T,\\
&\nabla_{A}A=-2\epsilon T,&&\nabla_{B}A=-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon T,&&\nabla_{T}A=A+2B,\\
&\nabla_{A}B=\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon T,&&\nabla_{B}B=\tfrac{1}{4}\epsilon B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)T,&&\nabla_{T}B=B+\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)A.\end{aligned}

Equivalently:

(12.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla\alpha-\tfrac{1}{2}d\alpha&=-(\alpha\otimes\tau+\tau\otimes\alpha)-\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)(\beta\otimes\tau+\tau\otimes\beta),\\
\nabla\beta-\tfrac{1}{2}d\beta&=-(\alpha\otimes\tau+\tau\otimes\alpha)-(\beta\otimes\tau+\tau\otimes\beta),\\
\nabla\tau-\tfrac{1}{2}d\tau&=-2\epsilon\alpha\otimes\alpha-\tfrac{1}{4}\epsilon B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\beta\otimes\beta-\tau\otimes\tau.\end{split}

The curvature and Ricci curvature of
\nabla
are

(12.14)

\displaystyle R(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon\alpha\wedge\beta\otimes(\alpha\otimes A+\beta\otimes B),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ric}(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon\alpha\wedge\beta=-\tfrac{3}{2}d\tau.

The connection
\nabla
is not projectively flat.

Proof.

That there is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
of the form claimed follow from Lemma
12.2
. That the connection
\nabla
has the form (
12.12
) follows from the proof of Theorem
12.4
. The identities (
12.13
) follow from (
12.12
) and
d\alpha=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\beta\wedge\tau
,
d\beta=-2\tau\wedge\alpha
, and
d\tau=-\epsilon\alpha\wedge\beta
.
The identities

(12.15)

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}(a,b)a=-\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}(a,b)b=-\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon,

and
\mathsf{ric}(a,b)=\tfrac{3}{2}\epsilon
express all nontrivial parts of the curvature tensor, and (
12.14
) follows.
The connection
\nabla
is not projectively flat. Its projective Schouten tensor is the antisymmetric tensor
P=-\tfrac{1}{4}\mathsf{Ric}=\tfrac{3}{8}\epsilon\alpha\wedge\beta=-\tfrac{3}{8}d\tau
. The projective Weyl tensor
W(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)
does not vanish, for
W(T,A)B=\tfrac{3}{8}T
.
∎

By Lemma
12.1
and Theorem
12.4
, with a semisimple element
t\in\mathfrak{g}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of a three-dimensional unimodular Lie group
G
there is associated a unique left-invariant conelike radiant connection having antisymmetric Ricci tensor, and the connections associated with elements of the same semisimple adjoint orbit in
\mathfrak{g}
are isomorphic.
Corollary
12.5
has the following slightly stronger consequence: if there is an automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
mapping one semisimple adjoint orbit to another then the associated connections are isomorphic. This occurs for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
, for which
\Aut(\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R}))=PGL(2,\mathbb{R})
and there is a nontrivial outer automorphism interchanging the two adjoint orbits with the same negative value for
B_{g}(t,t)
.

To describe the resulting connections more explicitly, it is necessary to describe the three-dimensional unimodular Lie groups and their adjoint orbits in a more explicit manner. To do so, it is convenient to give a uniform description of the nonabelian unimodular Lie groups based on a slight generalization of real quaternion algebras. Let
\mathbb{k}^{\ast}
be the group of invertible elements of a field
\mathbb{k}
with
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{k}\neq 2
.
For
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{k}
define
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
to be the
4
-dimensional algebra generated by
1
,
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, and
e_{3}
such that
1
is a multiplicative identity element, and

(12.16)

\displaystyle e_{1}^{2}=\alpha_{1}1,

\displaystyle e_{2}^{2}=\alpha_{2}1,

\displaystyle e_{1}e_{2}=-e_{2}e_{1}=e_{3}.

The relations (
12.16
) imply

(12.17)

\displaystyle e_{3}^{2}=-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},

\displaystyle e_{3}e_{1}=-e_{1}e_{3}=-\alpha_{1}e_{2},

\displaystyle e_{2}e_{3}=-e_{3}e_{2}=-\alpha_{2}e_{1}.

Although ugly, the notation
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is standard.
When
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{k}^{\ast}
, then
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is a
quaternion algebra
. For background on quaternion algebras see
[
103
, Chapter
2
]
or
[
93
, Chapter
3
]
. Here it is convenient to allow
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
to take the value
0
.
The algebra
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is isomorphic to the Clifford algebra
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k}^{2},Q)
of the quadratic form
Q(x_{1},x_{2})=\alpha_{1}x_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}x_{2}^{2}
on
\mathbb{k}^{2}
. This identification gives
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
the additional structure of a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebra, with
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}_{+}=\text{Span}\,\{1,e_{3}\}
and
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}_{-}=\text{Span}\,\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
. As a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebra,
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}\simeq\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k},\alpha_{1}|x|^{2})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k},\alpha_{2}|x|^{2})
where
\widehat{\otimes}
is the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded tensor product of
\mathbb{k}
-superalgebras. This is the most convenient way to think about
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
in the degenerate cases where
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=0
. The
dual numbers
(over
\mathbb{k}
) are
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k},0)
, the paracomplex numbers are
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{R},x^{2})
, and the complex numbers are
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{R},-x^{2})
. These are all
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebras, and their
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded tensor products with
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{R},0)
yield the Clifford algebras of possibly degenerate quadratic forms on
\mathbb{k}^{2}
.

It follows straightforwardly from (
12.17
) that the permutations of
\{1,2,3\}
generate isomorphisms of
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
with the algebras
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{2},-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
,
{\left(\frac{-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
, and
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
. The linear map sending
1
to
1
and rescaling
e_{i}
by
t_{i}\in\mathbb{k}^{\ast}
,
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, is an isomorphism
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}\simeq{\left(\frac{t_{1}^{2}\alpha_{1},t_{2}^{2}\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
.

The case of most interest here is
\mathbb{k}=\mathbb{R}
. In this case the preceding remarks show that
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
is isomorphic to one of
{\left(\frac{-1,-1}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
,
{\left(\frac{1,1}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
,
{\left(\frac{1,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
,
{\left(\frac{-1,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
, and
{\left(\frac{0,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
.

For
q\in{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
write
q=q^{0}1+q^{1}e_{1}+q^{2}e_{2}+q^{3}e_{3}
. The matrices with respect to the basis
\{e_{0}=1,e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
of the multiplication endomorphisms
L,R:{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}\to\text{End}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
defined by
L(p)q=pq=R(q)p
are

(12.18)

\displaystyle L(q)=\begin{pmatrix}q^{0}&\alpha_{1}q^{1}&\alpha_{2}q^{2}&-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}q^{3}\\
q^{1}&q^{0}&\alpha_{2}q^{3}&-\alpha_{2}q^{2}\\
q^{2}&-\alpha_{1}q^{3}&q^{0}&\alpha_{1}q^{1}\\
q^{3}&-q^{2}&q^{1}&q^{0}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle R(q)=\begin{pmatrix}q^{0}&\alpha_{1}q^{1}&\alpha_{2}q^{2}&-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}q^{3}\\
q^{1}&q^{0}&-\alpha_{2}q^{3}&\alpha_{2}q^{2}\\
q^{2}&\alpha_{1}q^{3}&q^{0}&-\alpha_{1}q^{1}\\
q^{3}&q^{2}&-q^{1}&q^{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\operatorname{Re}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}=\text{Span}\,\{1\}
and
\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}=\text{Span}\,\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
.
For
q\in{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
define the
conjugate

\bar{q}\in{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
to be the image of
q
under the
\mathbb{k}
-linear reflection of
\mathbb{A}
along
\operatorname{Re}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
and through
\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
, so
\bar{q}=q^{0}1-q^{1}e_{1}-q^{2}e_{2}-q^{3}e_{3}
. Alternatively, the conjugation is simply the canonical antiautomorphism of the Clifford algebra
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k}^{2},Q)
.
Define the
(reduced) trace

\mathsf{tr}\,q=q+\bar{q}=2q^{0}
and
(reduced) norm

n(q)=q\bar{q}=(q^{0})^{2}-\alpha_{1}(q^{1})^{2}-\alpha_{2}(q^{2})^{2}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}(q^{3})^{2}
.
On
\mathscr{M}_{2}(\mathbb{k})
these recover the usual trace and determinant and the conjugate is the adjugate.
Note that
n
is nondegenerate if and only if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{k}^{\ast}
. Using (
12.18
), it can be checked that
\mathsf{tr}\,L(q)=2\mathsf{tr}\,q=\mathsf{tr}\,R(q)
and
\det L(q)=n(q)^{2}=\det R(q)
.

The algebra
{\left(\frac{1,1}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is the algebra
\mathscr{M}_{2}(\mathbb{k})
of
2\times 2
matrices over
\mathbb{k}
. A quaternion algebra isomorphic to
\mathscr{M}_{2}(\mathbb{k})
is said to be
split
.
Since every element of an algebraically closed field is a square, the isomorphism
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}\simeq{\left(\frac{t_{1}^{2}\alpha_{1},t_{2}^{2}\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
implies that every quaternion algebra over an algebraically closed field
\mathbb{k}
is isomorphic to
\mathscr{M}_{2}(\mathbb{k})
.
In general there holds
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}\otimes_{\mathbb{k}}{\bar{\mathbb{k}}}={\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\bar{\mathbb{k}}}\right)}\simeq\mathscr{M}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{k}})
where
\bar{\mathbb{k}}
is an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{k}
, and since
\mathscr{M}_{2}(\bar{\mathbb{k}})
is central simple, it follows that
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is central simple when
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{k}^{\ast}
. It follows from the Artin-Wedderburn theorem that an algebra over a field
\mathbb{k}
of characteristic not equal to
2
is a
4
-dimensional central simple algebra if and only if it is a quaternion algebra; moreover, any such algebra is either split or is a division ring.

One or both of these claims fail when
\alpha_{2}=0
. The center
\mathfrak{Z}({\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)})
of
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is
\ker(L-R)
, while a
2
-sided ideal of
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
is a subspace invariant with respect to
L(q)
and
R(q)
for all
q\in{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}
. It is standard that the Jacobson radical
\operatorname{rad}\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k}^{2},Q)
of
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k}^{2},Q)
is the ideal generated by the radical of the bilinear form associated with
Q
via polarization. Let
g\in S^{2}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{k}}\right)}^{\ast}
be the
\mathbb{k}
-bilinear form obtained by polarizing
n
, so that
g(q,q)=n(q)
and
2g(p,q)=p\bar{q}+q\bar{p}=\mathsf{tr}\,(p\bar{q})
.
From (
12.18
) it follows that if
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{k}^{\ast}
, then the center
\mathfrak{Z}({\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},0}{\mathbb{k}}\right)})
is still
\text{Span}\,\{1\}
but the Jacobson radical
\operatorname{rad}\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k}^{2},Q)=\text{Span}\,\{e_{1},e_{2}\}=\operatorname{rad}g
is nontrivial. Similarly,
\mathfrak{Z}({\left(\frac{0,0}{\mathbb{k}}\right)})=\text{Span}\,\{1,e_{3}\}
and
\operatorname{rad}\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{k}^{2},Q)=\text{Span}\,\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}=\operatorname{rad}g
is nontrivial.

The first part of Lemma
12.6
is an observation of P. Meyer
[
112
]
. Although it makes sense over general fields, here it is needed only over
\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 12.6
.

For a
3
-dimensional unimodular nonabelian real Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathbb{R}
equipped with the multiplication
\circ
defined for
(x,r),(y,s)\in\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
by

(12.19)

\displaystyle(x,r)\circ(y,s)=\left(\tfrac{1}{2}[x,y]+ry+sx,rs+\tfrac{1}{8}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(x,y)\right),

is isomorphic to
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
with
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\{(1,1),(-1,-1),(1,0),(-1,0),(0,0)\}
. The canonical antiautomorphism of
{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
is given on
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
by
(x,r)\to(-x,r)
and its
-1
eigenspace
\mathfrak{g}\oplus\{0\}=\{(x,0)\in\hat{\mathfrak{g}}:x\in\mathfrak{g}\}
equipped with the product
[(x,0),(y,0)]=(x,0)\circ(y,0)-(y,0)\circ(x,0)
is a Lie algebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}
.

More precisely, if
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent then
(\hat{\mathfrak{g}},\circ)
is isomorphic to
{\left(\frac{0,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
, while otherwise each
t\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
determines an isomorphism
\Phi_{t}:\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
where
\alpha_{1}
is
0
or
\operatorname{sgn}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
as
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
is or is not
0
and
\alpha_{2}
is
-1
,
1
, or
0
as the restriction of
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
to
\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
is negative definite, split, or degenerate.

If
\Psi:\mathfrak{g}\to\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
is an isomorphism of
3
-dimensional unimodular nonabelian real Lie algebras then
\hat{\Psi}:\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\to\hat{\bar{g}}
defined by
\hat{\Psi}(x,r)=(\Psi(x),r)
is an isomorphism of the associated algebras, and every such isomorphism arises in this way. As a consequence a
3
-dimensional unimodular nonabelian real Lie algebra is determined up to isomorphism by the associated quadratic space
(\mathfrak{g},B_{\mathfrak{g}})
.

Proof.

Let
\{t,a,b\}
be a basis as in (
12.2
) of Lemma
12.2
and let
\kappa=\sqrt{|B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)|/8}
. The
\circ
products of the elements

e_{0}=(0,1)
,
e_{1}=(\kappa t,0)
,
e_{2}=(\sqrt{2}a,0)
, and
e_{3}=(\sqrt{2}\kappa b,0)
of
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfy the relations (
12.16
) with
\alpha_{1}=\operatorname{sgn}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
and
\alpha_{2}=-\epsilon
. The remaining claims follow via straightforward computations.
∎

Henceforth let
\mathbb{k}=\mathbb{R}
and write
\mathbb{A}={\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
.
It follows from the definitions that
\overline{pq}=\bar{q}\bar{p}
for
p,q\in\mathbb{A}
, so
n(pq)=n(p)n(q)
. Hence the set of units
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
in
\mathbb{A}
is a group. From
\det L(q)=n(q)^{2}
it follows that
\mathbb{A}^{\times}=\{q\in\mathbb{A}:n(q)\neq 0\}
. Factoring by the dilation action of the positive real numbers yields the three-dimensional Lie subgroup
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})=\{q\in\mathbb{A}:n(q)=1\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{\times}
that is connected if
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0
or
\alpha_{1}<0
and has two connected components if
\alpha_{2}=0
and
\alpha_{1}\geq 0
. The image
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
of
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
in the projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{A})
is a connected Lie group.

For
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathsf{C}_{\kappa}(t)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\tfrac{(-\kappa)^{j}}{(2j)!}t^{2j}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\kappa}(t)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\tfrac{(-\kappa)^{j}}{(2j+1)!}t^{2j+1}
be the solutions of
\ddot{x}+\kappa x=0
with the initial conditions
x(0)=1
,
\dot{x}(0)=0
, and
x(0)=0
,
\dot{x}(0)=1
, respectively.
Note that
\mathsf{C}_{\kappa}^{2}+\kappa\mathsf{S}_{\kappa}^{2}=1
. For
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathsf{C}_{c^{2}\kappa}(r)=\mathsf{C}_{\kappa}(cr)
and
\mathsf{S}_{c^{2}\kappa}(r)=c^{-1}\mathsf{S}_{\kappa}(cr)
, from which it follows that
2\mathsf{C}_{\kappa}(r)\mathsf{S}_{\kappa}(r)=\mathsf{S}_{\kappa}(2r)
and
\mathsf{C}_{\kappa}^{2}(r)-\kappa\mathsf{S}_{\kappa}^{2}(r)=\mathsf{C}_{\kappa}(2r)
.
For a three-dimensional real Lie algebra,
\mathfrak{g}
, define a quadratic form
n
on
\mathfrak{g}
by
-8n(u)=B_{\mathfrak{g}}(u,u)
for
u\in\mathfrak{g}
.
From (
12.1
) there follows

(12.20)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}(\exp_{\mathfrak{g}}(ru))=e^{r\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(u)}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathfrak{g}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{S}_{n(u)}(2r)\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(u)+\tfrac{1}{4}\tfrac{1-\mathsf{C}_{n(u)}(2r)}{n(u)}\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(u)^{2},

for
u\in\mathfrak{g}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Formula (
12.20
) generalizes a version of the usual Euler-Rodrigues formula for
SO(3)
.

The Lie algebra of
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\subset\mathbb{A}^{\times}
is
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}\subset\mathbb{A}
with the bracket
[r,s]=rs-sr
and exponential map
\exp:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{A}^{\times}
given by the usual exponential,
e^{r}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\tfrac{1}{k!}r^{k}
.
For any
w\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, there holds the generalized Euler formula

(12.21)

\displaystyle e^{tw}=\mathsf{C}_{n(w)}(t)+\mathsf{S}_{n(w)}(t)w.

For
r\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
, the vector field
E^{r}_{q}=\tfrac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}R(\exp(tr))q=R(r)q
is left-invariant with respect to the action of
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
.
In particular,
E_{i}=E^{e_{i}}
,
1\leq i\leq 3
, satisfy

(12.22)

\displaystyle[E_{1},E_{2}]=2E_{3},

\displaystyle[E_{2},E_{3}]=-2\alpha_{2}E_{1},

\displaystyle[E_{3},E_{1}]=-2\alpha_{1}E_{2}.

The adjoint representation is the homomomorphism
\operatorname{Ad}:\mathbb{A}^{\times}\to\Aut(\mathbb{A})
defined by
\operatorname{Ad}(q)(x)=qxq^{-1}
for
q\in\mathbb{A}^{\times}
and
x\in\mathbb{A}
. Note that
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{A}^{\times})
preserves the Lie bracket
[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,]
, the norm
n
, and the vector space direct sum
\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{A}\oplus\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. The restriction of
\operatorname{Ad}
to
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
is the usual adjoint representation of
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
.
By (
12.22
), for
w\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
,

(12.23)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w)=\begin{pmatrix}0&2\alpha_{2}w^{3}&-2\alpha_{2}w^{2}\\
-2\alpha_{1}w^{3}&0&2\alpha_{1}w^{1}\\
-2w^{2}&2w^{1}&0\end{pmatrix}.

It follows from (
12.23
) or (
12.1
) that

(12.24)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w)^{3}=-4n(w)\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w).

The matrix of the restriction of
\operatorname{Ad}(q)=n(q)^{-1}L(q)R(\bar{q})
to
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
with respect to the basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
can be computed from (
12.18
), and, using (
12.23
) and (
12.24
), there results

(12.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\resizebox{22655705}{}{$\begin{pmatrix}(q^{0})^{2}-\alpha_{1}(q^{1})^{2}+\alpha_{2}(q^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}(q^{3})^{2}&2\alpha_{2}(q^{0}q^{3}-q^{1}q^{2})&-2\alpha_{2}(q^{0}q^{2}-\alpha_{1}q^{1}q^{3})\\
-2\alpha_{1}(q^{0}q^{3}+q^{1}q^{2})&(q^{0})^{2}+\alpha_{1}(q^{1})^{2}-\alpha_{2}(q^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}(q^{3})^{2}&2\alpha_{1}(q^{0}q^{2}+\alpha_{2}q^{2}q^{3})\\
-2(q^{0}q^{2}+\alpha_{1}q^{1}q^{3})&2(q^{0}q^{1}-\alpha_{2}q^{2}q^{3})&(q^{0})^{2}+\alpha_{1}(q^{1})^{2}+\alpha_{2}(q^{2})^{2}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}(q^{3})^{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}$}\\
&=n(q)\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}+(\operatorname{Re}q)\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(\operatorname{Im}q)+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(\operatorname{Im}q)^{2},\end{split}

where
q=\operatorname{Re}q+\operatorname{Im}q
with
\operatorname{Re}q=q^{0}
and
\operatorname{Im}q=q^{1}e_{1}+q^{2}e_{2}+q^{3}e_{3}\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
.
From (
12.24
), (
12.25
) (or (
12.20
)) and (
12.21
) there follows

(12.26)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}(\exp_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(tw))=e^{t\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w)}=\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{S}_{n(w)}(2t)\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w)+\tfrac{1}{4}\tfrac{1-\mathsf{C}_{n(w)}(2t)}{n(w)}\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w)^{2}=\operatorname{Ad}(e^{tw}),

for
w\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Formula (
12.26
) generalizes a version of the usual Euler-Rodrigues formula for
SO(3)
.

If
\operatorname{Ad}(q)=\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}
for
q\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
, then
qr=rq
for all
r\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
, and so
q\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{A})
. If
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
or
\alpha_{2}\neq 0
, then
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{A})=\{\pm 1\}
, and the map
\operatorname{Ad}:\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\to\text{End}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})
is a twofold covering of its image, so descends to a group isomorphism from
\mathbb{P}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
onto its image. If
\alpha_{1}=0=\alpha_{2}
, then
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\mathbb{A})=\{\pm 1+te_{3}:t\in\mathbb{R}\}
, and
\theta:\mathbb{P}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\to\text{End}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})
is a line bundle over its image, which is the group of translations of
\text{Span}\,\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
.

The differentials of covectors of the left-invariant coframe
\{\epsilon^{1},\epsilon^{2},\epsilon^{3}\}
on
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
dual to the left-invariant frame
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
of (
12.22
) satisfy

(12.27)

\displaystyle d\epsilon^{1}=2\alpha_{2}\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3},

\displaystyle d\epsilon^{2}=2\alpha_{1}\epsilon^{3}\wedge\epsilon^{1},

\displaystyle d\epsilon_{3}=-2\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}.

From (
12.27
) there follow

•

The volume form
\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}
is biinvariant, reflecting that
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
is a unimodular Lie group.

•

There is a biinvariant one-form
\omega
on
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
if and only if
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}=0
, in which case
\omega=\omega_{2}\epsilon^{2}+\omega_{3}\epsilon^{3}
with
\alpha_{1}\omega_{2}=0
and
\alpha_{2}\omega_{3}=0
.

The restriction to
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
of the
\mathbb{k}
-bilinear form
g
obtained by polarizing
n
can be viewed as defining a left-invariant tensor on
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
, also denoted
g
and satisfying
g(E_{q}^{r},E_{q}^{s})=g(r,s)
for
r,s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. Moreover, because
g([r,s],t)=g(r,[s,t])
for
r,s,t\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
, the restriction to
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
of
g
determines a biinvariant tensor on
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
.

Lemma 12.7
.

If
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are not both zero, a biinvariant symmetric bilinear form
\beta
on
(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,])
is unique up to homothety, equal to a multiple of the Killing form of
(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,])
, which is
-8n(q)
. Moreover,
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
preserves
\beta
, so that
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))\subset SO(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},g)
if
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0
. If
\alpha_{1}=0=\alpha_{2}
, any symmetric bilinear form on
\text{Span}\,\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
is biinvariant.

Proof.

It follows from (
12.23
) that the Killing form of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
is
B_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w,w)=\mathsf{tr}\,\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(w)^{2}=-8n(w)
.

A symmetric bilinear form
\beta\in S^{2}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})^{\ast}
determines a left-invariant tensor on
\mathbb{S}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
. This tensor is biinvariant if and only if
\beta([x,y],z)=\beta(x,[y,z])
for all
x,y,z\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. In particular
\beta([x,y],y)=\beta(x,[y,y])=0
for all
x,y\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. If
i\neq j
, then
\beta([e_{i},e_{j}],e_{j})=0
, which yields the equations

(12.28)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\beta(e_{1},e_{2})=0,

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}\beta(e_{1},e_{2})=0,

\displaystyle\beta(e_{1},e_{3})=0,

\displaystyle\beta(e_{2},e_{3})=0,

and
\beta([e_{1},e_{2}],e_{3})=\beta([e_{2},e_{3}],e_{1})=\beta([e_{3},e_{1}],e_{2})
, which, by (
12.22
), imply that
\beta
has the form

(12.29)

\displaystyle\beta=b_{1}\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{1}+b_{2}\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{2}+b_{3}\epsilon^{3}\otimes\epsilon^{3}+c(\epsilon^{1}\otimes e^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{1})

where
b_{3}=-\alpha_{2}b_{1}=-\alpha_{1}b_{2}
, and
al_{1}c=0=\alpha_{2}c
. If
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are not both
0
, these equations have a unique solution up to homothety, which must be multiple of
B_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}
.
Since
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
acts as automorphisms of the Lie algebra
(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,])
, the uniqueness implies that it preserves
\beta
up to multiplication by a nonzero constant. Since
\det\operatorname{Ad}(q)=\det L(q)\det R(\bar{q})=n(q)^{2}n(\bar{q})^{2}=1
and
\dim\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
is odd, the constant must be
1
, so
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
preserves
\beta
. In particular, if
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0
,
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))\subset SO(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},g)
.

Finally, if
\alpha_{1}=0=\alpha_{2}
, then
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
, and
c
can be chosen arbitrarily.
∎

Up to isomorphism there are five nonabelian three-dimensional unimodular real Lie algebras
[
13
,
113
]
:
\mathfrak{so}(3)\simeq\mathfrak{su}(2)\simeq\mathfrak{sp}(1)
,
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathfrak{sp}(1,\mathbb{R})
,
\mathfrak{e}(2)
,
\mathfrak{e}(1,1)
, and the Heisenberg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{heis}_{3}
. They are represented by (
12.22
) for appropriate choices of parameters
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
:
(-1,-1)
for
\mathfrak{so}(3)\simeq\mathfrak{su}(2)\simeq\mathfrak{sp}(1)
,
(1,1)
for
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathfrak{sp}(1,\mathbb{R})
,
(0,-1)
, for
\mathfrak{e}(2)
,
(0,1)
for
\mathfrak{e}(1,1)
, and
(0,0)
for
\mathfrak{heis}_{3}
. Here
E(2)
and
E(1,1)
denote the groups of motions of a
2
-dimensional Euclidean vector space and a
2
-dimensional Lorenztian vector space, and
\mathfrak{e}(2)
and
\mathfrak{e}(1,1)
are their Lie algebras.

Although the Lie algebra
(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,])
has been identified, there remains to identify the Lie group
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
. In the cases where
\alpha_{2}=0
and
\alpha_{1}\geq 0
,
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
has two connected components and
\mathbb{S}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
denotes the connected component of the identity.

When
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(-1,-1)
,
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
comprises the unit norm quaternions, so is
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, while when
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,1)
,
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
comprises the unit determinant elements of
\mathscr{M}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so is
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
. In either case,
\operatorname{Ad}
is a nontrivial homomorphism from the connected Lie group
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
to
SO(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},g)
which is a twofold covering of its image. Since the image is connected it is a connected component of
SO(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},g)
. In the case
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,1)
, this means the image is the connected component of the identity,
SO_{0}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},g)
. In summary, when
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(-1,-1)
,
\operatorname{Ad}
is the twofold cover
Sp(1)\to SO(3)
, while when
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,1)
,
\operatorname{Ad}
is the twofold cover
SL(2,\mathbb{R})\to SO_{0}(1,2)
.

It is claimed that in the cases
(-1,0)
and
(1,0)
,
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
is isomorphic via
\operatorname{Ad}
to the connected component of the identity of the special Euclidean group acting on the algebraically trivial ideal
\operatorname{rad}\mathbb{A}
preserving the metric
(q^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{1}(q^{3})^{2}
. Suppose
\beta\in S^{2}(\operatorname{rad}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})^{\ast}
is
(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,])
invariant. The invariance implies
2\beta(e_{3},e_{3})=\beta([e_{1},e_{2}],e_{3})=-\beta(e_{2},[e_{1},e_{3}])=-2\alpha_{1}\beta(e_{2},e_{2})
and
2\beta(e_{2},e_{3})=\beta(e_{2},[e_{1},e_{2}])=\beta(e_{1},[e_{2},e_{2}])=0
, so that
\beta
has the form
B((q^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{1}(q^{3})^{2})
for some
B\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
acts on
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
by algebra automorphisms, it preserves
\beta=(q^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{1}(q^{3})^{2}
up to a constant factor. The constant factor is
1
. For
x\in\operatorname{rad}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
and
q\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
calculating using (
12.24
) and (
12.25
) yields
\beta(\operatorname{Ad}(q)x,\operatorname{Ad}(q)x)=n(q)^{2}\beta(x,x)=\beta(x,x)
.
It is claimed that
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
is the connected component of the identity of the Euclidean group of motions of
(\operatorname{rad}\mathbb{A},\beta)
, that is
SE(2)
or
SE_{0}(1,1)
, as
\alpha_{1}=-1
or
\alpha_{1}=1
.
Define
\phi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
by

(12.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi(&t,w_{1},w_{2})=(1-\tfrac{w_{2}}{2}e_{2}-\tfrac{w_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}}e_{3})e^{te_{1}}=(1-\tfrac{w_{2}}{2}e_{2}-\tfrac{w_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}}e_{3})(\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)+\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)e_{1})\\
&=\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)+\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)e_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(w_{1}\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)-w_{2}\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)\right)e_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(w_{2}\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)-\tfrac{w_{1}}{\alpha_{1}}\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(t)\right)e_{3}.\end{split}

(Some version of the parametrization (
12.30
) is well known in applied kinematics; see e.g.
[
122
, Chapter
9
]
.) It is apparent that
\phi
maps
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
onto
\mathbb{S}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
. If
\phi(t,w_{1},w_{2})=\phi(s,x_{1},x_{2})
, then
e^{(s-t)e_{1}}=(1+\tfrac{x_{2}-w_{2}}{2}e_{2}+\tfrac{x_{1}-w_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}}e_{3})
, which yields
x_{1}=w_{1}
,
x_{2}=w_{2}
, and
e^{(s-t)e_{1}}=0
, so, when
\alpha_{1}=1
,
\phi
is a diffeomorphism onto
\mathbb{S}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
, while when
\alpha_{1}=-1
,
\phi
descends to a diffeomorphism
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
. A straightforward calculation combining (
12.25
) and (
12.30
) shows that, for
q=\phi(t,w_{1},w_{2})
, the matrix of
\operatorname{Ad}(\phi(t,w_{1},w_{2}))
with respect to the ordered basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
of
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
is

(12.31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
-2\alpha_{1}(q^{0}q^{3}+q^{1}q^{2})&(q^{0})^{2}+\alpha_{1}(q^{1})^{2}&2\alpha_{1}q^{0}q^{2}\\
-2(q^{0}q^{2}+\alpha_{1}q^{1}q^{3})&2q^{0}q^{1}&(q^{0})^{2}+\alpha_{1}(q^{1})^{2}+\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
w_{1}&\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)&\alpha_{1}\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)\\
w_{2}&\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)&\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)\\
\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

so that the action of
\operatorname{Ad}(\phi(t,w_{1},w_{2}))
on
\operatorname{rad}\mathbb{A}=\text{Span}\,\{e_{2},e_{3}\}
is given by the affine map

(12.32)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\to\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)&\alpha_{1}\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)\\
\mathsf{S}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)&\mathsf{C}_{-\alpha_{1}}(2t)\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

This shows that
\operatorname{Ad}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
is the connected component of the identity of the special Euclidean group acting on
\operatorname{rad}\mathbb{A}
preserving the metric
(q^{2})^{2}-\alpha_{1}(q^{3})^{2}
.

In the case
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(0,0)
,
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})=\{\pm 1+q^{1}e_{1}+q^{2}e_{2}+q^{3}e_{3}\}
has two connected components and it is simpler to consider
\mathbb{S}_{0}(\mathbb{A})=\{1+q^{1}e_{1}+q^{2}e_{2}+q^{3}e_{3}\}
. Because the Lie algebra
(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,])
is
2
-step nilpotent with one-dimensional center
\text{Span}\,\{e_{3}\}
, by Lemma
11.5
it is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{heis}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
The Lie algebra exponential map
\exp:\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{S}_{0}(\mathbb{A})
is the diffeomorphism given by
\exp(q)=\gamma(1)=1+q
, where
\gamma(t)=e^{tq}
is the one-parameter subgroup such that
\gamma(0)=1
and
\dot{\gamma}(0)=q\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
.

For
c\in\mathbb{R}
, define
\mathbb{J}_{c}(\mathbb{A})=\{q\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:n(q)=c\}=\{q\in\mathbb{A}:\bar{q}=-q,q^{2}=-c\}
.
Note that
\mathbb{J}(\mathbb{A})
can have at most two connected components.
Each
r\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
determines a map
\Psi_{r}:\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathbb{J}_{n(r)}(\mathbb{A})
defined by
\Psi_{r}(q)=\operatorname{Ad}(q)r
. The image
\Psi_{r}(\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
is the adjoint orbit of
r\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
.
By definition
\Psi_{r}(qe^{tr})=\Psi_{r}(q)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, so the one-parameter subgroup
qe^{tr}
of
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
is contained in the fiber
\Psi_{r}^{-1}(\Psi_{r}(q))
.
For
w\in T_{q}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
the differential
T\Psi_{r}(q)w=[w\bar{q},\Psi_{r}(q)]=\operatorname{Ad}(q)([\bar{q}w,r])
, where it is used that
w\bar{q}=-q\bar{w}
is the condition defining
T_{q}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
. Equivalently
T_{q}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})=L(q)\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
, so every element to
T_{q}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
has the form
w=qv
for some
v\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. It follows that
T\Psi_{r}(q)w=0
if and only if
0=[\bar{q}w,r]=[v,r]
. Hence
\ker T\Psi_{r}=L(q)\ker\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(r)
.
In particular,
qr\in\ker T\Psi_{r}(q)
.
By (
12.24
), the characteristic polynomial of
\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(r)
is
t(t^{2}+4n(r))
, so
\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(r)
has rank
2
if and only if
n(r)\neq 0
. In this case
\ker T\Psi_{r}=\text{Span}\,\{qr\}
and the adjoint orbit
\Psi_{r}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})
is a two-dimensional smooth submanifold equal to the connected component of
\mathbb{J}_{n(r)}(\mathbb{A})
containing
r
. In general the dimension of the adjoint orbit
\Psi_{r}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})
is
\text{rank\,}\operatorname{ad}_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}}(r)

For
n(r)\neq 0
the map
\Psi_{r}\to\Psi_{r}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A})
is a version of the Hopf fibration.

When
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(-1,-1)
the adjoint orbits with positive
n(r)
are the spheres
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:n(s)=n(r)\}
centered on the origin in
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
and the only adjoint orbit with
n(r)=0
is the origin.
When
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,1)
there are three kinds of adjoint orbits. Those with
n(r)<0
are the
1
-sheeted hyperboloids
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:n(s)=n(r)\}
centered on the axis spanned by
e_{3}
. Those with
n(r)>0
are the two connected components of the
2
-sheeted hyperboloids
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:n(s)=n(r)\}
. There are three adjoint orbits with
n(r)=0
, the two connected components of the complement of the origin in
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:n(s)=0\}
and the origin itself.

When
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(-1,0)
, the adjoint orbits with
n(r)>0
are the two planes
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:s^{1}=\pm\sqrt{n(r)}\}
, while the adjoint orbits with
n(r)=0
are the origin and the circles centered on the origin in the plane
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:s^{1}=0\}
.
When
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(1,0)
, the adjoint orbits with
n(r)<0
are the two planes
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:s^{1}=\pm\sqrt{-n(r)}\}
, while the adjoint orbits with
n(r)=0
are the origin and the connected components of the level sets of
(q^{2})^{2}-(q^{3})^{2}
in the plane
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:s^{1}=0\}
.
When
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(0,0)
, the adjoint orbits planes
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:s^{1}=c\}
for
c\neq 0
and the individual points of the plane
\{s\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:s^{1}=0\}
.

Two pairs
(\mathfrak{g},t)
and
(\bar{\mathfrak{g}},\bar{t})
where
t\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\bar{r}\in\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
are isomorphic if there is a Lie algebra isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}\to\bar{\mathfrak{g}}
that sends
t\to\bar{t}
. The connections associated by Theorem
12.4
with two adjoint orbits in
\mathfrak{g}
are isomorphic if there is an automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
mapping one adjoint orbit to the other. Consequently, Theorem
12.4
yields a bijection between nontrivial
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
orbits in
\mathfrak{g}
and the isomorphism classes of left-invariant conelike radiant structures on a three-dimensional unimodular Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
G
be a unimodular three-dimensional Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and suppose
t\in\mathfrak{g}
is
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
-semisimple, so
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
. Up to an inner isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
,
(\mathfrak{g},t)
has the form
(\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)},t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1})
where
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
and the parameters
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are as in the following table:

\alpha_{1}=\operatorname{sgn}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)

\alpha_{2}=-\epsilon

B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=-8n(t)

\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}

-1

-1

-8\kappa^{-2}<0

\mathfrak{so}(3)

1

1

8\kappa^{-2}>0

\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})

-1

1

-8\kappa^{-2}<0

\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})

-1

0

-8\kappa^{-2}<0

\mathfrak{e}(2)

1

0

8\kappa^{-2}>0

\mathfrak{e}(1,1)

Proof.

Given
0\neq\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
, the basis

(12.39)

\displaystyle t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1},

\displaystyle a=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e_{2},

\displaystyle b=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\kappa}e_{3}

of
\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
has brackets

(12.40)

\displaystyle[t,a]=2b,

\displaystyle[t,b]=\tfrac{1}{4}B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)a,

\displaystyle[a,b]=-\alpha_{2}t,

where there has been used
-8n(t)=B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=8\kappa^{-2}\alpha_{1}
. The brackets (
12.40
) are as in (
12.2
) provided
\epsilon=-\alpha_{2}
. By Lemma
12.2
any unimodular three-dimensional Lie algebra containing an
\operatorname{ad}
-semisimple element is isomorphic to one with a basis as in (
12.2
), so this shows that
\mathfrak{g}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
for some
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=-\epsilon)
as in Table
12.8
, where
\epsilon\in\{0,1,-1\}
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
if
\epsilon=1
. In every case there are at most two distinct adjoint orbits corresponding with a given value of
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
, and by choosing
\alpha_{1}
suitably, it can always be supposed that
t
is a multiple of
e_{1}
.

If
-\alpha_{2}=\epsilon=1
and
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
, then
\mathfrak{g}
is compact and simple. In this case
\alpha_{1}=-1
and the adjoint orbits of
\mathbb{S}({\left(\frac{-1,-1}{\mathbb{R}}\right)})=S^{3}
are the spheres comprising vectors of constant Killing norm, so after an inner automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
it can be supposed that
t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1}
for some
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is simple but noncompact, then it can be supposed that
-\alpha_{2}=\epsilon=-1
and
\alpha_{1}
is either
1
or
-1
. The adjoint orbits of
\mathbb{S}({\left(\frac{1,1}{\mathbb{R}}\right)})
with
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)<0
are
1
-sheeted hyperboloids, so in this case it can be supposed that
\alpha_{1}=1
and
t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1}
with
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
. The adjoint orbits with
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)>0
are connected components of
2
-sheeted hyperboloids and
\mathbb{S}({\left(\frac{-1,1}{\mathbb{R}}\right)})
acts transitively on each of these connected components, so it can be supposed
\alpha_{1}=-1
and
t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1}
with
\kappa\neq 0
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is not simple then it is solvable because it contains an
\operatorname{ad}
-semisimple element, so
\epsilon=0
but
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0
. It can be supposed that
-\alpha_{2}=\epsilon=0
and
\mathfrak{g}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{e}(2)\simeq\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{-1,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
or
\mathfrak{e}(1,1)\simeq\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{1,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
. In either case, each of the two connected components of a nonzero level set of
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)
is an adjoint orbit of
\mathbb{S}({\left(\frac{\pm 1,0}{\mathbb{R}}\right)})
, and so it can be supposed
t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1}
with
\kappa\neq 0
.

The preceding shows that up to inner automorphism
(\mathfrak{g},t)
has the form
(\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)},t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1})
where the parameters
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
and
\kappa
are as in Table
12
.

\alpha_{1}

\alpha_{2}=-\epsilon

range for
\kappa

B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)=-8n(t)

\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}

-1

-1

(0,\infty)

-8\kappa^{-2}<0

\mathfrak{so}(3)

1

1

(0,\infty)

8\kappa^{-2}>0

\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})

-1

1

(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)

-8\kappa^{-2}<0

\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})

-1

0

(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)

-8\kappa^{-2}<0

\mathfrak{e}(2)

1

0

(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)

8\kappa^{-2}>0

\mathfrak{e}(1,1)

To complete the proof, it suffices to observe that in each case where
\kappa<0
is possible there is an outer automorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
sending
t
to
-t
.
∎

Theorem 12.9
.

Let
G
be a unimodular three-dimensional Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and suppose
t\in\mathfrak{g}
is
\operatorname{ad}_{\mathfrak{g}}(t)
-semisimple, so
B_{\mathfrak{g}}(t,t)\neq 0
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
for some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
and there is a unique left-invariant torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
on
G
constituting with
\mathbb{E}=E^{t}
, where
t=\kappa^{-1}e_{1}
, a left-invariant conelike radiant structure having antisymmetric Ricci tensor, and it has the form

(12.47)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\nabla_{E_{1}}E_{1}=\kappa E_{1},&&\nabla_{E_{2}}E_{1}=\kappa E_{2},&&\nabla_{E_{3}}E_{1}=\kappa E_{3},\\
&\nabla_{E_{1}}E_{2}=\kappa E_{2}+2E_{3},&&\nabla_{E_{2}}E_{2}=4\alpha_{2}\kappa^{-1}E_{1},&&\nabla_{E_{3}}E_{2}=\alpha_{2}E_{1},\\
&\nabla_{E_{1}}E_{3}=\kappa E_{3}+2\alpha_{1}E_{2},&&\nabla_{E_{2}}E_{3}=-\alpha_{2}E_{1},&&\nabla_{E_{3}}E_{3}=-4\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\kappa^{-1}E_{1},\end{aligned}

where
\{E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\}
is the left-invariant frame satisfying (
12.22
). Equivalently, with respect to the dual left-invariant coframe
\{\epsilon^{1},\epsilon^{2},\epsilon^{3}\}
,

(12.48)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla\epsilon^{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}d\epsilon^{1}&=-\kappa\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{1}-4\alpha_{2}\kappa^{-1}\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{2}+4\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\kappa^{-1}\epsilon^{3}\otimes\epsilon^{3},\\
\nabla\epsilon^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}d\epsilon^{2}&=-\kappa(\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{1})-\alpha_{1}(\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{3}+\epsilon^{3}\otimes\epsilon^{1}),\\
\nabla\epsilon^{3}-\tfrac{1}{2}d\epsilon^{3}&=-(\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{1})-\kappa(\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{3}+\epsilon^{3}\otimes\epsilon^{1}).\end{split}

(1)

The curvature and Ricci curvature of
\nabla
have the form

(12.49)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &R(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=-3\alpha_{2}\kappa(\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3})\otimes\left(\epsilon^{2}\otimes E_{2}+\epsilon^{3}\otimes E_{3}\right)=-\tfrac{3}{2}\kappa d\epsilon^{1}\otimes\left(\epsilon^{2}\otimes E_{2}+\epsilon^{3}\otimes E_{3}\right),\\
&\mathsf{Ric}(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=-3\alpha_{2}\kappa\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}=-\tfrac{3}{2}\kappa d\epsilon^{1}.\end{aligned}

(2)

The connection
\nabla
is not projectively flat if
\alpha_{2}\neq 0
(it is flat if
\alpha_{2}=0
).

(3)

For
\beta=\kappa\epsilon^{1}
, the symmetric tensor

(12.50)

\displaystyle G=\nabla\beta-\tfrac{1}{2}d\beta+2\beta\otimes\beta

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\epsilon^{1}\otimes\epsilon^{1}-4\alpha_{2}\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{2}+4\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\epsilon^{3}\otimes\epsilon^{3},

satisfies
G(\mathbb{E},\,\cdot\,\,)=\beta
and

(12.51)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}G_{jk}

\displaystyle=-2\beta_{i}G_{jk}+d\beta_{i(j}\beta_{k)}=-2\beta_{i}G_{jk}+\tfrac{1}{3}\nabla_{(j}d\beta_{k)i},

(12.52)

\displaystyle\nabla_{[i}G_{j]k}

\displaystyle=-2\beta_{[i}G_{j]k}+\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{k}d\beta_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{[i}d\beta_{j]k}=-2\beta_{[i}G_{j]k}-\tfrac{1}{4}\nabla_{k}d\beta_{ij}.

(Abstract indices are used because they facilitate indication of the symmetries).

Proof.

That
\mathfrak{g}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Im}{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
for some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
follows from Lemma
12.8
.
That
\nabla
has the form (
12.47
) follows from Corollary
12.5
in conjunction with (
12.39
) and (
12.40
). The identities (
12.48
) follow from (
12.47
) and (
12.27
). That its curvature has the form (
12.49
) follows from (
12.14
).
The projective Weyl tensor
B
of
\nabla
satisfies
B(t,\,\cdot\,\,)\,\cdot\,\,=\tfrac{3}{4}\alpha_{2}\kappa\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}
, so
\nabla
is not projectively flat if
\alpha_{2}\neq 0
. For
G
as in (
12.50
), straightforward calculations using (
12.47
) or (
12.48
) show (
12.51
). Straightforward calculations using (
12.47
) or (
12.48
) show

(12.53)

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}d\beta_{jk}=-2\beta_{i}d\beta_{jk}+2\beta_{[j}d\beta_{k]i},

and this yields the second equality of (
12.51
). Antisymmetrizing (
12.51
) yields (
12.52
).
∎

Example 35
.

This example completes Remark
10
.
Regard
S^{3}
as the group of unit quaternions.
By Theorem
12.9
, for
\kappa\neq 0
, the left-invariant connection defined by (
12.47
) with
\alpha_{1}=-1=\alpha_{2}

determines with
\mathbb{E}=\kappa^{-1}E_{1}
a conelike nonsingular radiant structure on
S^{3}
having Ricci curvature
\mathsf{Ric}(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=-\tfrac{3}{2}\kappa d\epsilon^{1}
.
However, there is no one-form
\sigma
on
S^{3}
satisfying simultaneously
\sigma(E_{1})=0
and
d\sigma=d\epsilon^{1}
. Were there, then

(12.54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}d(\sigma\wedge\epsilon^{1})&=d\sigma\wedge\epsilon^{1}-\sigma\wedge d\epsilon^{1}=d\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{1}+2\sigma\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}\\
&=-2\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}+2\sigma(E_{1})\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}=-2\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}\end{split}

which by Stokes’ Theorem contradicts that
\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}
is a volume form on
S^{3}
.

Remark 32
.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0
. In this case, the tensor
G
of (
12.50
) is a pseudo-Riemannian metric. It follows from (
12.51
) that the Levi-Civita connection
D
of
G
is

(12.55)

\displaystyle D=\nabla-2\beta_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}+G_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}+\beta_{(i}d\beta_{j)}\,^{k}G^{pk},

where here and in what follows indices are raised and lowered using
G_{ij}
and the inverse symetric bivector
G^{ij}
. In particular, for
{\left(\frac{-1,-1}{\mathbb{R}}\right)}
,
G^{\kappa}
(the superscript indicates the dependence on
\kappa
) is a one-parameter family of Riemannian metrics, equal, up to normalizations, to the Berger metrics on the
3
-sphere
[
31
, Example
3.35
]
.

As in (
10.24
) of Lemma
10.4
, consider the connection

(12.56)

\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD}=\nabla+\Omega_{ij}\,^{k}=\nabla+\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}+(s-1)(2\beta_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}-G_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k})=D+s(2\beta_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}-G_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k}),

where
\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}=\beta_{(i}d\beta_{j)p}G^{pk}
and
\Omega_{ij}\,^{k}=\Pi_{ij}\,^{k}+(s-1)(2\beta_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}-G_{ij}\mathbb{E}^{k})
. From (
12.51
) it follows that
\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD}_{i}G_{jk}=-2s\beta_{i}G_{jk}
, so that
(\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD},G)
is a Weyl structure for every
s\in\mathbb{R}
. For
s=0
,
\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD}=D
is the Levi-Civita connection of
G_{ij}
.

Because
\mathbb{E}^{p}G_{ip}=\beta_{i}
and
\mathbb{E}^{p}d\beta_{ip}=0
,
\Pi_{ip}\,^{p}=0
.
By (
12.53
),
G^{pq}\nabla_{p}d\beta_{iq}=0
, and, by (
12.51
),
G^{pq}\nabla_{i}G_{pq}=-6\beta_{i}
and
G^{pq}\nabla_{p}G_{qi}=-2\beta_{i}
. Together with these observations, the same calculations showing (
10.29
) of Lemma
10.4
show
\Omega_{ip}\,^{p}=3(s-1)\beta_{i}
and

(12.57)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Omega_{ij}\,^{p}\Omega_{pq}\,^{q}=3(s-1)^{2}\left(2\beta_{i}\beta_{j}-G_{ij}\right),&&\Omega_{ip}\,^{q}\Omega_{jq}\,^{p}=\tfrac{1}{4}\beta_{i}\beta_{j}d\beta_{p}\,^{q}d\beta_{q}\,^{p}+(s-1)^{2}\left(5\beta_{i}\beta_{j}-2G_{ij}\right),&\end{aligned}

(12.58)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\nabla_{i}\Omega_{jp}\,^{p}=3(s-1)(G_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}d\beta_{ij}-2\beta_{i}\beta_{j}),&&\nabla_{p}\Omega_{ij}\,^{p}=\tfrac{1}{2}d\beta_{i}\,^{p}d\beta_{pj}+(s-1)\left(G_{ij}-4\beta_{i}\beta_{j}\right).&\end{aligned}

Substituting (
12.57
) and (
12.58
) into (
12.56
) yields that the Ricci curvature
\mathsf{Ric}(\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD})_{ij}
of
\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD}
is related to the Ricci curvature
R_{ij}=-\tfrac{3}{2}d\beta_{ij}
of
\nabla
by

(12.59)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{Ric}(\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD})_{ij}&=R_{ij}+\nabla_{p}\Omega_{ij}\,^{p}-\nabla_{i}\Omega_{pj}\,^{p}+\Omega_{pq}\,^{p}\Omega_{ij}\,^{q}-\Omega_{ip}\,^{q}\Omega_{jq}\,^{p}\\
&=-\tfrac{3}{2}sd\beta_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}d\beta_{i}\,^{p}d\beta_{pj}+(1-s^{2})G_{ij}+\left(s^{2}-1-\tfrac{1}{4}d\beta_{p}\,^{q}d\beta_{q}\,^{p}\right)\beta_{i}\beta_{j}.\end{aligned}

The tensor corresponding with
d\beta_{i}\,^{j}=G^{jp}d\beta_{ip}
is
\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\epsilon^{3}\otimes E_{2}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2\alpha_{1}}\epsilon^{2}\otimes E_{3}
, and it follows that the tensor corresponding with
d\beta_{ip}d\beta_{qj}G^{pq}
is

(12.60)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\kappa^{2}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\epsilon^{2}\otimes\epsilon^{2}+\kappa^{2}\alpha_{2}\epsilon^{3}\otimes\epsilon^{3}=\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{4\alpha_{1}}(G-\beta\otimes\beta),

so that

(12.61)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}d\beta_{ip}d\beta^{p}\,_{j}=\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{8\alpha_{1}}(G_{ij}-\beta_{i}\beta_{j}),

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{4}d\beta_{p}\,^{q}d\beta_{q}\,^{p}\beta_{i}\beta_{j}=-\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{8\alpha_{1}}\beta_{i}\beta_{j}.

Substituting (
12.61
) in (
12.59
) yields

(12.62)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{Ric}(\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD})_{ij}=&-\tfrac{3}{2}sd\beta_{ij}+\left(\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{8\alpha_{1}}+1-s^{2}\right)G_{ij}+\left(s^{2}-1-\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{4\alpha_{1}}\right)\beta_{i}\beta_{j}.\end{aligned}

If
s=\pm\sqrt{1+\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{4\alpha_{1}}}
(when
\alpha_{1}=-1
, this necessitates
\kappa^{2}\leq 4
), then

(12.63)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{Ric}(\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD})_{ij}=&-\tfrac{3}{2}sd\beta_{ij}+\left(\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{8\alpha_{1}}+1-s^{2}\right)G_{ij}=-\tfrac{3}{2}sd\beta_{ij}-\tfrac{\kappa^{2}}{8\alpha_{1}}G_{ij},\end{aligned}

so
(\mkern 0.0mu{\mkern 1.0mu{}^{s}\mkern-2.0muD},G)
is an Einstein-Weyl structure. In the case
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=-1
, these are the well-known Einstein-Weyl structures on the Berger spheres
[
82
, Equation
5.10
]
(the case
s=0
being the standard round metric of scalar curvature
3/2
). See also
[
119
, p.
387
]
and
[
24
, Section
6
]
. The resulting Einstein-Weyl structures in the Lorentz signature case
\alpha_{1}=-1
and
\alpha_{2}=1
are described in
[
10
]
.

Lemma 12.10
.

A biinvariant section of
S^{2}T\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
is a constant multiple of
\Gamma=-\alpha_{2}E_{1}\otimes E_{1}-\alpha_{1}E_{2}\otimes E_{2}+E_{3}\otimes E_{3}
. For any left-invariant
\theta=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\theta_{i}\epsilon^{i}\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A}))
,

(12.64)

\displaystyle\theta\wedge d\theta=2(\alpha_{2}\theta_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{1}\theta_{2}^{2}-\theta_{3}^{2})\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}=-2\Gamma(\theta,\theta)\epsilon^{1}\wedge\epsilon^{2}\wedge\epsilon^{3}.

Consequently:

(1)

\theta
is a contact one-form if and only if
\Gamma(\theta,\theta)\neq 0
.

(2)

\Gamma(\theta,\theta)
is constant on a coadjoint orbit of
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
.

(3)

\ker\theta
generates an integrable left-invariant subbundle of
T\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
if and only if
\Gamma(\theta,\theta)=0
. This can occur for
\theta\neq 0
if and only if at least one of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
is positive.

(4)

If
\alpha_{1}\leq 0
and
\alpha_{2}\leq 0
then
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
contains no two-dimensional subalgebras.

(5)

If
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=1
, then, for
\theta^{\pm}=\epsilon^{2}\pm\epsilon^{3}
,
\ker\theta^{\pm}=\text{Span}\,\{E_{1},E_{2}\mp E_{3}\}
are integrable,
\nabla
-totally geodesic subbundles of
T\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
where
\nabla
is the connection of Theorem
12.9
.

Proof.

Identify
\Gamma\in S^{2}T\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
with
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}a_{ij}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}+e_{j}\otimes e_{i})\in S^{2}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. That
\Gamma
be biinvariant is equivalent to
r\cdot\gamma=0
for all
r\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. This yields

(12.65)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=e_{1}\cdot\gamma=2(a_{2}+\alpha_{1}a_{3})(e_{2}\otimes e_{3}+e_{3}\otimes e_{2})+2a_{12}(e_{1}\otimes e_{3}+e_{3}\otimes e_{1})\\
&\quad+2\alpha_{1}a_{13}(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}+e_{2}\otimes e_{1})+4a_{23}(\alpha_{1}e_{2}\otimes e_{2}+e_{3}\otimes e_{3}),\end{split}

so that
a_{12}=0
,
a_{13}=0
,
a_{23}=0
, and
a_{2}=-\alpha_{1}a_{3}
. Hence
\gamma=a_{1}e_{1}\otimes e_{1}-\alpha_{1}a_{3}e_{2}\otimes e_{2}+a_{3}e_{3}\otimes e_{3}
. Consequently,

(12.66)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=e_{2}\cdot\gamma=-2(a_{1}+\alpha_{2}a_{3})(e_{1}\otimes e_{3}+e_{3}\otimes e_{1}),\end{split}

so that
a_{1}=-\alpha_{2}a_{3}
. Thus
\gamma=a_{3}(-\alpha_{2}e_{1}\otimes e_{1}-\alpha_{1}e_{2}\otimes e_{2}+e_{3}\otimes e_{3})
, and it is straightforward to check that
e_{3}\cdot\gamma=0
. Straightforward calculations show (
12.64
).

By (
12.64
),
\ker\theta
generates an integrable left-invariant distribution if and only if
\Gamma(\theta,\theta)=0
, and this has nontrivial solutions if and only if
\theta_{3}^{2}=\alpha_{2}\theta_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{1}\theta_{2}^{2}
, which occurs if and only if at least one of
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
is positive. If
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
has a two-dimensional subalgebra, then this subalgebra generates an integrable left-invariant subbundle of
T\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{A})
, and a one-form
\theta
annihilating the subalgebra must satisfy
\Gamma(\theta,\theta)=0
, so if
\alpha_{1}\leq 0
and
\alpha_{2}\leq 0
then
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
has no two-dimensional subalgebras.

If
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=1
, then
d\theta^{\pm}=\pm 2\theta^{\pm}\wedge\epsilon^{1}
and, by (
12.48
),
\nabla\theta^{\pm}=\mp\theta^{\pm}\wedge\epsilon^{1}-(\kappa\pm 1)(\epsilon^{1}\otimes\theta^{\pm}+\theta^{\pm}\otimes\epsilon^{1})
, which shows that
\ker\theta^{\pm}
are integrable and totally geodesic subbundles.
∎
